\displaystyle\text{for all }b_{1},b_{2}\in\{0,1\}.

In this setting, the problem
\mathsf{MaxCSP}(\mathcal{F})
is also known as
\mathsf{Max}
-
\mathsf{2SAT}
. If the essentially unary predicates
f^{(0)}
and
f^{(1)}
are removed from the family
\mathcal{F}
, the resulting problem is called
\mathsf{Max}
-
\mathsf{E2SAT}
, where the letter “
\mathsf{E}
” stands for “exact.”

From
Theorem

3.8
(or
[
FMW25
]
), it follows that
\mathsf{Max}
-
\mathsf{E2SAT}
is approximation resistant
8
8

8

That is,
\mathsf{Max}
-
\mathsf{E2SAT}[1,\tfrac{3}{4}+\varepsilon]
is hard for every
\varepsilon>0
, while every nonempty instance has value at least
\tfrac{3}{4}
.
under multipass streaming, achieving an approximation ratio of
3/4
. However, it is not immediately clear whether the non-exact version
\mathsf{Max}
-
\mathsf{2SAT}
admits the same ratio. We show in
Theorem

1.6
that
\mathsf{Max}
-
\mathsf{2SAT}
indeed has the same approximation ratio of
3/4
.

This contrasts with the single-pass streaming setting, where the approximation ratios for
\mathsf{Max}
-
\mathsf{2SAT}
and
\mathsf{Max}
-
\mathsf{E2SAT}
are
\sqrt{2}/2
and
3/4
, respectively, as shown in
[
CGV20
]
. Thus, in the single-pass regime, the non-exact version
\mathsf{Max}
-
\mathsf{2SAT}
is strictly harder to approximate than
\mathsf{Max}
-
\mathsf{E2SAT}
, whereas in the multipass regime this gap disappears.

We are now ready to prove
Theorem

1.6
, restated below.
See
1.6

Proof Sketch.

By considering a random assignment where each variable is independently assigned a value from
\{0,1\}
uniformly at random, we observe that every nonempty instance
\mathcal{I}
of
\mathsf{Max}\text{-}\mathsf{2SAT}
satisfies
\mathrm{val}_{\mathcal{I}}\geqslant 1/2
. Hence,
\vartheta_{\mathcal{F}}(c)=c
for
c\in[0,\tfrac{1}{2}]
.

To bound
\vartheta_{\mathcal{F}}(c)
for larger
c
, consider two extremal constructions:

•

First instance: A variable set
\{1,2\}
with two constraints,
((1,2),f^{(0)})
and
((1,2),f^{(1)})
.
Both the integral value and LP value of this instance are
1/2
, implying
\vartheta_{\mathcal{F}}^{*}(1/2)\leqslant 1/2
.

•

Second instance: A variable set
[n]
with
4\binom{n}{2}
constraints, where on each pair
\{i,j\}\subseteq[n]
all four E2SAT constraints
f^{(0,0)},f^{(0,1)},f^{(1,0)},f^{(1,1)}
are placed.
This instance has LP value
1
and integral value approaching
3/4
as
n\to\infty
.

These two constructions imply
\vartheta_{\mathcal{F}}^{*}(1/2)\leqslant 1/2
and
\vartheta^{*}_{\mathcal{F}}(1)\leqslant 3/4
. By
Lemma

3.5
, we then obtain

\vartheta_{\mathcal{F}}(c)\leqslant\frac{2c+1}{4}\quad\text{for }c\in[\tfrac{1}{2},1].

Thus, it remains to prove the reverse inequality
\vartheta_{\mathcal{F}}(c)\geqslant(2c+1)/4

for
c\in[\tfrac{1}{2},1]
. By
Definition

3.4
, this is equivalent to showing that for every nonempty
\mathsf{Max}\text{-}\mathsf{2SAT}
instance
\mathcal{I}
,

\mathrm{val}_{\mathcal{I}}\geqslant\frac{1}{2}\cdot\mathrm{val}^{\mathrm{LP}}_{\mathcal{I}}+\frac{1}{4}.

(3.3)

Let
\mathcal{I}=(\mathcal{V},(C_{1},\dots,C_{m}))
be a
\mathsf{Max}\text{-}\mathsf{2SAT}
instance, where
C_{i}=((\mathsf{v}_{i,1},\mathsf{v}_{i,2}),f_{i})
.
For convenience, define
R^{(1)}:[0,1]\to[0,1]
as the identity map and
R^{(0)}:[0,1]\to[0,1]
by
R^{(0)}(x)=1-x
. For any
(b_{1},b_{2})\in\{0,1\}^{2}
and
i\in[m]
, if
f_{i}=f^{(b_{1},b_{2})}
, define

g_{i}(x_{1},x_{2}):=1-R^{(b_{1})}(x_{1})\,R^{(b_{2})}(x_{2}),\qquad h_{i}(x_{1},x_{2}):=\min\!\left(1,2-R^{(b_{1})}(x_{1})-R^{(b_{2})}(x_{2})\right).

Similarly, for
i\in[m]
and
b\in\{0,1\}
, if
f_{i}=f^{(b)}
, set

g_{i}(x_{1},x_{2})=h_{i}(x_{1},x_{2})=R^{(b)}(x_{1}).

The basic linear program
{\textsc{BasicLP}}_{\mathcal{I}}
can then be written as:

\displaystyle\text{maximize}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}z_{i}

\displaystyle\text{subject to}

\displaystyle 0\leqslant z_{i}\leqslant h_{i}\big(x_{\mathsf{v}_{i,1}},x_{\mathsf{v}_{i,2}}\big)\qquad

\displaystyle\forall i\in[m],

\displaystyle 0\leqslant x_{\mathsf{v}}\leqslant 1

\displaystyle\forall\mathsf{v}\in\mathcal{V}.

Let
P\subseteq[0,1]^{\mathcal{V}\cup[m]}
be the polytope defined by these constraints.
By an argument analogous to
Lemma

3.11
, every vertex of
P
has coordinates in
\{0,\tfrac{1}{2},1\}
.
Hence, there exists an optimal solution

(x^{*},z^{*})\in\{0,\tfrac{1}{2},1\}^{\mathcal{V}\cup[m]}

such that

\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}h_{i}\big(x^{*}_{\mathsf{v}_{i,1}},x^{*}_{\mathsf{v}_{i,2}}\big)=\mathrm{val}^{\mathrm{LP}}_{\mathcal{I}}.

(3.4)

Define a random assignment
\tau:\mathcal{V}\to\{0,1\}
by setting
\tau(\mathsf{v})=1
with probability
x^{*}_{\mathsf{v}}
, independently for all
\mathsf{v}
.
Then each constraint
C_{i}
is satisfied with probability
g_{i}(x^{*}_{\mathsf{v}_{i,1}},x^{*}_{\mathsf{v}_{i,2}})
.
Hence,

\underset{\tau}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\mathrm{val}_{\mathcal{I}}(\tau)\right]=\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}g_{i}\big(x^{*}_{\mathsf{v}_{i,1}},x^{*}_{\mathsf{v}_{i,2}}\big).

Thus, to prove (
3.3
), it suffices to establish

\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}g_{i}\big(x^{*}_{\mathsf{v}_{i,1}},x^{*}_{\mathsf{v}_{i,2}}\big)\geqslant\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\left(\frac{1}{2}\cdot h_{i}\big(x_{\mathsf{v}_{i,1}}^{*},x_{\mathsf{v}_{i,2}}^{*}\big)+\frac{1}{4}\right).

(3.5)

To prove (
3.5
), we let
T\subseteq[m]
be the set of constraint indices
i\in[m]
where
x_{\mathsf{v}_{i,1}}^{*}=x_{\mathsf{v}_{i,2}}^{*}=\frac{1}{2}
. For
i\in T
, a direct calculation shows

g_{i}\big(x^{*}_{\mathsf{v}_{i,1}},x^{*}_{\mathsf{v}_{i,2}}\big)=\frac{3}{4}=\frac{1}{2}\cdot h_{i}\big(x^{*}_{\mathsf{v}_{i,1}},x^{*}_{\mathsf{v}_{i,2}}\big)+\frac{1}{4}.

(3.6)

We claim that

\frac{1}{n-|T|}\sum_{i\in[n]\setminus T}g_{i}\big(x_{\mathsf{v}_{i,1}}^{*},x_{\mathsf{v}_{i,2}}^{*}\big)=\frac{1}{n-|T|}\sum_{i\in[n]\setminus T}h_{i}\big(x_{\mathsf{v}_{i,1}}^{*},x_{\mathsf{v}_{i,2}}^{*}\big)\geqslant\frac{1}{2},

(3.7)

which combined with (
3.6
) would yield (
3.5
). The first transition in (
3.7
) can be justified by verifying
1-xy=\min\{1,2-x-y\}
for all
(x,y)\in\{0,\frac{1}{2},1\}^{2}\setminus\{(\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2})\}
. The second transition holds because otherwise, reassigning the value
1/2
to all
x^{*}_{\mathsf{v}}
, for
\mathsf{v}\in[n]
, would increase the sum on the left hand side of (
3.4
), contradicting the optimality of the LP solution
(x^{*},z^{*})
.
∎

4
The Multi-Pass Algorithm

In this section we prove the main algorithmic result of the paper,
Theorem

3.7
. Our approach combines the high-level strategy of
[
Yos11
]
and
[
SSSV25
]
. Given a bounded-degree instance
\mathcal{I}\in\mathrm{CSP}(\mathcal{F})
of the CSP maximization problem,
[
Yos11
]
showed that the linear programming relaxation
{\textsc{BasicLP}}_{\mathcal{I}}
(defined in
Definition

3.1
) can be approximately solved by a constant-round local algorithm, and that such local algorithms can in turn be simulated by constant-query property testers. A key observation, exploited by
[
SSSV25
]
, is that multi-pass streaming algorithms can likewise simulate constant-round local algorithms. This essentially yields
Theorem

3.7
, with one caveat: unlike the bounded-degree instances considered in
[
Yos11
]
for property testing, our streaming setting must handle instances of unbounded degree.

To bridge this gap, we show that the classic reduction from general CSPs to bounded-degree CSPs, originally developed for polynomial-time algorithms in
[
Tre01
]
, can in fact be adapted to the streaming model. Combined with Yoshida’s local algorithm for solving the LP relaxation on bounded-degree instances, this yields the desired streaming algorithm.

The section is organized as follows.

Section

4.1
presents Yoshida’s local algorithm for approximately solving the LP on bounded-degree instances.

Section

4.2
shows how general CSP maximization problems can be reduced to the bounded-degree setting, and finally,
Section

4.3
implements this reduction in the streaming model.

4.1
Yoshida’s Local Algorithm

In order to define local algorithms, we first define the notion of
degrees
for CSP instances.

Definition 4.1
.

Consider a
\mathrm{CSP}(\mathcal{F})
instance
\mathcal{I}=(\mathcal{V},(C_{1},\dots,C_{m}))
, where
{\mathcal{F}}\subseteq\{f:\Sigma^{k}\rightarrow\{0,1\}\}
. The
degree
of a variable
\mathsf{v}\in\mathcal{V}
, denoted by
\deg_{\mathcal{I}}(\mathsf{v})
, is defined as the number of pairs
(i,\ell)\in[m]\times[k]
such that
\mathsf{v}
appears as the
\ell
-th variable in the scope of constraint
C_{i}
. The
maximum degree
of
\mathcal{I}
is the maximum degree over all variables in
\mathcal{V}
and is denoted by
\deg_{\mathcal{I}}
.

A large CSP instance with bounded maximum degree naturally corresponds to a sparse hypergraph, where variables are represented as vertices and constraints as hyperedges. This perspective motivates the study of distributed algorithms over the hypergraph structure, in which each variable acts as an agent that communicates with its “neighbors” to determine its assignment.

One drawback of the hypergraph view, however, is that the notion of a vertex’s neighborhood is less canonical than in standard graphs. To address this, we alternatively model the CSP instance as a bipartite graph capturing the incidence relation between variables and constraints. In this setting, distances between vertices are defined using standard graph distance, and distributed algorithms can be analyzed within the standard “LOCAL model” of distributed computing
[
Pel00
]
.

Definition 4.2
.

Given a
\mathrm{CSP}(\mathcal{F})
instance
\mathcal{I}=(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{C})
where
\mathcal{C}=(C_{1},\dots,C_{m})
, we define the associated
auxiliary (labeled) bipartite graph
as follows:

(1)

The vertex set of the graph is partitioned into two parts: the set of variables
\mathcal{V}
on the left side, and the index set
[m]
on the right, representing the constraints.

(2)

For each variable
\mathsf{v}\in\mathcal{V}
and each constraint index
i\in[m]
, we add an edge between
\mathsf{v}
and
i
labeled with
j
if
\mathsf{v}
appears as the
j
-th variable in the scope of the constraint
C_{i}
.

(3)

Each vertex
i\in[m]
on the right side is additionally labeled with the predicate
f_{i}
associated with the constraint
C_{i}
.

Note that the degree of each variable
\mathsf{v}\in\mathcal{V}
, as defined in
Definition

4.1
, coincides with the usual notion of the degree when
\mathsf{v}
is viewed as a vertex on the left side of the associated auxiliary bipartite graph.

As is standard in the LOCAL model, a local algorithm is formalized as a function from neighborhood profiles to output values at a designated root vertex. The round complexity of such an algorithm corresponds to the radius of the neighborhood it inspects.

We now define the notion of a neighborhood in the auxiliary bipartite graph representation of a CSP instance. For our purposes — particularly in describing the local algorithm from
[
Yos11
]
— we are primarily interested in neighborhoods rooted at vertices representing constraints.

Definition 4.3
.

Fix an instance
\mathcal{I}=(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{C})\in\mathrm{CSP}(\mathcal{F})
, where
\mathcal{C}=(C_{1},\dots,C_{m})
, and let
G
denote its associated auxiliary bipartite graph. For any vertex
u
in
G
and any positive integer
r
, we define
\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{I}}(u,r)
to be the radius-
r
labeled neighborhood of the vertex
u
. This neighborhood includes all vertices within graph distance at most
r
from
u
, all edges among them, and the associated labels on both vertices and edges.

We let
\mathscr{N}_{\mathcal{F}}(r)
denote the collection of all possible radius-
r
labeled neighborhoods with a distinguished root vertex
on the right side
(i.e. corresponding to constraints, not variables) that can arise from
\mathrm{CSP}(\mathcal{F})
instances. Formally speaking,
\mathscr{N}_{\mathcal{F}}(r)
is defined as

\displaystyle\mathscr{N}_{\mathcal{F}}(r):=\{\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{I}}(i,r):\mathcal{I}=(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{C}=(C_{1},\dots,C_{m}))\in\mathrm{CSP}(\mathcal{F}),i\in[m]\}.

For instance,
\mathscr{N}_{\mathcal{F}}(1)
consists of a single element: a star graph with
k
edges labeled
1
through
k
. For any fixed
r\geqslant 2
, however, the collection
\mathscr{N}_{\mathcal{F}}(r)
is infinite, since a variable in a
\mathrm{CSP}(\mathcal{F})
instance — equivalently, a vertex on the left side of the auxiliary bipartite graph — can have arbitrarily large degree. If we instead restrict our attention to neighborhoods arising from
\mathrm{CSP}(\mathcal{F})
instances with maximum degree at most
B
(which we will do in
Lemma

4.5
), then we are concerned with only a finite subset of
\mathscr{N}_{\mathcal{F}}(r)
.

Remark 4.4
.

One simple but useful observation about neighborhoods is that, when
\mathcal{I}=(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{C}=(C_{1},\dots,C_{m}))
has maximum degree at most
B
, then for any
\mathsf{v}\in\mathcal{V}
(or
i\in[m]
), the number of vertices and edges contained in
\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{I}}(\mathsf{v},r)
(or
\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{I}}(i,r)
) is at most
2\left(\max\{B,k\}\right)^{r}+1
.

We now state the result of Yoshida
[
Yos11
]
, which says that for constant arity
k
, alphabet-size
|\Sigma|
, and degree bound
B
, for any instance
\mathcal{I}\in\mathrm{CSP}(\mathcal{F})
with degree at most
B
, the objective value of
{\textsc{BasicLP}}_{\mathcal{I}}
can be approximated within an additive error
\varepsilon
by an local algorithm with constant locality.

Lemma 4.5

(
[
Yos11
, Theorem 3.1]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{F}\subseteq\{f:\Sigma^{k}\rightarrow\{0,1\}\}
be a fixed family of predicates, and fix a positive integer
B
and an error parameter
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
. Then there exists a positive integer
r\leqslant\exp\big(\mathrm{poly}(kB|\Sigma|/\varepsilon)\big)
and a deterministic map
\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{loc}}:\mathscr{N}_{\mathcal{F}}(r)\rightarrow[0,1]^{\Sigma^{k}}
such that the following holds:

Given any
\mathrm{CSP}(\mathcal{F})
instance
\mathcal{I}=(\mathcal{V},(C_{1},\dots,C_{m}))
with maximum degree at most
B
, the output vectors
\hat{z}^{(i)}=\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{I}}(i,r))
(for each
i\in[m]
) satisfies

\mathrm{val}^{\mathrm{LP}}_{\mathcal{I}}-\varepsilon\leqslant\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{b\in\Sigma^{k}}f_{i}(b)\,\hat{z}^{(i)}_{b}\leqslant\mathrm{val}^{\mathrm{LP}}_{\mathcal{I}}+\varepsilon,

where
f_{i}\in\mathcal{F}
is the predicate associated with constraint
C_{i}
.

In words, for constant
k,|\Sigma|
, and
B
, the above lemma offers a local algorithm with constant locality that can approximate
\mathrm{val}^{\mathrm{LP}}_{\mathcal{I}}
with an additive error
\varepsilon
given any instance
\mathcal{I}\in\mathrm{CSP}(\mathcal{F})
with maximum degree at most
B
.

We briefly sketch the main idea behind Lemma
4.5
, referring the reader to
[
Yos11
]
for full details. The algorithm consists of two main steps. First, the original LP relaxation is reduced to a
fractional packing problem
(that is, maximizing
c\cdot x
subject to
Ax\preceq b
and
x\succeq 0
, where
A
,
b
, and
c
have only nonnegative entries), also known as a
positive linear program
. Similar reductions appear in earlier works such as
[
Tre96
,
FS97
]
. Second, Yoshida applies the distributed algorithm of
[
KMW06
]
, which approximates the value of a bounded-degree positive linear program. This algorithm simultaneously maintains primal and dual solutions, and guarantees that their values converge to each other as the number of rounds (and hence the locality) increases.

4.2
Reduction to Bounded-Degree Instances

In the polynomial-time setting, the approximability of CSPs is known to reduce to instances with bounded degree, due to the reduction of
[
Tre01
]
. In this subsection, we present a slightly different reduction that is more amenable to implementation in the streaming model. Our (randomized) reduction map is described in
Algorithm

1
.

Input :
a
\mathrm{CSP}(\mathcal{F})
instance
\mathcal{I}=(\mathcal{V},(C_{1},\dots,C_{m}))
and integer parameters
B,D\geqslant 1

Output :
a
\mathrm{CSP}(\mathcal{F})
instance
\mathcal{I}_{B,D}

Let
\mathcal{V}_{D}:=\left\{(\mathsf{v},j)\mid\mathsf{v}\in\mathcal{V},\,j\in[D\cdot\deg_{\mathcal{I}}(\mathsf{v})]\right\}

1

for

\ell\in[B]

do

2

Initialize the pool of available variables
U\leftarrow\mathcal{V}_{D}

3

for

i\in[m]

do

4

//
next: define the constraint
C_{i,\ell}

for

t\in[k]

do

5

Suppose
\mathsf{v}
is the
t
-th variable in the scope of
C_{i}

6

Pick a uniformly random variable
v
from
U\cap\{(\mathsf{v},j)\mid j\in[D\cdot\deg_{\mathcal{I}}(\mathsf{v})]\}

7

Let the
t
-th variable of
C_{i,\ell}
be
v

8

U\leftarrow U\setminus\{v\}

//
ensures bounded maximum degree in
\mathcal{I}_{B,D}

return

\mathcal{I}_{B,D}=\big(\mathcal{V}_{D},(C_{i,\ell})_{i\in[m],\,\ell\in[B]}\big)

Algorithm 1

Definition of the Random Bounded-Degree Instance
\mathcal{I}_{B,D}

In words, for each
\ell

in the outer loop, we take
a copy of
\mathcal{I}
where each variable
v
is replaced with one of its copies
(v,j)
.
In the following lemma, we show that this reduction preserves the value of the original instance with high probability.

Lemma 4.6
.

Let
\mathcal{F}\subseteq\{f:\Sigma^{k}\rightarrow\{0,1\}\}
be a fixed family of predicates, and fix an error parameter
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
. There exist positive integers
B,D\leqslant\poly(k|\Sigma|/\varepsilon)
such that the random instance
\mathcal{I}_{B,D}
sampled by
Algorithm

1
satisfies

(1)

\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{I}_{B,D}}\left[\mathrm{val}_{\mathcal{I}_{B,D}}\geqslant\mathrm{val}_{\mathcal{I}}\right]=1
;

(2)

\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{I}_{B,D}}\left[\mathrm{val}_{\mathcal{I}_{B,D}}\geqslant\mathrm{val}_{\mathcal{I}}+\varepsilon\right]\leqslant 0.01
;

(3)

\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{I}_{B,D}}\left[\deg_{\mathcal{I}_{B,D}}\leqslant B\right]=1
.

Proof.

Due to the removal step in
Algorithm

1
of
Algorithm

1
, every variable
v\in\mathcal{V}_{D}
is picked at most once in each iteration of the outer-most for-loop (on
Algorithm

1
). Since there are
B
iterations of the outer-most for-loop, any variable
v\in\mathcal{V}_{D}
is used at most
B
times in total, and hence the maximum degree of
\mathcal{I}_{B,D}
is always at most
B
. It remains to prove the first and the second items in the statement.

We first show that
\mathrm{val}_{\mathcal{I}_{B,D}}\geqslant\mathrm{val}_{\mathcal{I}}
always holds. For an assignment
\tau:\mathcal{V}\rightarrow\Sigma
, we can lift it to an assignment
\widetilde{\tau}:\mathcal{V}_{D}\rightarrow\Sigma
by setting
\widetilde{\tau}((\mathsf{v},j)):=\tau(\mathsf{v})
for each
\mathsf{v}\in\mathcal{V}
and
j\in[D\cdot\deg_{\mathcal{I}}(\mathsf{v})]
. By the construction of
\mathcal{I}_{B,D}
, it is easy to see that
\mathrm{val}_{\mathcal{I}_{B,D}}(\widetilde{\tau})=\mathrm{val}_{\mathcal{I}}(\tau)
always holds.

Next, we prove that
\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{I}_{B,D}}\left[\mathrm{val}_{\mathcal{I}_{B,D}}\geqslant\mathrm{val}_{\mathcal{I}}+\varepsilon\right]\leqslant 0.01
. Consider any fixed assignment
\widetilde{\tau}:\mathcal{V}_{D}\rightarrow\Sigma
. For each index pair
(i,\ell)\in[m]\times[B]
, define a Bernoulli random variable

X_{(i,\ell)}=\begin{cases}1,&\text{if the constraint }C_{i,\ell}\text{ is satisfied by $\widetilde{\tau}$ in }\mathcal{I}_{B,D},\\
0,&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Note that the randomness in
X_{(i,\ell)}
comes from
\mathcal{I}_{B,D}
but not from
\widetilde{\tau}
, which is
fixed
. On the other hand, if we define a
random
assignment
\tau:\mathcal{V}\rightarrow\Sigma
by assigning value
\sigma\in\Sigma
to
\mathsf{v}
with probability

\frac{\Big|\big\{j\in[D\cdot\deg_{\mathcal{I}}(\mathsf{v})]\,\big|\,\widetilde{\tau}((\mathsf{v},j))=\sigma\big\}\Big|}{D\cdot\deg_{\mathcal{I}}(\mathsf{v})},

independently for each
\mathsf{v}\in\mathcal{V}
, it is easy to see that

\underset{\mathcal{I}_{B,D}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[X_{(i,1)}\right]=\underset{\mathcal{I}_{B,D}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[X_{(i,2)}\right]=\dots=\underset{\mathcal{I}_{B,D}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[X_{(i,\ell)}\right]=\underset{\tau}{\mathbb{P}}\left[C_{i}\text{ is satisfied by }\tau\right]

holds for each
i\in[m]
. Therefore, we have

\frac{1}{m|B|}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{\ell=1}^{B}\underset{\mathcal{I}_{B,D}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[X_{(i,\ell)}\right]=\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\underset{\tau}{\mathbb{P}}\left[C_{i}\text{ is satisfied by }\tau\right]=\underset{\tau}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\mathrm{val}_{\mathcal{I}}(\tau)\right]\leqslant\mathrm{val}_{\mathcal{I}}.

(4.1)

In order to apply
Proposition

2.4
, we must specify a total order on the index set
[m]\times[B]
. We consider the consider the order in which the constraints
C_{i,\ell}
are specified during the execution of
Algorithm

1
: let
(i,\ell)<(i^{\prime},\ell^{\prime})
if either (1)
\ell<\ell^{\prime}
or (2)
\ell=\ell^{\prime}
and
i<i^{\prime}
. Each time
Algorithm

1
of
Algorithm

1
is executed, the number of available copies
(\mathsf{v},j)
of
\mathsf{v}
is

\displaystyle\big|U\cap\{(\mathsf{v},j)\mid j\in[D\cdot\deg_{\mathcal{I}}(\mathsf{v})]\}\big|

\displaystyle\geqslant D\cdot\deg_{\mathcal{I}}(\mathsf{v})-\deg_{\mathcal{I}}(\mathsf{v})

\displaystyle\geqslant(1-D^{-1})\cdot\big|\{(\mathsf{v},j)\mid j\in[D\cdot\deg_{\mathcal{I}}(\mathsf{v})]\}\big|.

This means that even if the algorithm sampled the variables of
C_{i,\ell}
without avoiding those that have already been occupied (i.e. those that are not in
U
), the probability that none of the
k
sampled variables would actually be occupied is still at least
(1-D^{-1})^{k}
. Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\underset{\mathcal{I}_{B,D}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[X_{(i,\ell)}\right]\geqslant(1-D^{-1})^{k}\cdot\underset{\mathcal{I}_{B,D}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[X_{(i,\ell)}\mid(X_{(i^{\prime},\ell^{\prime})})_{(i^{\prime},\ell^{\prime})<(i,\ell)}\right]

for each
(i,\ell)\in[m]\times[B]
. Picking
D=\lceil 10k/\varepsilon\rceil
, we have

\underset{\mathcal{I}_{B,D}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[X_{(i,\ell)}\mid(X_{(i^{\prime},\ell^{\prime})})_{(i^{\prime},\ell^{\prime})<(i,\ell)}\right]\leqslant\underset{\mathcal{I}_{B,D}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[X_{(i,\ell)}\right]+\frac{\varepsilon}{2},

since
(1-D^{-1})^{-k}\leqslant e^{2k/D}\leqslant 1+\varepsilon/2
.

Let
X=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{\ell=1}^{B}X_{(i,\ell)}
. Now we can apply
Proposition

2.4
to
(X_{(i,\ell)})_{(i,\ell)\in[m]\times[B]}
and get

\underset{\mathcal{I}_{B,D}}{\mathbb{P}}\left[X\geqslant\frac{mB\varepsilon}{2}+\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{\ell=1}^{B}\left(\underset{\mathcal{I}_{B,D}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[X_{(i,\ell)}\right]+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\right)\right]\leqslant\exp\left(-\frac{(mB\varepsilon/2)^{2}}{4mB}\right)=\exp\left(-\frac{mB\varepsilon^{2}}{16}\right).

By the definition of the variables
X_{(i,\ell)}
and applying (
4.1
), we arrive at

\underset{\mathcal{I}_{B,D}}{\mathbb{P}}\left[\mathrm{val}_{\mathcal{I}_{B,D}}(\widetilde{\tau})\geqslant\mathrm{val}_{\mathcal{I}}+\varepsilon\right]\leqslant\exp\left(-\frac{mB\varepsilon^{2}}{16}\right).

Taking union bound over all
|\Sigma|^{|\mathcal{V}_{D}|}=|\Sigma|^{mkD}
possibilities of
\widetilde{\tau}
, we have

\displaystyle\underset{\mathcal{I}_{B,D}}{\mathbb{P}}\left[\mathrm{val}_{\mathcal{I}_{B,D}}\geqslant\mathrm{val}_{\mathcal{I}}+\varepsilon\right]\leqslant|\Sigma|^{mkD}\cdot\exp\left(-\frac{mB\varepsilon^{2}}{16}\right)\leqslant 0.01,

for some constant
B\leqslant\poly(kD|\Sigma|/\varepsilon)\leqslant\poly(k|\Sigma|/\varepsilon)
.
∎

4.3
Efficient Implementation in Multi-Pass Streaming

In this subsection, we combine Yoshida’s local algorithm (
Lemma

4.5
) with the reduction in
Lemma

4.6
to obtain an efficient streaming algorithm for
Theorem

3.7
(restated below).

See
3.7

The idea behind
Theorem

3.7
is, given an instance
\mathcal{I}
, to approximate the LP-value of a randomly sampled instance bounded degree instance
\mathcal{I}_{B,D}
and accept if its value exceeds
c+\varepsilon/2
. Using
Lemma

4.6
we know that (if we are able to produce a good such approximation) with high probability we will accept if
\mathrm{val}_{\mathcal{I}}\geqslant c+\varepsilon
, and reject if
\mathrm{val}_{I}\leqslant\vartheta_{\mathcal{F}}(c)-\varepsilon
.

To approximate the LP value of
\mathcal{I}_{B,D}
we use
Yoshida’s algorithm (
Lemma

4.5
). We integrate the local algorithm into the streaming setting using a similar approach to that of
[
SSSV25
]
. More precisely, we begin by uniformly sampling a set of constraints from
\mathcal{I}_{B,D}
and, using a constant number of passes, recover the constant-radius neighborhoods of these constraints. The local algorithm
\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{loc}}
from Lemma
4.5
is then applied to approximate the contribution of each sampled constraint to the LP objective, and their average is used to estimate the LP value
\mathrm{val}^{\mathrm{LP}}_{\mathcal{I}_{B,D}}
.

The main difference between our setting and the one in
[
SSSV25
]
is that our method requires an
implicit
construction of the bounded degree instance
\mathcal{I}_{B,D}
using only logarithmic space. This is necessary for us, as we cannot afford to sample a full instance
\mathcal{I}_{B,D}
and store it on the memory. In contrast,
[
SSSV25
]
does not apply generic degree-reduction transformations, and instead works directly with the original, potentially unbounded-degree instance
\mathcal{I}
. Their approach, however, is tailored to the specific class of local algorithms they consider, whereas our reduction applies in a black-box manner to any local algorithm, including Yoshida’s.

We now give the formal proof of
Theorem

3.7
.

Proof of
Theorem

3.7
.

We will present the proof in several parts.

Part 1: the reduction oracle.

Ideally, we would like to first transform the input stream
\mathcal{I}=(\mathcal{V},(C_{1},\dots,C_{m}))
into the data stream corresponding to a bounded-degree instance
\mathcal{I}_{B,D}
, as defined by the random reduction procedure in
Algorithm

1
. However, due to constraints on space and the number of passes, we cannot afford to run
Algorithm

1
in its entirety. Instead, we simulate the reduction
on the fly
— computing only local portions of the random instance
\mathcal{I}_{B,D}
when needed and storing them in memory. This approach leads to
Algorithm

2
, a local, streaming-compatible variant of
Algorithm

1
.

Input :
a
\mathrm{CSP}(\mathcal{F})
instance
\mathcal{I}=(\mathcal{V},(C_{1},\dots,C_{m}))
presented as a data stream, and integers
B,D\geqslant 1

Output :
answers queries on
\mathcal{I}_{B,D}
, each given as an index tuple
(i,\ell,t)\in[m]\times[B]\times[k]

Initialize an empty list
L

//
the memory kept by the algorithm

while

(i,\ell,t)\in[m]\times[B]\times[k]

do

1

Suppose
\mathsf{v}
is the
t
-th variable in the scope of
C_{i}

2

Conditioned on
L
do

3

//
using fresh randomness

Determine a
j\in[D\cdot\deg_{\mathcal{I}}(\mathsf{v})]
such that
(\mathsf{v},j)
is the
t
-th variable of
C_{i,\ell}

4

Determine the radius-1 neighborhood
\mathcal{N}=\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{I}_{B,D}}\big((\mathsf{v},j),1\big)

5

6

\mathcal{N}
, including the root vertex
(\mathsf{v},j)
, as the answer to the query

7

Append
\mathcal{N}
to
L

//
memory update

Algorithm 2

Oracle for Answering Queries on
\mathcal{I}_{B,D}
— Local Version of
Algorithm

1

In

\mathsf{v}
is retrieved; the second in

\mathsf{v}

\mathcal{I}

Algorithm

, where the relevant constraints to include in the neighborhood of the variable
(\mathsf{v},j)
in
\mathcal{I}_{B,D}
are determined.

Importantly, the responses of
Algorithm

are always consistent with some fixed instantiation of the random instance
\mathcal{I}_{B,D}
, although this instantiation is never computed explicitly. Moreover, its answers to any (possibly adaptive) sequence of queries are indistinguishable from those of a hypothetical oracle that first samples the entire instance
\mathcal{I}_{B,D}
and then responds deterministically to each query.

Input :
a
\mathrm{CSP}(\mathcal{F})

\mathcal{I}=(\mathcal{V},(C_{1},\dots,C_{m}))
presented as a data stream, and integers
B,D,Q,r\geqslant 1

Output :
an approximation of
\mathrm{val}^{\mathrm{LP}}_{\mathcal{I}}
with high probability

\widetilde{\mathrm{val}}\leftarrow 0

Start running

times

Sample

[m]\times[B]

//

Obtain

using multiple queries to
Algorithm

2

4

//
randomness of
Algorithm

2
involved

Using the map
\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{loc}}
from

4.5
to compute
\hat{z}\leftarrow\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{loc}}\Big(\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{I}_{B,D}}\big((i,\ell),r\big)\Big)

5

//
deterministic

Suppose
f_{i}\in\mathcal{F}
is the predicate used by the constraint
C_{i}

6

Add the result to the counter:
\widetilde{\mathrm{val}}\leftarrow\widetilde{\mathrm{val}}+\sum_{b\in\Sigma^{k}}f_{i}(b)\hat{z}_{b}

7

8

return

\widetilde{\mathrm{val}}/Q

9

Algorithm 3

\textsc{ApproxLP}(\mathcal{I},B,D,Q,r)

We can then use the local reduction oracle provided by
Algorithm

2
to build
Algorithm

3
, which samples a constant number of constraints in the bounded-degree instance
\mathcal{I}_{B,D}
(which is in turn implicitly sampled by
Algorithm

) to approximate the LP value
\mathrm{val}^{\mathrm{LP}}_{\mathcal{I}_{B,D}}
.

Part 2: correctness of
Algorithm

3
.

We argue that
Algorithm

3
correctly approximates the LP value of
\mathcal{I}_{B,D}
. First, we let
Q=\lceil 10/\varepsilon_{0}^{2}\rceil
and let
r\leqslant\exp(\poly(kB|\Sigma|/\varepsilon_{0}))
be as in Lemma
4.5
, where
\varepsilon_{0}
is a parameter that only depends on
\varepsilon
and to be determined later. We then pick
B,D\leqslant\poly(k|\Sigma|/\varepsilon_{0})
as in
Lemma

4.6
.

Conditioned on a fixed instantiation of
\mathcal{I}_{B,D}
, the
Q
iterations of the loop on
Algorithm

3
of
Algorithm

3
are independent of each other. Furthermore, for a fixed instantiation of
\mathcal{I}_{B,D}
, if we let
\hat{z}^{(i,\ell)}
be the vector
\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{loc}}\Big(\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{I}_{B,D}}\big((i,\ell),r\big)\Big)
, the expected value of each increment to the counter on
Algorithm

3
of
Algorithm

3
is

\underset{(i,\ell)\text{ chosen on \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{line:sample_constraint}}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\sum_{b\in\Sigma^{k}}f_{i}(b)\hat{z}_{b}\right]=\frac{1}{mB}\sum_{(i,\ell)\in[m]\times[B]}\left(\sum_{b\in\Sigma^{k}}f_{i}(b)\hat{z}^{(i,\ell)}_{b}\right),

We denote this expected value by
\mathrm{val}^{\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{loc}}}_{\mathcal{I}_{B,D}}
. By Hoeffding’s inequality (
Proposition

2.3
), it follows that for a fixed instantiation of
\mathcal{I}_{B,D}
,

\underset{\textsc{ApproxLP}}{\mathbb{P}}\left[\left|\textsc{ApproxLP}(\mathcal{I},B,D,Q,r)-\mathrm{val}^{\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{loc}}}_{\mathcal{I}_{B,D}}\right|\geqslant\varepsilon_{0}\,\Bigg|\,\mathcal{I}_{B,D}\right]\leqslant 2\exp(-2\varepsilon_{0}^{2}Q)\leqslant 0.01,

(4.2)

Recall that Lemma
4.5
guarantees

\mathrm{val}^{\mathrm{LP}}_{\mathcal{I}_{B,D}}-\varepsilon_{0}\leqslant\mathrm{val}^{\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{loc}}}_{\mathcal{I}_{B,D}}\leqslant\mathrm{val}^{\mathrm{LP}}_{\mathcal{I}_{B,D}}+\varepsilon_{0}.

(4.3)

Combining (
4.2
) and (
4.3
), we conclude that

\underset{\textsc{ApproxLP}}{\mathbb{P}}\left[\left|\textsc{ApproxLP}(\mathcal{I},B,D,Q,r)-\mathrm{val}^{\mathrm{LP}}_{\mathcal{I}_{B,D}}\right|\geqslant 2\varepsilon_{0}\,\Bigg|\,\mathcal{I}_{B,D}\right]\leqslant 0.01.

(4.4)

Part 3: efficiency of
Algorithm

3
.

We begin by providing an upper bound on the number of passes used by the algorithm
\textsc{ApproxLP}(\cdot,B,D,Q,r)
. Aside from the initial pass to determine the number of constraints
m
, only
Algorithm

3
in
Algorithm

3
requires access to the input stream. In other words, after the first pass, all subsequent passes over the input are delegated to
Algorithm

2
.

Each execution of
Algorithm

3
makes at most
(\max\{B,k\})^{r+1}
queries to
Algorithm

2
, and each query incurs 3 passes over the input stream. Since
Algorithm

3
is executed
Q
times, the total number of passes required by
Algorithm

3
is
O(Q\cdot(Bk)^{r+1})\leqslant\exp(\exp(\poly(k|\Sigma|/\varepsilon_{0})))
.

Regarding space complexity, it is straightforward to observe that the memory usage of
Algorithm

2
grows linearly with the number of queries made, with each query contributing an additional
O(\log n)
bits. Moreover, the memory maintained internally by
Algorithm

3
(i.e., not delegated to
Algorithm

2
) is at most comparable to that used by the oracle. Therefore, the overall space complexity of
Algorithm

3
is
O_{k,|\Sigma|,\varepsilon_{0}}(\log n)
.

Finally, we note that
Algorithm

3
never runs out of fresh random bits during the execution of
Algorithm

3
. The only steps requiring fresh randomness are
Algorithms

2
and
2
of
Algorithm

2
and
Algorithm

3
of
Algorithm

3
. These steps are executed only constant times in total. Furthermore, each such operation consumes only
O(\log n)
random bits, which can be readily generated within the
O(\log n)
-space budget of our streaming algorithm (see
Definition

2.2
).

Part 4: wrapping up.

We now summarize the preceding components into a complete algorithm for
Theorem

3.7
, presented below as
Algorithm

4
.

Input :
A
\mathrm{CSP}(\mathcal{F})
instance
\mathcal{I}
presented as a data stream

Output :
0 if
\mathrm{val}_{\mathcal{I}}\leqslant\vartheta_{\mathcal{F}}(c)-\varepsilon
; 1 if
\mathrm{val}_{\mathcal{I}}\geqslant c+\varepsilon
; both with probability at least
2/3

Let
c^{\prime}
be any rational number in
[c+2\varepsilon/5,\,c+3\varepsilon/5]

1

Choose a rational number
\varepsilon_{0}\in(0,\varepsilon/5)
and set
Q=\lceil 10/\varepsilon_{0}^{2}\rceil

2

Select parameters
r,B,D
according to
Lemmas

4.5
and
4.6

3

Run
\textsc{ApproxLP}(\mathcal{I},B,D,Q,r)
(
Algorithm

3
)

4

if

the output is at least
c^{\prime}

then

5

return

1

else

6

return

0

Algorithm 4

Final Algorithm
\mathcal{A}
for
Theorem

3.7

If
\mathrm{val}_{\mathcal{I}}\geqslant c+\varepsilon
, then since
\mathrm{val}^{\mathrm{LP}}_{\mathcal{I}_{B,D}}\geqslant\mathrm{val}_{\mathcal{I}_{B,D}}\geqslant\mathrm{val}_{\mathcal{I}}
always holds (by
Lemma

4.6
), we get from (
4.4
) that

\mathbb{P}\left[\textsc{ApproxLP}(\mathcal{I},B,D,Q,r)\geqslant c+\varepsilon-2\varepsilon_{0}\right]\geqslant 0.99.

Since
c+\varepsilon-2\varepsilon_{0}\geqslant c^{\prime}
, it follows that
\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{A}}\left[\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{I})=1\right]\geqslant 0.99\geqslant 2/3
.

Conversely, if
\mathrm{val}_{\mathcal{I}}\leqslant\vartheta_{\mathcal{F}}(c)-\varepsilon
, then by
Lemma

4.6
, we have
\mathrm{val}_{\mathcal{I}_{B,D}}\leqslant\vartheta_{\mathcal{F}}(c)-\varepsilon+\varepsilon_{0}
with probability at least
0.99
. From
Definition

3.4
, whenever
\mathrm{val}_{\mathcal{I}_{B,D}}<\vartheta_{\mathcal{F}}(c)
, it holds that
\mathrm{val}^{\mathrm{LP}}_{\mathcal{I}_{B,D}}<c
, and in that case, we get from (
4.4
) that

\mathbb{P}\left[\textsc{ApproxLP}(\mathcal{I},B,D,Q,r)<c+2\varepsilon_{0}\,\Big|\,\mathcal{I}_{B,D}\right]\geqslant 0.99.

Since
c+2\varepsilon_{0}\leqslant c^{\prime}
, we conclude that
\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{A}}\left[\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{I})=0\right]\geqslant 0.99^{2}\geqslant 2/3
.

Finally, by the analysis in part 3 of this proof, the algorithm
\mathcal{A}
requires
O_{\varepsilon}(1)
passes and
O_{\varepsilon}(\log n)
bits of memory.
∎

5
Streaming Lower Bound from Communication Complexity

As shown in
Theorem

3.7
, efficient constant-pass streaming algorithms can approximately match the performance of the basic linear programming relaxation for approximating CSPs. In this section, we establish the complementary hardness result,
Theorem

3.8
, which says that multi-pass streaming algorithms cannot outperform the linear programming relaxation by a constant margin.

In
Theorem

3.8
, we are given a fixed integrality gap instance
\mathcal{I}\in\mathrm{CSP}(\mathcal{F})
, with
\mathrm{val}_{\mathcal{I}}=s
and
\mathrm{val}^{\mathrm{LP}}_{\mathcal{I}}=c
. To establish the hardness of the gap problem
\mathsf{MaxCSP}(\mathcal{F})[c-\varepsilon,s+\varepsilon]
, we construct two probability distributions over
\mathrm{CSP}(\mathcal{F})
instances, referred to as the YES and NO distributions. Instances drawn from the YES distribution typically have value at least
c-\varepsilon
, while those from the NO distribution typically have value at most
s+\varepsilon
. The goal is to show that any
p
-pass streaming algorithm with limited memory cannot reliably distinguish between instances sampled from these two distributions.

The construction of the YES and NO distributions largely follows the ideas of
[
Yos11
]
. Starting from the fixed integrality gap instance
\mathcal{I}
, we perform a blow-up: each variable is replaced with
n
copies, and each constraint is replaced with
O(n)
copies. We then define two different but streaming-indistinguishable methods for selecting which variable copies appear in each constraint copy. These two selection procedures yield the YES and NO distributions, respectively.

The key distinction between our hardness result and that of
[
Yos11
]
lies in the model of computation: we aim to establish lower bounds against multi-pass streaming algorithms, which are potentially more powerful than the query-based property testing algorithms considered in
[
Yos11
]
. To more cleanly capture the broader range of behaviors that a streaming algorithm might exhibit when processing inputs from the YES and NO distributions, we introduce an abstract communication game, called
\mathsf{DIHP}(G,n,\alpha,K)
, that models these possibilities. This communication game is in turn based on an abstract object
G
that we call a
distribution-labeled
k
-graph
.

This section is structured as follows. In
Sections

5.1
,
5.2
and
5.3
, we introduce the abstract mathematical objects underlying the communication game
\mathsf{DIHP}(G,n,\alpha,K)
. The formal definition of the communication game is given in
Section

5.4
. In
Section

5.5
, we explain how the communication complexity of
\mathsf{DIHP}(G,n,\alpha,K)
yields the desired lower bound against streaming algorithms. Proving a communication lower bound of
\mathsf{DIHP}(G,n,\alpha,K)
will be the subject of
Sections

6
,
7
and
8
.

5.1
Labeled Matchings

Labeled matchings are combinatorial objects that have been widely used in establishing streaming lower bounds for approximating CSPs (e.g.
[
KKS14
,
KK19
,
CGSV24
,
FMW25
]
). In
[
FMW25
]
especially, the
space
of labeled matchings plays a prominent role in proof of the lower bound, and a significant emphasis was placed on the exploration of Fourier analytic properties of this space. While
[
FMW25
]
studies the space of labeled matchings on a
complete graph
, which is tailored to the specific CSP of Max-Cut, our goal of analyzing general CSPs requires considering labeled matchings on a
complete
k
-partite hypergraph
. The following preliminary definition captures the set-theoretic structure of complete
k
-partite hypergraphs.

Definition 5.1
.

For finite sets
U_{1},\dots,U_{k}
of equal cardinality, we call the tuple
\mathcal{U}=(U_{1},\dots,U_{k})
a
k
-universe
. The
cardinality
of
\mathcal{U}
, denoted by
|\mathcal{U}|
, is defined to be the common cardinality of the sets
U_{i}
. For convenience, we use the shorthand
\textstyle\bigcup\mathcal{U}
for the union
\bigcup_{i\in[k]}U_{i}
and
\textstyle\prod\mathcal{U}
for the Cartesian product
\prod_{i=1}^{k}U_{i}
.

Before introducing labeled matchings, we first introduce a convenient notation for the collection of unlabeled matchings.

Definition 5.2
.

For a
k
-universe
\mathcal{U}
and a nonnegative integer
m\leqslant|\mathcal{U}|
, we let
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{U},m}
denote the collection of all matchings (without labels) in the complete
k
-partite hypergraph
(\textstyle\bigcup\mathcal{U},\textstyle\prod\mathcal{U})
(the hypergraph with vertex set
\textstyle\bigcup\mathcal{U}
and edge set
\textstyle\prod\mathcal{U}
) with
m
edges. We also write
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{U},\leqslant m}:=\bigcup_{d=0}^{m}\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{U},d}
.

We now define the space of labeled matchings as follows.

Definition 5.3
.

For a
k
-universe
\mathcal{U}
and a nonnegative integer
m\leqslant|\mathcal{U}|
, we define the following space of labeled matchings:

\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}:=\left\{\mathbf{y}\in\mathrm{Map}\left(\textstyle\prod\mathcal{U},\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}\cup\{\textup{{nil}}\}\right):\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{y})\text{ is a matching with }m\text{ edges}\right\}.

Here,
\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{y})
denotes the support of
\mathbf{y}
, i.e., the edges in
\prod\mathcal{U}
mapped to
\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}
(see
Section

2.1
).

Note that the labels on edges of the matchings are elements of
\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}
. This differs from the usual
\mathbb{F}_{2}
-labels considered for Max-Cut, and aligns with what has been used for general CSPs
[
CGSV24
]
.

5.2
The Markov Kernel

In previous works on the Max-Cut problem
[
KKS14
,
KK19
,
FMW25
]
, an important concept used in the construction of the YES distribution is random generation of labeled matchings that are
compatible
with a given bipartition of a vertex set. However, as already evidenced by
[
CGSV24
]
, it turns out that for studying general CSPs, the black-and-white notion of compatibility needs to be relaxed into a range of probabilities in
[0,1]
. The more general formalism is that of a
Markov transition
from the space of bipartitions (or, for us,
N
-partitions) to the space of labeled matchings.

The following notation will be helpful in defining the Markov transition, as well as in later parts of the paper.

Notation 5.4
.

Suppose
\Lambda
is a ground set and
x\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\Lambda}
is a
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
-vector indexed by
\Lambda
. If
e=(v_{1},\dots,v_{k})
is a tuple of elements with each
v_{i}\in\Lambda
for
i\in[k]
, we denote by
x_{|e}
the vector
(x_{v_{1}},\dots,x_{v_{k}})\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}
.

We now define the matrix specifying the probability that we want to draw each labeled matching given an “
N
-partition” of the vertices. Such matrices are known as
Markov kernels
.

Definition 5.5
.

Fix a
k
-universe
\mathcal{U}
, a positive integer
m\leqslant|\mathcal{U}|
, and a one-wise independent distribution
\mu
over
\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}
. We define a right stochastic matrix
\mathbf{P}^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\mu}:\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\bigcup\mathcal{U}}\times\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}\rightarrow[0,\infty)
as follows. For each
x\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\bigcup\mathcal{U}}
and
\mathbf{y}\in\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}
, the entry
\mathbf{P}^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\mu}(x,\mathbf{y})
is the probability that the output of the following process equals
\mathbf{y}
:

1.

sample a matching
M
uniformly at random from
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{U},m}
;

2.

let
\mathbf{z}\in\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}
have support
\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z})=M
, and

3.

for each edge
e\in M
, draw
w_{e}\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}
independently from
\mu
and set
\mathbf{z}(e)=x_{|e}-w_{e}
, where the subtraction is performed in the Abelian group
\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}
;

4.

output
\mathbf{z}
.

As noted earlier, the seed vector
x\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\bigcup\mathcal{U}}
can be viewed as an
N
-partition of the vertices in
\textstyle\bigcup\mathcal{U}
. A uniformly random matching
M
is drawn, and the label
\mathbf{y}(e)
on each edge
e\in M
reveals certain information about
x_{|e}
, i.e., how the vertices of
e
are partitioned. Due to the “masking vector”
w_{e}
drawn from the one-wise independent distribution
\mu
, no information is revealed about
x_{v}
for any single vertex
v
. However, information about correlations may be revealed — for instance, if
u
and
v
are two vertices of a same edge
e\in M
and
\mu
is the uniform distribution on diagonal elements of
\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}
, the difference
x_{u}-x_{v}\pmod{N}
might be completely recoverable from
\mathbf{y}(e)
. We will formalize this intuition using Fourier analysis later in the paper (see
Section

8.4
).

5.3
Distribution-Labeled
k
-Graphs

As promised in the introductory text of
Section

5
, the communication game
\mathsf{DIHP}(G,n,\alpha,K)
(to be defined in
Section

5.4
) is based on an abtract structure
G
called a distribution-labeled
k
-graph, which we now define as follows.

Definition 5.6
.

A
distribution-labeled
k
-graph

G
consists of the following data: a vertex set
\mathcal{V}
; a multi-set
\mathcal{E}
of hyperedges, each an ordered
k
-tuple of distinct vertices in
\mathcal{V}
; a positive integer
N
; and a collection of probability distributions
(\mu_{\mathsf{e}})_{\mathsf{e}\in\mathcal{E}}
, where each
\mu_{\mathsf{e}}
is a one-wise independent probability distribution on the Abelian group
\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}
.

Having made clear the first parameter
G
in
\mathsf{DIHP}(G,n,\alpha,K)
, we now turn to the second parameter
n
: this is the blow-up factor of the distribution-labeled
k
-graph
G
. Indeed, the communication game is not played over
G
itself, but rather over the
n
-fold blow-up of
G
. The set-theoretic structure of the blow-up is captured by the following definition.

Definition 5.7
.

Given a distribution-labeled
k
-graph
G=(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E},N,(\mu_{\mathsf{e}})_{\mathsf{e}\in\mathcal{E}})
and a positive integer
n
, we define the following associated combinatorial objects.

1.

The set
\mathcal{V}\times[n]
, i.e. the
n
-blow-up of the vertex set
\mathcal{V}
, will be referred to as the ground set.

2.

For each
\mathsf{v}\in\mathcal{V}
, let
U_{\mathsf{v}}:=\{\mathsf{v}\}\times[n]
be the subset of
\mathcal{V}\times[n]
consisting of the
n
copies of
\mathsf{v}
.

3.

We associate with each hyperedge
\mathsf{e}=(\mathsf{v}_{1},\dots,\mathsf{v}_{k})\in\mathcal{E}
the
k
-universe
\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{e}}:=(U_{\mathsf{v}_{1}},\dots,U_{\mathsf{v}_{k}})
.

5.4
The Communication Game

The following notation will be helpful in defining the communication game, as well as in later parts of the paper.

Definition 5.8
.

Fix a distribution-labeled
k
-graph
G=(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E},N,(\mu_{\mathsf{e}})_{\mathsf{e}\in\mathcal{E}})
. The Abelian group
\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\mathcal{V}\times[n]}
will play a central role throughout
Sections

5
and
7
. For each edge
\mathsf{e}\in\mathcal{E}
, recall from
Definition

5.7
that
\bigcup\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{e}}\subseteq\mathcal{V}\times[n]
. We denote by
\mathrm{proj}_{\mathsf{e}}
the canonical projection from
\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\mathcal{V}\times[n]}
onto
\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\bigcup\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{e}}}
.

We are now ready to define the communication game
\mathsf{DIHP}(G,n,\alpha,K)
.

Definition 5.9
.

Given a distribution-labeled
k
-graph
G=(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E},N,(\mu_{\mathsf{e}})_{\mathsf{e}\in\mathcal{E}})
, parameters
n,K\in\mathbb{N}
and
\alpha\in(0,1)
, we define the communication game
\mathsf{DIHP}(G,n,\alpha,K)
as follows:

1.

There are
|\mathcal{E}|\cdot K
players, each indexed by a pair
(\mathsf{e},j)
, where
\mathsf{e}\in\mathcal{E}
and
j\in[K]
.

2.

Each player
(\mathsf{e},j)
receives as input a labeled matching in
\Omega^{\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{e}},\alpha n}
.

3.

The no distribution:
define
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{no}}
to be the uniform distribution on the Cartesian product
\prod_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}\Omega^{\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{e}},\alpha n}
, i.e. each player gets a independent uniformly random input.

4.

The yes distribution:
define
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{yes}}
to be the joint distribution of
(\mathbf{y}^{(\mathsf{e},j)})_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}
obtained by the following procedure:

•

Sample a uniformly random vector
\widetilde{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\mathcal{V}\times[n]}
.

•

For each player
(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]
, independently draw a labeled matching
\mathbf{y}\in\Omega^{\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{e}},\alpha n}
according to the distribution given by the probability mass function
\mathbf{P}^{\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{e}},\alpha n}_{\mu_{\mathsf{e}}}\big(\mathrm{proj}_{\mathsf{e}}(\widetilde{x}),\cdot\big)
.

The goal of the players is to decide whether their inputs
(\mathbf{y}^{(\mathsf{e},j)})_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}
comes from
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{yes}}
or
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{no}}
.

Remark 5.10
.

Throughout this paper, whenever we refer to the communication game
\mathsf{DIHP}(G,n,\alpha,K)
, we treat
G,\alpha
and
K
as fixed parameters, and consider the asymptotic regime
n\rightarrow\infty
.

As is standard in distributional communication complexity, we measure the performance of a communication protocol by its “advantage”, defined as follows.

Definition 5.11
.

A deterministic communication protocol
\Pi
for
\mathsf{DIHP}(G,n,\alpha,K)
computes a function
\Pi:\prod_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}\Omega^{\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{e}},\alpha n}\rightarrow\{0,1\}
. We define its
advantage
in the communication game as

\displaystyle\mathrm{adv}(\Pi):=\left|\underset{\mathbf{Y}\sim\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{yes}}}{\mathbb{P}}\left[\Pi(\mathbf{Y})=1\right]-\underset{\mathbf{Y}\sim\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{no}}}{\mathbb{P}}\left[\Pi(\mathbf{Y})=1\right]\right|,

where
\mathbf{Y}
denotes a joint input
\mathbf{Y}=(\mathbf{y}^{(\mathsf{e},j)})_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}
.

Since a randomized communication protocol is a distribution over deterministic protocols, it may well be replaced by the deterministic protocol in its support with the highest advantage. It is therefore without loss of generality to only consider deterministic protocols for
\mathsf{DIHP}(G,n,\alpha,K)
.

The communication complexity of
\mathsf{DIHP}
is then defined as follows.

Definition 5.12
.

The
communication cost
of a protocol
\Pi
, denoted by
|\Pi|
, is the total number of bits broadcasted by all players across all rounds during its execution.
The
communication complexity
of the game
\mathsf{DIHP}(G,n,\alpha,K)
, denoted by
\mathsf{CC}(G,n,\alpha,K)
, is the minimum communication cost over all protocols
\Pi
that satisfy
\mathrm{adv}(\Pi)\geqslant 0.1
.

We have the following communication lower bound for the
\mathsf{DIHP}(G,n,\alpha,K)
problem, the proof of which will occupy
Sections

6
,
7
and
8
.

Theorem 5.13
.

Fix a distribution-labeled
k
-graph
G
, an integer
K>0
and a parameter
\alpha\in\big(0,10^{-8}k^{-3}\big]
. There exists a constant
\gamma=\gamma(G,\alpha,K)>0
such that
\mathsf{CC}(G,n,\alpha,K)\geqslant\gamma n^{1/3}
.

5.5
Streaming Lower Bound

It is now time to demonstrate how the communication complexity of
\mathsf{DIHP}(G,n,\alpha,K)
is related to lower bounds for streaming approximation of CSPs and give a proof of
Theorem

3.8
. We present the general reduction lemma from
\mathsf{DIHP}(G,n,\alpha,K)
to
\mathsf{MaxCSP}(\mathcal{F})[c-\varepsilon,c+\varepsilon]
:

Lemma 5.14
.

Fix a nonempty
\mathrm{CSP}(\mathcal{F})
instance
\mathcal{I}=(\mathcal{V},(C_{1},\dots,C_{m}))
, and let
s:=\mathrm{val}_{\mathcal{I}}
and
c:=\mathrm{val}^{\mathrm{LP}}_{\mathcal{I}}
.
Then there exists a distribution-labeled
k
-graph
G=(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E},N,(\mu_{\mathsf{e}})_{\mathsf{e}\in\mathcal{E}})
such that for any fixed error parameter
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
and constants

\alpha\leqslant(100k)^{-1}\varepsilon\quad\text{and}\quad K\geqslant 100\alpha^{-1}\varepsilon^{-2}N^{2k}\cdot|\mathcal{V}|\log|\Sigma|,

(5.1)

the following holds for sufficiently large
n
:

(1)

If
c<1
, then any
p
-pass algorithm for
\mathsf{MaxCSP}(\mathcal{F})[c-\varepsilon,s+\varepsilon]
requires at least
(pmK)^{-1}\cdot\mathsf{CC}(G,n,\alpha,K)
bits of memory on input instances with
|\mathcal{V}|\cdot n
variables.

(2)

If
c=1
, then any
p
-pass algorithm for
\mathsf{MaxCSP}(\mathcal{F})[1,s+\varepsilon]
requires at least
(pmK)^{-1}\cdot\mathsf{CC}(G,n,\alpha,K)
bits of memory on input instances with
|\mathcal{V}|\cdot n
variables.

We observe that
Theorem

3.8
(restated below) follows immediately from this reduction lemma:

See
3.8

Proof of
Theorem

3.8
assuming
Lemma

5.14
.

Suppose
\mathcal{I}=(\mathcal{V},(C_{1},\dots,C_{m}))
. We take constants
\alpha\leqslant\min\{10^{-8}k^{-3},(100k)^{-1}\varepsilon\}
, and
K\geqslant 100\alpha^{-1}
. The conclusions then follow by first applying
Lemma

5.14
and then applying
Theorem

5.13
(note that
m,|\mathcal{V}|,K
are all constants).
∎

Finally, we arrive at the main task of this section, which is to prove
Lemma

5.14
. The following observation will be useful for the proof.

Proposition 5.15
.

For every
\mathrm{CSP}(\mathcal{F})
instance
\mathcal{I}
, there exists an optimal solution to
{\textsc{BasicLP}}_{\mathcal{I}}
(achieving the optimal value
\mathrm{val}^{\mathrm{LP}}_{\mathcal{I}}
) where all variables take rational values.

Proof.

Since all coefficients of the
{\textsc{BasicLP}}_{\mathcal{I}}
are rational and the feasible region is nonempty, by a folklore
9
9

9

See Section 3.7 of
[
LV12
]
for a reference.
result of linear programming, there exists at least one rational-valued optimal solution to
{\textsc{BasicLP}}_{\mathcal{I}}
.
∎

The proof of
Lemma

5.14
is rather lengthy and consists of 5 steps.

Proof of
Lemma

5.14
.

The organization of the proof is as follows. We first construct a distribution-labeled
k
-graph
G
from the gap instance
\mathcal{I}
. Then we present a general scheme of mapping a joint input
\mathbf{Y}
of
\mathsf{DIHP}(G,n,\alpha,K)
to a
\mathrm{CSP}(\mathcal{F})
instance — this also translates
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{yes}}
and
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{no}}
to distributions over
\mathrm{CSP}(\mathcal{F})
. Finally, we prove soundness and completeness of the reduction.

Step 1: construction of
G
.

We construct the distribution-labeled
k
-graph
G
by specifying its four components as follows:

(1)

The vertex set of
G
is simply the variable set
\mathcal{V}
.

(2)

For each constraint
C_{i}=((\mathsf{v}_{i,1},\dots,\mathsf{v}_{i,k}),f_{i})
, define the hyperedge
\mathsf{e}_{i}=(\mathsf{v}_{i,1},\dots,\mathsf{v}_{i,k})
. Let the multi-set
\mathcal{E}=\{\mathsf{e}_{1},\dots,\mathsf{e}_{m}\}
be the edge set of
G
.

(3)

By
Proposition

5.15
, there exist a rational solution
\left((x^{*}_{\mathsf{v},\sigma})_{\mathsf{v}\in\mathcal{V},\,\sigma\in\Sigma},(z^{*}_{i,b})_{i\in[m],\,b\in\Sigma^{k}}\right)
that achieves the optimal LP value
\mathrm{val}^{\mathrm{LP}}_{\mathcal{I}}=c
.
Let
N
be the least common denominator of all
x^{*}_{\mathsf{v},\sigma}
and
z^{*}_{i,b}
. We then scale the values by a factor of
N
:

x^{\prime}_{\mathsf{v},\sigma}:=Nx^{*}_{\mathsf{v},\sigma},\quad z^{\prime}_{i,b}:=Nz^{*}_{i,b}.

These are integers by construction. Also define:

p_{i}^{*}:=\sum_{b\in\Sigma^{k}}f_{i}(b)z^{*}_{i,b},

which represents the contribution of constraint
C_{i}
to the objective under the solution
(x^{*},z^{*})
.

(4)

Fix a total order
\prec
on
\Sigma
. For each vertex
\mathsf{v}\in\mathcal{V}
, define a map
q_{\mathsf{v}}:\mathbb{Z}_{N}\to\Sigma
such that for each
i\in\{0,1,\dots,N-1\}
,

q_{\mathsf{v}}(i)=\sigma\quad\text{if}\quad\sum_{\sigma^{\prime}\prec\sigma}x^{\prime}_{\mathsf{v},\sigma^{\prime}}\leqslant i<\sum_{\sigma^{\prime}\preceq\sigma}x^{\prime}_{\mathsf{v},\sigma^{\prime}}.

This is well-defined since the total sum of
x^{\prime}_{\mathsf{v},\sigma}
over
\sigma\in\Sigma
is
N
. For each
i\in[m]
now we obtain distribution
\mu_{\mathsf{e}_{i}}
over
\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}
from the following process:

•

Sample
b=(b_{1},\dots,b_{k})\in\Sigma^{k}
with probability
z^{*}_{i,b}
;

•

Then uniformly sample
w\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}
from the Cartesian product

q_{\mathsf{v}_{1}}^{-1}(b_{1})\times\cdots\times q_{\mathsf{v}_{k}}^{-1}(b_{k}).

Recall from
Definition

3.1
that the second set of constraints in
BasicLP
ensures

\sum_{b\in\Sigma^{k}}\mathbbm{1}\left\{b_{j}=\sigma\right\}\cdot z^{*}_{i,b}=x^{*}_{\mathsf{v},\sigma}

if
\mathsf{v}
is the
j
-th variable of
C_{i}
. Therefore, a sample
w
from
\mu_{\mathsf{e}_{i}}
has probability exactly
x^{*}_{\mathsf{v},\sigma}
of falling in the set
\{w\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}:w_{j}\in q_{\mathsf{v}}^{-1}(\sigma)\}
. Since each pre-image
q^{-1}_{\mathsf{v}}(\sigma)
has cardinality exactly
x^{\prime}_{\mathsf{v},\sigma}=Nx^{*}_{\mathsf{v},\sigma}
, it follows that
\mu_{\mathsf{e}_{i}}
is one-wise independent.

The distribution-labeled
k
-graph
G
defined above, together with fixed constants satisfying (
5.1
), specifies a communication game
\mathsf{DIHP}(G,n,\alpha,K)
. The following three steps together give a reduction from
\mathsf{DIHP}(G,n,\alpha,K)
to
\mathsf{MaxCSP}(\mathcal{F})[c-\varepsilon,s+\varepsilon]
(or
\mathsf{MaxCSP}(\mathcal{F})[c,s+\varepsilon]
when
c=1
).

Step 2: the reduction map.

Recall that in the communication game
\mathsf{DIHP}(G,n,\alpha,K)
, each player
(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]
has a labeled matching
\mathbf{y}^{(\mathsf{e},j)}\in\Omega^{\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{e}},\alpha n}
in hand. As promised in the beginning of the proof, in this step we show how to map a joint input
\mathbf{Y}=(\mathbf{y}^{(\mathsf{e},j)})_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}
in the communication game to a
\mathrm{CSP}(\mathcal{F})
instance
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbf{Y}}
. The construction of
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbf{Y}}
is as follows:

(1)

The variable set of
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbf{Y}}
is
\mathcal{V}\times[n]
, which is also the ground set in the game
\mathsf{DIHP}(G,n,\alpha,K)
.

(2)

Recall that the edge set of
G
is
\mathcal{E}=\{\mathsf{e}_{1},\dots,\mathsf{e}_{m}\}
, where each
\mathsf{e}_{i}
corresponds to a constraint
(\mathsf{e}_{i},f_{i})
in the starting instance
\mathcal{I}
. For each
i\in[m]
and
j\in[K]
, the player
(\mathsf{e}_{i},j)
gets a labeled matching
\mathbf{y}^{(\mathsf{e}_{i},j)}\in\Omega^{\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{e}_{i}},\alpha n}
. Let
M_{i,j}
be the sub-matching of
\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{y}^{(\mathsf{e}_{i},j)})
consisting of all edges
e\in\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{y}^{(\mathsf{e}_{i},j)})
such that
\mathbf{y}^{(\mathsf{e}_{i},j)}(e)=\mathbf{0}
, where
\mathbf{0}
is the identity element of the Abelian group
\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}
. We let
\mathcal{C}^{(i,j)}
be the collection of constraints
(e,f_{i})
where
e
ranges in the matching
M_{i,j}
.

(3)

Finally, the constraint sequence of
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbf{Y}}
, denoted by
\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{Y}}
, is defined to be the concatenation of all constraint sequences
\mathcal{C}^{(i,j)}
for
i\in[m]
and
j\in[K]
.

(4)

Note that as
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbf{Y}}
is meant to be fed to a hypothetical streaming algorithm, we also need to specify the order in which the constraints in
\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{Y}}
appear in the stream. This is straightforward: we fix an arbitrary total order on the index set
[m]\times[K]
, and concatenate the constraint sequences
\mathcal{C}^{(i,j)}
with respect to that order. Within each segment
\mathcal{C}^{(i,j)}
, the individual constraints can be ordered arbitrarily.

This completes the definition of the reduction map.

Step 3: reduction justification.

It is easy to see that a multi-pass streaming algorithm taking input
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbf{Y}}
can be translated back to a communication protocol for
\mathsf{DIHP}(G,n,\alpha,K)
: in any pass whenever the streaming algorithm finishes processing a segment
\mathcal{C}^{(i,j)}
, the player
(\mathsf{e}_{i},j)
in the communication game correspondingly broadcasts the memory state. In this way, any
p
-pass streaming algorithm
\mathcal{A}
that achieves

\left|\underset{\mathbf{Y}\sim\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{yes}}}{\mathbb{P}}\left[\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{I}_{\mathbf{Y}})=1\right]-\underset{\mathbf{Y}\sim\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{no}}}{\mathbb{P}}\left[\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{I}_{\mathbf{Y}})=1\right]\right|\geqslant 0.1

(5.2)

using
S
bits of memory implies a communication protocol
\Pi
for
\mathsf{DIHP}(G,n,\alpha,K)
with
\mathrm{adv}(\Pi)\geqslant 0.1
using
p\cdot mK\cdot S
total bits of communication. We thus conclude that any
p
-pass streaming algorithm that achieves (
5.2
) must use at least
(pmK)^{-1}\cdot\mathsf{CC}(G,n,\alpha,K)
bits of memory.

The next step is to show the completeness and soundness of the reduction: it remains to prove

\displaystyle\underset{\mathbf{Y}\sim\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{yes}}}{\mathbb{P}}\left[\mathrm{val}_{\mathcal{I}_{\mathbf{Y}}}\geqslant c-\varepsilon\right]

\displaystyle\geqslant 1-o_{n}(1),

(5.3)

\displaystyle\underset{\mathbf{Y}\sim\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{no}}}{\mathbb{P}}\left[\mathrm{val}_{\mathcal{I}_{\mathbf{Y}}}\leqslant s+\varepsilon\right]

\displaystyle\geqslant 1-o_{n}(1),

(5.4)

and if
c=1
we will show

\underset{\mathbf{Y}\sim\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{yes}}}{\mathbb{P}}\left[\mathrm{val}_{\mathcal{I}_{\mathbf{Y}}}=1\right]=1.

(5.5)

For the
c<1
case, the combination of (
5.3
) and (
5.4
) imply that any
p
-pass streaming algorithm for
\mathsf{MaxCSP}(\mathcal{F})[c-\varepsilon,s+\varepsilon]
(with error probability at most
1/3
, as per
Definition

1.2
) must satisfy (
5.2
), and thus have memory size at least
(pmK)^{-1}\cdot\mathsf{CC}(G,n,\alpha,K)
on input instances with
|\mathcal{V}|\cdot n
variables (note that
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbf{Y}}
always have
|\mathcal{V}|\cdot n
variables).

For the
c=1
case, the combination of (
5.5
) and (
5.4
) imply that any
p
-pass streaming algorithm for
\mathsf{MaxCSP}(\mathcal{F})[1,s+\varepsilon]
must satisfy (
5.2
), and thus have memory size at least
(pmK)^{-1}\cdot\mathsf{CC}(G,n,\alpha,K)
on input instances with
|\mathcal{V}|\cdot n
variables.

In Step 4 below we prove (
5.3
) and (
5.5
), while (
5.4
) is proven in Step 5.

Step 4: completeness.

Recall from
Definition

5.9
that in the process of drawing a sample
\mathbf{Y}\sim\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{yes}}
, the first step is to sample a random vector
\widetilde{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\mathcal{V}\times[n]}
. For each such vector
\widetilde{x}
, we define an assignment

\widetilde{\tau_{x}}:\mathcal{V}\times[n]\rightarrow\Sigma\quad\text{by letting}\quad\widetilde{\tau_{x}}((\mathsf{v},\ell))=q_{\mathsf{v}}(\widetilde{x}_{(\mathsf{v},\ell)})

for each variable
(\mathsf{v},\ell)\in\mathcal{V}\times[n]
.

Fix a player
(\mathsf{e}_{i}=(\mathsf{v}_{i,1},\dots,\mathsf{v}_{i,k}),j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]
in the communication game
\mathsf{DIHP}(G,n,\alpha,K)
as defined in Step 1. Recall from
Definition

5.9
that the YES-case input
\mathbf{y}^{(\mathsf{e}_{i},j)}
given to this player is determined by:

(1)

the sampled random vector
\widetilde{x}
,

(2)

a random matching
\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{y}^{(\mathsf{e}_{i},j)})
, and

(3)

labels on the edges in
\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{y}^{(\mathsf{e}_{i},j)})
determined by drawing
w_{e}\sim\mu_{\mathsf{e}_{i}}
independently for each
e\in\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{y}^{(\mathsf{e}_{i},j)})
.

According to the reduction map in Step 2, a constraint
(e,f_{i})
is placed if and only if

\mathbf{y}^{(\mathsf{e}_{i},j)}(e){\mathrel{\to}^{\text{def}}}\widetilde{x}_{|e}-w_{e}=\mathbf{0}.

Furthermore, such a constraint is satisfied by the assignment
\widetilde{\tau_{x}}
if and only if

\widetilde{x}_{|e}\in\bigcup_{b\in\Sigma^{k},\,f_{i}(b)=1}q_{\mathsf{v}_{i,1}}^{-1}(b_{1})\times\cdots\times q_{\mathsf{v}_{i,k}}^{-1}(b_{k}).

Therefore, conditioned on
\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{y}^{(\mathsf{e}_{i},j)})
, for each
e\in\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{y}^{(\mathsf{e}_{i},j)})
we have

\underset{\widetilde{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\mathcal{V}\times[m]},\,w_{e}\sim\mu_{\mathsf{e}_{i}}}{\mathbb{P}}\left[(e,f_{i})\text{ is placed in }\mathcal{I}_{\mathbf{Y}}\,\middle|\,\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{y}^{(\mathsf{e}_{i},j)})\right]=\underset{\widetilde{x}_{|e}\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k},\,w_{e}\sim\mu_{\mathsf{e}_{i}}}{\mathbb{P}}\left[\widetilde{x}_{e}=w_{e}\right]=\frac{1}{N^{k}}

(5.6)

and

\displaystyle\quad\underset{\widetilde{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\mathcal{V}\times[m]},\,w_{e}\sim\mu_{\mathsf{e}_{i}}}{\mathbb{P}}\left[(e,f_{i})\text{ is placed in }\mathcal{I}_{\mathbf{Y}}\text{ and satisfied by }\widetilde{\tau_{x}}\,\middle|\,\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{y}^{(\mathsf{e}_{i},j)})\right]

\displaystyle=\underset{\widetilde{x}_{|e}\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k},\,w_{e}\sim\mu_{\mathsf{e}_{i}}}{\mathbb{P}}\left[\widetilde{x}_{e}=w_{e}\quad\text{and}\quad\widetilde{x}_{|e}\in\bigcup_{b\in\Sigma^{k},\,f_{i}(b)=1}q_{\mathsf{v}_{i,1}}^{-1}(b_{1})\times\cdots\times q_{\mathsf{v}_{i,k}}^{-1}(b_{k})\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N^{k}}\sum_{b\in\Sigma^{k},\,f_{i}(b)=1}\mu_{\mathsf{e}_{i}}\left(q_{\mathsf{v}_{i,1}}^{-1}(b_{1})\times\cdots\times q_{\mathsf{v}_{i,k}}^{-1}(b_{k})\right)=\frac{1}{N^{k}}\sum_{b\in\Sigma^{k}}f_{i}(b)z^{*}_{i,b}.

(5.7)

The
c=1
case:
in this case
\mathrm{val}^{\mathrm{LP}}_{\mathcal{I}}{\mathrel{\to}^{\text{def}}}c=1
, which means
\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{b\in\Sigma^{k}}f_{i}(b)z^{*}_{i,b}=1
. By
3.2
, we must have
\sum_{b\in\Sigma^{k}}f_{i}(b)z^{*}_{i,b}=1
for all
i\in[m]
. From (
5.6
) and (
5.7
) we know that this implies all constraints that are placed in
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbf{Y}}
by any player are satisfied by
\widetilde{\tau_{x}}
with probability 1. This proves (
5.5
).

The
c<1
case:
we let
X^{(i,j)}
be the number of constraints placed by the player
(\mathsf{e}_{i},j)
(in other words, the length of the sequence
\mathcal{C}^{(i,j)}
) and let
Z^{(i,j)}
be the number of those constraints satisfied by
\widetilde{\tau_{x}}
. Due to independence among edges
e\in\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{y}^{(\mathsf{e}_{i},j)})
and Hoeffding’s inequality (
Proposition

2.3
), we have

\underset{\mathbf{Y}\sim\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{yes}}}{\mathbb{P}}\left[X^{(i,j)}\geqslant(1+\varepsilon/2)N^{-k}\alpha n\,\middle|\,\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{y}^{(\mathsf{e}_{i},j)})\right]\leqslant\exp\left(-\alpha n\cdot\varepsilon^{2}N^{-2k}/16\right)

by (
5.6
) and

\underset{\mathbf{Y}\sim\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{yes}}}{\mathbb{P}}\left[Z^{(i,j)}\leqslant\left(\big(\textstyle\sum_{b\in\Sigma^{k}}f_{i}(b)z^{*}_{i,b}\big)-\varepsilon/2\right)N^{-k}\alpha n\,\middle|\,\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{y}^{(\mathsf{e}_{i},j)})\right]\leqslant\exp\left(-\alpha n\cdot\varepsilon^{2}N^{-2k}/16\right)

by (
5.7
).
Now taking expectation over
\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{y}^{(\mathsf{e}_{i},j)})
and taking union bound over all players
(\mathsf{e}_{i},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]
, it follows that with probability at least

1-2mK\exp\left(-\alpha n\cdot\varepsilon^{2}N^{-2k}/16\right)=1-o_{n}(1)

over
\mathbf{Y}\sim\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{yes}}
, we have both

\sum_{(i,j)\in[m]\times[K]}X^{(i,j)}\leqslant(1+\varepsilon/2)mK\cdot N^{-k}\alpha n

and

\sum_{(i,j)\in[m]\times[K]}Z^{(i,j)}\geqslant\left(\frac{1}{m}\big(\textstyle\sum_{i=1}^{m}\textstyle\sum_{b\in\Sigma^{k}}f_{i}(b)z^{*}_{i,b}\big)-\varepsilon/2\right)mK\cdot N^{-k}\alpha n=(c-\varepsilon/2)mK\cdot N^{-k}\alpha n

and hence

\mathrm{val}_{\mathcal{I}_{\mathbf{Y}}}(\widetilde{\tau_{x}})=\frac{\sum_{(i,j)\in[m]\times[K]}Z^{(i,j)}}{\sum_{(i,j)\in[m]\times[K]}X^{(i,j)}}\geqslant\frac{c-\varepsilon/2}{1+\varepsilon/2}\geqslant c-\varepsilon.

This proves (
5.3
).

Step 5: soundness.

In order to upper bound
\mathrm{val}_{\mathcal{I}_{\mathbf{Y}}}
with high probability, we upper bound the value of any fixed assignment
\widetilde{\tau}:\mathcal{V}\times[n]\rightarrow\Sigma
under
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbf{Y}}
with high probability. Similarly to the proof of
Lemma

4.6
, we define an associated
random
assignment
\tau:\mathcal{V}\rightarrow\Sigma
by assigning value
\sigma\in\Sigma
to
\mathsf{v}\in\mathcal{V}
with probability

\displaystyle\frac{\Big|\big\{j\in[n]\,\big|\,\widetilde{\tau}((\mathsf{v},j))=\sigma\big\}\Big|}{n},

independently for each
v\in\mathcal{V}
.

We define similar random variables as in Step 4: let
X^{(i,j)}
be the number of constraints placed by the player
(\mathsf{e}_{i},j)
into
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbf{Y}}
, and let
Z^{(i,j)}
be the number of those constraints satisfied by
\widetilde{\tau}
. Note that unlike in Step 4, the assignment
\widetilde{\tau}
is fixed, and all randomness lies in
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbf{Y}}
. According to
Definition

5.9
, in the NO case, and conditioned on the support of
\mathbf{y}^{(\mathsf{e}_{i},j)}
, each edge
e\in\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{y}^{(\mathsf{e}_{i},j)})
contributes a constraint to
\mathcal{I}_{\mathbf{Y}}
independently with probability
1/N^{k}
. Therefore, due to the further independence among players and Hoeffding’s inequality (
Proposition

2.3
), we have

\underset{\mathbf{Y}\sim\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{no}}}{\mathbb{P}}\left[\sum_{j=1}^{K}X^{(i,j)}\leqslant\left(1-\frac{\varepsilon}{4}\right)KN^{-k}\alpha n\right]\leqslant\exp\left(-\frac{K\alpha n}{64}\varepsilon^{2}N^{-2k}\right).

To obtain a high-probability lower bound for
\sum_{j=1}^{K}Z^{(i,j)}
, we use
Proposition

2.4
in the same way as in the proof of
Lemma

4.6
. We can think of each random matching
\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{y}^{(\mathsf{e}_{i},j)})
as the result of a sequential random selection of edges in the hypergraph
(\textstyle\bigcup\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{e}_{i}},\textstyle\prod\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{e}_{i}})
, without replacement of vertices. For a perfectly random edge
e\in\textstyle\prod\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{e}_{i}}
, we have

\underset{e\in\prod\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{e}_{i}}}{\mathbb{P}}\left[(e,f_{i})\text{ is satisfied by }\widetilde{\tau}\right]=\underset{\tau}{\mathbb{P}}\left[C_{i}\text{ is satisfied by }\tau\right].

In the sequential random selection process, the number of available edges at any selection step is at least
(1-\alpha)^{k}n^{k}
. As in the proof of
Lemma

4.6
, it follows that for any
t\in[\alpha n]
, the probability that the
t
-th selected edge in
\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{y}^{(\mathsf{e}_{i},j)})
contributes a constraint
and

\widetilde{\tau}
satisfies it is at most

N^{-k}\cdot(1-\alpha)^{-k}\cdot\underset{\tau}{\mathbb{P}}\left[C_{i}\text{ is satisfied by }\tau\right].

Note that the edge selection processes of all players in
\mathcal{E}\times[K]
are independent, and we may apply
Proposition

2.4
to processes of multiple players combined as a whole. Combining the processes of players
(\mathsf{e}_{i},j)
, where
j
ranges in
[K]
, we conclude that

\underset{\mathbf{Y}\sim\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{no}}}{\mathbb{P}}\left[\sum_{j=1}^{K}Z^{(i,j)}\geqslant\left((1-\alpha)^{-k}\cdot\underset{\tau}{\mathbb{P}}\left[C_{i}\text{ is satisfied by }\tau\right]+\frac{\varepsilon}{4}\right)KN^{-k}\alpha n\right]\leqslant\exp\left(-\frac{K\alpha n}{64}\varepsilon^{2}N^{-2k}\right).

Taking union bound over
i\in[m]
, it follows that with probability at least

1-2m\exp\left(-\alpha n\cdot\varepsilon^{2}N^{-2k}/64\right)

over
\mathbf{Y}\sim\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{no}}
, we have both

\sum_{(i,j)\in[m]\times[K]}X^{(i,j)}\geqslant(1-\varepsilon/4)m\cdot KN^{-k}\alpha n

(5.8)

and

\displaystyle\sum_{(i,j)\in[m]\times[K]}Z^{(i,j)}

\displaystyle\leqslant\left(\frac{1}{m}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{m}(1-\alpha)^{-k}\cdot\mathbb{P}_{\tau}\left[C_{i}\text{ is satisfied by }\tau\right]\right)+\frac{\varepsilon}{4}\right)m\cdot KN^{-k}\alpha n

\displaystyle=\left((1-\alpha)^{-k}\cdot\underset{\tau}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\mathrm{val}_{\mathcal{I}}(\tau)\right]+\frac{\varepsilon}{4}\right)m\cdot KN^{-k}\alpha n

\displaystyle\leqslant\left((1+\varepsilon/4)\cdot s+\varepsilon/4\right)m\cdot KN^{-k}\alpha n.

(5.9)

In the last transition of (
5.9
), we used the definition
\mathbb{E}_{\tau}\left[\mathrm{val}_{\mathcal{I}}(\tau)\right]\leqslant\mathrm{val}_{\mathcal{I}}{\mathrel{\to}^{\text{def}}}s
and the bound
(1-\alpha)^{-k}\leqslant e^{2k/\alpha}\leqslant 1+\varepsilon/4
due to the choice
\alpha\leqslant 10^{-8}\varepsilon k^{-3}
. Whenever both (
5.8
) and (
5.9
) hold, we have

\mathrm{val}_{\mathcal{I}_{\mathbf{Y}}}(\widetilde{\tau})=\frac{\sum_{(i,j)\in[m]\times[K]}Z^{(i,j)}}{\sum_{(i,j)\in[m]\times[K]}X^{(i,j)}}\leqslant\frac{(1+\varepsilon/4)s+\varepsilon/4}{1-\varepsilon/4}\leqslant s+\varepsilon.

Finally, taking a union bound over all
\widetilde{\tau}:\mathcal{V}\times[n]\rightarrow\Sigma
, we conclude that
\mathrm{val}_{\mathcal{I}_{\mathbf{Y}}}\leqslant s+\varepsilon
with probability at least

1-|\Sigma|^{n|\mathcal{V}|}\cdot 2m\exp\left(-\frac{K\alpha n}{64}\varepsilon^{2}N^{-2k}\right)\geqslant 1-o_{n}(1),

due to the choice
K\geqslant 100\alpha^{-1}\varepsilon^{-2}N^{2k}\cdot|\mathcal{V}|\log|\Sigma|
. This completes the proof of (
5.4
) and the proof of the lemma.
∎

6
Communication Lower Bound for DIHP

This section is devoted to the proof of
Theorem

5.13
, which establishes the communication lower bound for the game
\mathsf{DIHP}(G,n,\alpha,K)
. As in
[
FMW25
]
, the argument follows the standard structure-vs.-randomness framework in communication complexity (see e.g.
[
RM97
,
GPW17
]
), and consists of two main steps:

1.

Given a communication protocol
\Pi
with
|\Pi|\lesssim n^{1/3}
, we decompose the rectangles induced by
\Pi
into smaller subrectangles. We show that, after decomposition, most subrectangles are “good” — that is, each carries a well-structured piece of information combined with a controlled form of pseudorandom noise. This is done in the “decomposition lemma”,
Lemma

6.11
.

2.

For each “good” rectangle
R
, we establish a discrepancy bound of the form

|\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{no}}(R)-\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{yes}}(R)|\leqslant 0.001\cdot\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{no}}(R).

This is done in the “discrepancy lemma”,
Lemma

6.12
.

These two steps are then combined to complete the proof of the communication lower bound; see
Lemma

6.13
.

The decomposition lemma (
Lemma

6.11
) closely follows its counterpart in
[
FMW25
]
, and its proof is deferred to
Appendix

A
. In contrast, the discrepancy lemma (
Lemma

6.12
) requires a different treatment than
[
FMW25
, Lemma 2.11]
, and is developed in
Sections

7
and
8
. This (relatively short) section is devoted to laying out the overarching framework that connects these components. In particular, we formalize the notion of “good” rectangles in
Sections

6.1
and
6.2
. Then, in
Section

6.3
, we lay out the main lemmas, from which we derive the desired communication lower bound in
Section

6.4
.

6.1
Pseudorandomness Notions

A “good” rectangle is one in which the structural information and the pseudorandom noise are cleanly separated. In this subsection, we formalize the notions of pseudorandomness for sets of labeled matchings. This will allow us, in
Section

6.2
, to control the pseudorandomness in rectangles.

Throughout this subsection, we fix a
k
-universe
\mathcal{U}=(U_{1},\dots,U_{k})
and a positive integer
m\leqslant|\mathcal{U}|
. We will consider pseudorandomness notions for the space of labeled matchings
\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}
.
Our notion is based on the following type of restriction on the space
\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}
.

Definition 6.1
.

We define the set of
restrictions
to be
\Omega^{\mathcal{U},\leqslant m}:=\bigcup_{0\leqslant d\leqslant m}\Omega^{\mathcal{U},d}
, i.e., the subset of
\mathrm{Map}\left(\textstyle\prod\mathcal{U},\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}\cup\{\textup{{nil}}\}\right)
that consists of all labeled matchings with at most
m
edges. For each such labeled matching
\mathbf{z}\in\Omega^{\mathcal{U},\leqslant m}
, we let
\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\mathbf{z}}\subseteq\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}
be the restricted domain defined by

\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\mathbf{z}}:=\left\{\mathbf{y}\in\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}:\mathbf{y}(e)=\mathbf{z}(e)\text{ for all }e\in\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z})\right\}.

In
Definition

6.1
, restrictions are placed on the space of labeled matchings
\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}
. Alternatively, we may view restrictions as directly acting on the universe
\mathcal{U}
, as made precise by the following notation.

Notation 6.2
.

For a matching
M\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{U},\leqslant m}
, we denote by
\mathcal{U}_{\setminus M}
the
k
-universe
(U_{1}^{\prime},U_{2}^{\prime},\dots,U_{k}^{\prime})
defined by setting for each
i\in[k]

U_{i}^{\prime}:=U_{i}\setminus\left\{u:\text{some edge of $M$ has $u$ as its $i$-th vertex}\right\}.

Remark 6.3
.

A key observation is that the restricted domain
\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\mathbf{z}}
is naturally “isomorphic” to the unrestricted domain
\Omega^{\mathcal{U}_{\setminus M},\,m-|M|}
associated to the smaller
k
-universe
\mathcal{U}_{\setminus M}
, where
M:=\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z})
.

Notation 6.4
.

Given a matching
M\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{U},\leqslant m}
, we will use shorthand
\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\setminus M}
to denote the space
\Omega^{\mathcal{U}_{\setminus M},\,m-|M|}
.

Before formalizing the main notion of pseudorandomness, we define the following convenient concept of subsumption of restrictions.

Definition 6.5
.

For two restrictions
\mathbf{z},\mathbf{z}^{\prime}\in\Omega^{\mathcal{U},\leqslant m}
, we say
\mathbf{z}^{\prime}

subsumes

\mathbf{z}
if
\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z})\subseteq\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z}^{\prime})
and for all
e\in\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z})
we have
\mathbf{z}(e)=\mathbf{z}^{\prime}(e)
.

We are now ready to define pseudorandomness for sets of labeled matchings:

Definition 6.6
.

A subset
A\subseteq\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}
is said to be
\mathbf{z}
-global
if
A\subseteq\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\mathbf{z}}
, and for all restrictions
\mathbf{z}^{\prime}
that subsume
\mathbf{z}
we have

\frac{\left|A\cap\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\mathbf{z}^{\prime}}\right|}{\left|\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\mathbf{z}^{\prime}}\right|}\leqslant 2^{|\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z}^{\prime})|-|\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z})|}\cdot\frac{\left|A\cap\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\mathbf{z}}\right|}{\left|\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\mathbf{z}}\right|}.

When
\mathbf{z}=\boldsymbol{0}
is the trivial restriction, we simply say
that
A
is global (omitting the
\mathbf{z}
).

In words, for a set
A
and a restriction
\mathbf{z}
, we say that
A
is

\mathbf{z}
-global if any further restrictions
\mathbf{z}^{\prime}
that subsumes
\mathbf{z}
increases
the relative density of
A
by factor at most
2^{|\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z}^{\prime})|-|\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z})|}
.

Remark 6.7
.

Continuing from
Remark

6.3
, suppose
A\subseteq\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}
is a
\mathbf{z}
-global subset. Then, under the natural identification between the restricted domain
\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\mathbf{z}}
and the unrestricted domain

\Omega^{\mathcal{U}_{\setminus\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z})},\,m-|\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z})|}=\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\setminus\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z})},

the set
A
corresponds to a subset
A_{\mathrm{rem}}\subseteq\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\setminus\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z})}
that is
\boldsymbol{0}
-global. This correspondence follows directly from the definition of globalness and will play an important role in
Section

7
. In particular, when a set
A\subseteq\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\mathbf{z}}
arises and the restriction
\mathbf{z}
is clear from context, we will use the same notation
A_{\mathrm{rem}}
to denote the corresponding subset of the domain
\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\setminus\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z})}
.

6.2
“Good” Rectangles

Now, we turn to pseudorandomness notions for
rectangles
. In this subsection, we fix a distribution-labeled
k
-graph
G=(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E},N,(\mu_{\mathsf{e}})_{\mathsf{e}\in\mathcal{E}]})
and a communication game
\mathsf{DIHP}(G,n,\alpha,K)
.

Recall from
Definition

5.9
that in the communication game
\mathsf{DIHP}(G,n,\alpha,K)
, the joint input to the
|\mathcal{E}|\cdot K
players is an element
\mathbf{Y}
in the product space
\prod_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}\Omega^{\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{e}},\alpha n}
. As is standard in communication complexity, a subset of this product space that is a Cartesian product is referred to as a
rectangle
, formally defined below.

Definition 6.8
.

A subset
R\subseteq\prod_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}\Omega^{\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{e}},\alpha n}
is called a
rectangle
if it is a Cartesian product of sets
A^{(\mathsf{e},j)}\subseteq\Omega^{\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{e}},\alpha n}
, one for each
(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]
; that is,

R=\prod_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}A^{(\mathsf{e},j)}.

Then, it is natural to extend our definitions of global sets to rectangles, which requires each component
A^{(\mathsf{e},j)}
to be a global set.

Definition 6.9
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\zeta}=\left(\mathbf{z}^{(\mathsf{e},j)}\right)_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}
be a sequence where each
\mathbf{z}^{(\mathsf{e},j)}
is a restriction on the space
\Omega^{\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{e}},\alpha n}
. A rectangle
R=\prod_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}A^{(\mathsf{e},j)}
is called
\boldsymbol{\zeta}
-global
if each set
A^{(\mathsf{e},j)}
is
\mathbf{z}^{(\mathsf{e},j)}
-global. When a rectangle
R
is
\boldsymbol{\zeta}
-global, we also say that the pair
(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)
is a
structured rectangle
.
