We are now ready to give the formal definition of “good” rectangles. The rationale behind the three technical requirements in the following definition will become clear in
Section

7
.

Definition 6.10
.

Let
W
be a positive real number. We say a structured rectangle
(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)
, where
R=\prod_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}A^{(\mathsf{e},j)}
and
\boldsymbol{\zeta}=\left(\mathbf{z}^{(\mathsf{e},j)}\right)_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}
, is
W
-good if the following conditions hold:

(1)

The hyperedge sets
\left(\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z}^{(\mathsf{e},j)})\right)_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}
are pairwise disjoint, and their union does not contain any cycle (for the definition of cycle-freeness in hypergraphs, see
Section

2.1
).

(2)

\sum_{(\mathsf{e},j)}\left|\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z}_{(\mathsf{e},j)})\right|\leqslant W
.

(3)

\left|A^{(\mathsf{e},j)}\right|/\left|\Omega^{\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{e}},\alpha n}_{\mathbf{z}^{(\mathsf{e},j)}}\right|\geqslant 2^{-W}
for all
(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]
.

6.3
Two Main Lemmas

We now present the two main lemmas as promised in the introductory text of
Section

6
.

The
decomposition lemma
captures the following fact: a protocol
\Pi
induces at most
2^{|\Pi|}
of rectangles, most rectangles
R
of measure
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{no}}(R)\gtrsim 2^{-|\Pi|}
; then, if
|\Pi|\lesssim\sqrt{n}
holds, one can decompose those rectangles into structured rectangles while most of the structured rectangles are
\Theta(|\Pi|)
-good.

Lemma 6.11

(Decomposition lemma)
.

Fix a distribution-labeled
k
-graph
G=(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E},N,(\mu_{\mathsf{e}})_{\mathsf{e}\in\mathcal{E}})
, an integer
K>0
and a parameter
\alpha>0
. There exists a constant
\eta>0
such that given any communication protocol
\Pi
for
\mathsf{DIHP}(G,n,\alpha,K)
with
|\Pi|\leqslant\eta\sqrt{n}
, there exists a collection
\mathcal{R}
of pairwise-disjoint structured rectangles
(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)
in the space
\prod_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}\Omega^{\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{e}},\alpha n}
such that the following conditions hold:

(1)

\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{no}}\left(\bigcup_{(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)\in\mathcal{R}}R\right)\geqslant 0.99
.

(2)

Each
(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)\in\mathcal{R}
is
\left(10^{5}\cdot|\Pi|\right)
-good.

(3)

For each
(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)\in\mathcal{R}
, there exists
a_{R}\in\{0,1\}
such that
\Pi(\mathbf{Y})=a_{R}
for every
\mathbf{Y}\in R
.

The proof of Lemma
6.11
is included in Appendix
A
. Furthermore, for the good rectangles, we have the following discrepancy bound:

Lemma 6.12

(Discrepancy lemma)
.

Fix a distribution-labeled
k
-graph
G=(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E},N,(\mu_{\mathsf{e}})_{\mathsf{e}\in\mathcal{E}})
, an integer
K>0
and a parameter
\alpha\in\big(0,10^{-8}k^{-3}\big]
. There exists a constant
\gamma=\gamma(G,\alpha,K)>0
such that for any
(\gamma n^{1/3})
-good structured rectangle
(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)
, we have

\displaystyle|\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{no}}(R)-\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{yes}}(R)|\leqslant 0.001\cdot\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{no}}(R).

The proof of
Lemma

6.12
will take up
Sections

7
and
8
.

6.4
The Communication Lower Bound

We note that
Lemma

6.11
and
Lemma

6.12
differ in their tolerance with respect to the parameter
n
. In particular, the goodness parameter in
Lemma

6.12
scales as
n^{1/3}
, which is the primary reason why our space lower bound in
Theorem

1.4
is limited to
\Omega(n^{1/3})
. If, instead, we were able to establish the discrepancy bound for rectangles with goodness parameter as large as
\Theta(n^{1/2})
, the lower bound on space would improve to
\Omega(n^{1/2})
, as formalized in the following lemma.

Lemma 6.13
.

Fix a distribution-labeled
k
-graph
G=(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E},N,(\mu_{\mathsf{e}})_{\mathsf{e}\in\mathcal{E}})
, an integer
K>0
and a parameter
\alpha>0
. There exists a constant
\eta>0
such that for every
W\leqslant\eta\sqrt{n}
, if
|\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{no}}(R)-\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{yes}}(R)|\leqslant 0.001\cdot\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{no}}(R)
holds for every
W
-good structured rectangle
(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)
, then we have
\mathsf{CC}(G,n,\alpha,K)\geqslant 10^{-5}\cdot W
.

Proof.

Take
\eta
to be the constant obtained from Lemma
6.11
. We fix a communication protocol
\Pi
with
|\Pi|\leqslant 10^{-5}\cdot W
, and we show that
\mathrm{adv}(\Pi)<0.1
.

Since we have
|\Pi|\leqslant 10^{-5}\cdot W\leqslant\eta\sqrt{n}
, we may apply Lemma
6.11
and obtain a collection
\mathcal{R}
of structured rectangles. We know that each pair
(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)\in\mathcal{R}
is
\left(10^{5}\cdot|\Pi|\right)
-good, which is also
W
-good since
10^{5}\cdot|\Pi|\leqslant W
. By the assumption in the statement, we then have

\displaystyle|\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{no}}(R)-\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{yes}}(R)|\leqslant 0.001\cdot\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{no}}(R),

holds for each
(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)\in\mathcal{R}
.

Note that for every
(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)\in\mathcal{R}
, the output of
\Pi
is constant on
R
. By
Definition

5.11
, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{adv}(\Pi)

\displaystyle=\left|\underset{\mathbf{Y}\sim\cal{D}_{\mathrm{yes}}}{\mathbb{P}}\left[\Pi(\mathbf{Y})=1\right]-\underset{\mathbf{Y}\sim\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{no}}}{\mathbb{P}}\left[\Pi(\mathbf{Y})=1\right]\right|

\displaystyle\leqslant\underset{\mathbf{Y}\sim\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{yes}}}{\mathbb{P}}\left[\mathbf{Y}\not\in\bigcup_{(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)\in\mathcal{R}}R\right]+\underset{\mathbf{Y}\sim\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{no}}}{\mathbb{P}}\left[\mathbf{Y}\not\in\bigcup_{(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)\in\mathcal{R}}R\right]+\sum_{(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)\in\mathcal{R}}\left|\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{yes}}(R)-\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{no}}(R)\right|

\displaystyle\leqslant 2\cdot\underset{\mathbf{Y}\sim\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{no}}}{\mathbb{P}}\left[\mathbf{Y}\not\in\bigcup_{(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)\in\mathcal{R}}R\right]+2\cdot\sum_{(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)\in\mathcal{R}}\left|\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{yes}}(R)-\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{no}}(R)\right|

\displaystyle\leqslant 2(1-0.99)+2\sum_{(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)\in\mathcal{R}}0.001\cdot\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{no}}(R)<0.1,

as desired.
∎

Theorem

5.13
then follows easily from
Lemma

6.12
.

Proof of
Theorem

5.13
.

Let
\eta
be the constant obtained from
Lemma

6.13
. Lemma
6.12
tells us that there exists a constant
\gamma>0
such that
|\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{no}}(R)-\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{yes}}(R)|\leqslant 0.001\cdot\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{no}}(R)
holds for all
\left(\gamma n^{1/3}\right)
-good rectangles, and it is clear that
\gamma n^{1/3}\leqslant\eta\sqrt{n}
for large enough
n
. So from
Lemma

6.13
we conclude that
\mathsf{CC}(G,n,\alpha,K)=\Omega(\gamma n^{1/3})=\Omega(n^{1/3})
.
∎

7
Bounding the Discrepancy of Good Rectangles

The goal of this section is to prove
Lemma

6.12
, modulo a Fourier analytic lemma that we prove in
Section

8
. We begin with a high-level overview of the proof strategy.

Recall that in
Lemma

6.12
, we are given a restriction sequence
\boldsymbol{\zeta}=\left(\mathbf{z}^{(\mathsf{e},j)}\right)_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}
and a
\boldsymbol{\zeta}
-global rectangle
R=\prod_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}A^{(\mathsf{e},j)}
. We will define a probability density function
f^{(\mathsf{e},j)}
on
\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\mathcal{V}\times[n]}
, induced by the set
A^{(\mathsf{e},j)}
. It turns out that
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{yes}}(R)
and
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{no}}(R)
can then be related by the identity (see
Lemma

7.5
)

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{yes}}(R)=\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{no}}(R)\cdot\underset{\widetilde{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\mathcal{V}\times[n]}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\prod_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}f^{(\mathsf{e},j)}(\widetilde{x})\right].

(7.1)

Thus, it suffices to show that the expectation of the product
\prod_{(\mathsf{e},j)}f^{(\mathsf{e},j)}(\widetilde{x})
is close to 1.

Now the main difference from the setting of
[
FMW25
]
arises. In
[
FMW25
]
, each of the probability density functions in the product is supported on and approximately uniform over an affine subspace of the underlying vector space. This structure allows the analysis to proceed by restricting to the intersection of these affine subspaces and examining the product of the functions on this intersection.

In our setting, the functions
f^{(\mathsf{e},j)}
are not close to uniform on their support. Instead, we decompose each
f^{(\mathsf{e},j)}
as a product
g^{(\mathsf{e},j)}\cdot h^{(\mathsf{e},j)}
, where
g^{(\mathsf{e},j)}
captures the structured component of
A^{(\mathsf{e},j)}
, and
h^{(\mathsf{e},j)}
models the pseudorandom noise. The structured-only product
\prod_{(\mathsf{e},j)}g^{(\mathsf{e},j)}
will play a similar role to the intersection of affine subspaces in
[
FMW25
]
, while each
h^{(\mathsf{e},j)}
is expected to be close to uniform over the entire space
\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\mathcal{V}\times[n]}
.

When we combine the structured-only product and the pseudorandom parts
h^{(\mathsf{e},j)}
together, we will analyze the overall product using what we call a “hybrid method” (see
Section

7.5
). This method is inspired by
[
FMW25
, Lemma 3.12]
, but also draws from classical hybrid arguments in streaming lower bounds (e.g.,
[
KKS14
,
CGSV24
]
).

7.1
Relating YES and NO Distributions

The goal of this section is to give an explicit formula of the ratio
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{yes}}(R)/\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{no}}(R)
. Since the YES distribution
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{yes}}
is generated by the Markov kernel in
Definition

5.5
, the main task is to analyze this Markov kernel.

A Markov kernel from
\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\mathcal{V}\times[n]}
to
\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}
pushes forward a probability distribution from the former space to a distribution on the latter. At the same time, it also induces a pull-back operation, mapping functions defined on
\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}
to functions on
\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\mathcal{V}\times[n]}
. For the Markov kernel defined in
Definition

5.5
, it turns out that the pull-back perspective is the more convenient one for analysis. We denote this pull-back operator by the italic bold symbol
\boldsymbol{P}^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\mu}[\cdot]
, distinguishing it from the matrix expression
\mathbf{P}^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\mu}(\cdot,\cdot)
to reflect that, while formally distinct, the two represent the same underlying transformation.

Notation 7.1
.

Fix a
k
-universe
\mathcal{U}
, a nonnegative integer
m\leqslant|\mathcal{U}|
, and a one-wise independent distribution
\mu
over
\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}
. The (right) stochastic matrix
\mathbf{P}^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\mu}:\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\bigcup\mathcal{U}}\times\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}\to\mathbb{R}
, defined in
Definition

5.5
, can be viewed as a linear operator

\boldsymbol{P}^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\mu}:L^{2}(\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m})\to L^{2}\big(\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\bigcup\mathcal{U}}\big)

given by

\boldsymbol{P}^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\mu}[f](x)=\sum_{\mathbf{y}\in\Omega^{\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{e}},\alpha n}}\mathbf{P}^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\mu}(x,\mathbf{y})f(\mathbf{y}),

for all
x\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\bigcup\mathcal{U}}
and
f\in L^{2}(\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m})
.

The operator
\boldsymbol{P}_{\mu}^{\mathcal{U},m}
satisfies the following two basic properties.

Proposition 7.2
.

For any
f\in L^{2}(\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m})
, we always have
\left\|\boldsymbol{P}^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\mu}[f]\right\|_{\infty}\leqslant\|f\|_{\infty}
.

Proof.

This is obvious since the value of
\boldsymbol{P}^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\mu}[f]
at any input
x
is a convex combination of function values of
f
.
∎

Proposition 7.3
.

The operator
\boldsymbol{P}^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\mu}
maps a density function on
\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}
to a density function on
\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\bigcup\mathcal{U}}
.

Proof.

Since the matrix
\mathbf{P}^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\mu}(\cdot,\cdot)
has only nonnegative entries, the operator
\boldsymbol{P}^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\mu}
preserves nonnegativity. It suffices to check that
\boldsymbol{P}^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\mu}
also preserves expected values.

Now we need to revisit the definition of the matrix
\mathbf{P}^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\mu}(\cdot,\cdot)
in
Definition

5.5
. In the Markov process given in
Definition

5.5
, it is clear that if
x\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\bigcup\mathcal{U}}
is chosen uniformly at random, then the output
\mathbf{z}
of the process is also uniformly distributed in
\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}
. This means that for any
\mathbf{y}\in\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}
, the expected value
\underset{x\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\bigcup\mathcal{U}}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\boldsymbol{P}^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\mu}(x,\mathbf{y})\right]
is equal to
1/\left|\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}\right|
. Therefore, for any
f\in L^{2}(\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m})
, we have

\underset{x\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\bigcup\mathcal{U}}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\boldsymbol{P}^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\mu}[f](x)\right]=\sum_{\mathbf{y}\in\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}}\left(f(\mathbf{y})\underset{x\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\bigcup\mathcal{U}}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\boldsymbol{P}^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\mu}(x,\mathbf{y})\right]\right)=\underset{\mathbf{y}\in\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[f(\mathbf{y})\right],

and we conclude that the operator
\boldsymbol{P}^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\mu}
preserves expected values.
∎

Armed with the operator formalism, we are now ready to prove the main lemma of this subsection that relates the YES and NO distributions (
Lemma

7.5
). The following notation is useful for stating the lemma as well as later throughout this section.

Notation 7.4
.

Given a
k
-universe
\mathcal{U}
, a nonnegative integer
m\leqslant|\mathcal{U}|
, and a set
A\subseteq\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}
, the density function of the uniform distribution on
A
is denoted by
\phi_{A}:\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}\rightarrow[0,\infty)
, specifically defined as

\phi_{A}(\mathbf{y}):=\begin{cases}\left|\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}\right|/|A|,&\text{if }\mathbf{y}\in A,\\
0,&\text{if }\mathbf{y}\not\in A.\end{cases}

Lemma 7.5
.

Fix a
\mathsf{DIHP}(G,n,\alpha,K)
communication game, where
G=(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E},N,(\mu_{\mathsf{e}})_{\mathsf{e}\in\mathcal{E}})
. Given a rectangle
R=\prod_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}A^{(\mathsf{e},j)}
, where
A^{(\mathsf{e},j)}\subseteq\Omega^{\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{e}},\alpha n}
, we have

\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{yes}}(R)=\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{no}}(R)\cdot\underset{\widetilde{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\mathcal{V}\times[n]}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\prod_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}\boldsymbol{P}^{\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{e}},\alpha n}_{\mu_{\mathsf{e}}}\Big[\phi_{A^{(\mathsf{e},j)}}\Big]\circ\mathrm{proj}_{\mathsf{e}}(\widetilde{x})\right].

Proof.

The result follows from direct calculation:

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{yes}}(R)

\displaystyle=\underset{\widetilde{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\mathcal{V}\times[n]}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\prod_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}\left(\sum_{\mathbf{y}\in A^{(\mathsf{e},j)}}\mathbf{P}^{\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{e}},\alpha n}_{\mu_{\mathsf{e}}}\Big(\mathrm{proj}_{\mathsf{e}}(\widetilde{x}),\mathbf{y}\Big)\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\underset{\widetilde{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\mathcal{V}\times[n]}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\prod_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}\left(\sum_{\mathbf{y}\in\Omega^{\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{e}},\alpha n}}\mathbf{P}^{\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{e}},\alpha n}_{\mu_{\mathsf{e}}}\Big(\mathrm{proj}_{\mathsf{e}}(\widetilde{x}),\mathbf{y}\Big)\phi_{A^{(\mathsf{e},j)}}(\mathbf{y})\right)\right]\cdot\prod_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}\frac{\left|A^{(\mathsf{e},j)}\right|}{\left|\Omega^{\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{e}},\alpha n}\right|}

\displaystyle=\underset{\widetilde{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\mathcal{V}\times[n]}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\prod_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}\boldsymbol{P}_{\mu_{\mathsf{e}}}^{\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{e}},\alpha n}\Big[\phi_{A^{(\mathsf{e},j)}}\Big]\Big(\mathrm{proj}_{\mathsf{e}}(\widetilde{x})\Big)\right]\cdot\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{no}}(R).

The definitions of
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{yes}}
and
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{no}}
in
Definition

5.9
are used in the first and the third transitions above, respectively.
∎

7.2
Separating Structured and Pseudorandom Parts

Given a
\mathbf{z}
-global set
A\subseteq\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}
, the goal of this subsection is to express the function
\boldsymbol{P}_{\mu}^{\mathcal{U},m}[\phi_{A}]
as the product of two functions: the structured part and the pseudorandom part. Now, we first give a formal description of what the structured part looks like.

Definition 7.6
.

Fix a
k
-universe
\mathcal{U}
, a nonnegative integer
m
, and a one-wise independent distribution
\mu
over
\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}
. Let
\mathbf{z}
be a restriction on the space
\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}
. We define a density function
g_{\mathbf{z}}:\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\bigcup\mathcal{U}}\rightarrow[0,\infty)
by

g_{\mathbf{z}}(x):=\prod_{e\in\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z})}N^{k}\mu\Big(x_{|e}-\mathbf{z}(e)\Big).

Next, we define the pseudorandom part. For that purpose, we introduce the following two notations.

Notation 7.7
.

Fix a
k
-universe
\mathcal{U}
, a nonnegative integer
m\leqslant|\mathcal{U}|
, and a matching
M\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{U},\leqslant m}
. The canonical projection from
\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\bigcup\mathcal{U}}
to
\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\bigcup\mathcal{U}_{\setminus M}}
is denote by
\mathrm{proj}_{\setminus M}
, i.e., for every
x\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\bigcup\mathcal{U}}
, we define

\displaystyle\mathrm{proj}_{\setminus M}(x)=x_{|\bigcup\mathcal{U}_{\setminus M}}.

Notation 7.8
.

Suppose
\mathbf{z}
is a restriction on a labeled matching space
\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}
, and let
M:=\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z})
. For an element
\mathbf{y}\in\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\mathbf{z}}
, we define
\mathbf{y}_{\setminus M}
to be the restriction of the map
\mathbf{y}:\textstyle\prod\mathcal{U}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}\cup\{\textup{{nil}}\}
to the set
\textstyle\prod\mathcal{U}_{\setminus M}\subseteq\textstyle\prod\mathcal{U}
. Therefore,
\mathbf{y}_{\setminus M}
is an element of
\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\setminus M}
.

Suppose
\mathbf{z}
is a restriction on a labeled matching space
\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}
. Recall from
Remark

6.7
that a subset
A\subseteq\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\mathbf{z}}
is identified with a subset
A_{\mathrm{rem}}\subseteq\Omega^{\mathcal{U}_{M},\,m-|M|}=\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\setminus M}
, where
M:=\mathrm{supp}(z)
. Therefore, in addition to the density function
\phi_{A}
defined on
\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}
, we have another density function
\phi_{A_{\mathrm{rem}}}
defined on
\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\setminus M}
. The following lemma establishes a relation between the pull-backs of
\phi_{A}
and
\phi_{A_{\mathrm{rem}}}
under the Markov operators.

Lemma 7.9
.

Fix a
k
-universe
\mathcal{U}
, a nonnegative integer
m\leqslant|\mathcal{U}|
, and a one-wise independent distribution
\mu
over
\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}
. Let
\mathbf{z}
be a restriction on the space
\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}
with support
M:=\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z})
, and let
A\subseteq\Omega_{\mathbf{z}}^{\mathcal{U},m}
. Then for every
x\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\bigcup\mathcal{U}}
, we have

\boldsymbol{P}^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\mu}[\phi_{A}](x)=g_{\mathbf{z}}(x)\cdot\boldsymbol{P}_{\mu}^{\mathcal{U}_{\setminus M},\,m-|M|}\Big[\phi_{A_{\mathrm{rem}}}\Big]\Big(\mathrm{proj}_{\setminus M}(x)\Big).

(7.2)

Proof.

For elements
x\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\bigcup\mathcal{U}}
and
\mathbf{y}\in\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\mathbf{z}}
, consider the value of
\mathbf{P}^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\mu}(x,\mathbf{y})
, which is the probability of obtaining
\mathbf{y}
in the sampling process of
Definition

5.5
. Since
\mathbf{y}(e)=\mathbf{z}(e)\neq\textup{{nil}}
for all
e\in M
, the first requirement for producing
\mathbf{y}
is that the uniformly random matching sampled in Step 1 of
Definition

5.5
contains
M
. This occurs with probability

\frac{N^{k|M|}\cdot\left|\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\setminus M}\right|}{\left|\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}\right|}.

Conditioned on this event, the second requirement is that the labels on each
e\in M
sampled in Step 3 of
Definition

5.5
match
\mathbf{z}(e)
. This occurs with probability

\prod_{e\in M}\mu\big(x_{|e}-\mathbf{z}(e)\big).

Finally, conditioned on the first two requirements, the third requirement is that the remainder of the labeled matching coincides with
\mathbf{y}_{\setminus M}
. This occurs with probability

\mathbf{P}^{\mathcal{U}_{\setminus M},\,m-|M|}_{\mu}\big(\mathrm{proj}_{\setminus M}(x),\,\mathbf{y}_{\setminus M}\big).

Combining these factors, we have

\mathbf{P}^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\mu}(x,\mathbf{y})=\frac{N^{k|M|}\cdot\left|\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\setminus M}\right|}{\left|\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}\right|}\cdot\prod_{e\in M}\mu\big(x_{|e}-\mathbf{z}(e)\big)\cdot\mathbf{P}^{\mathcal{U}_{\setminus M},\,m-|M|}_{\mu}\big(\mathrm{proj}_{\setminus M}(x),\,\mathbf{y}_{\setminus M}\big).

It then follows from direct calculation that

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{P}^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\mu}[\phi_{A}](x)

\displaystyle=\frac{\left|\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}\right|}{|A|}\sum_{\mathbf{y}\in A}\mathbf{P}^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\mu}(x,\mathbf{y})

\displaystyle=\frac{N^{k|M|}\cdot\left|\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\setminus M}\right|}{|A|}\left(\sum_{\mathbf{y}\in A}\mathbf{P}^{\mathcal{U}_{\setminus M},\,m-|M|}_{\mu}\Big(\mathrm{proj}_{\setminus M}(x),\mathbf{y}_{\setminus M}\Big)\right)\prod_{e\in M}\mu(x_{|e}-\mathbf{z}(e))

\displaystyle=\left(\prod_{e\in M}N^{k}\mu(x_{|e}-\mathbf{z}(e))\right)\cdot\frac{\left|\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\setminus M}\right|}{|A|}\sum_{\mathbf{y}\in A_{\mathrm{rem}}}\mathbf{P}^{\mathcal{U}_{\setminus M},\,m-|M|}_{\mu}\Big(\mathrm{proj}_{\setminus M}(x),\mathbf{y}\Big)

\displaystyle=g_{\mathbf{z}}(x)\cdot\boldsymbol{P}_{\mu}^{\mathcal{U}_{\setminus M},\,m-|M|}\Big[\phi_{A_{\mathrm{rem}}}\Big]\Big(\mathrm{proj}_{\setminus M}(x)\Big).\qed

We remark that on the right hand side of (
7.2
), the first factor
g_{\mathbf{z}}(x)
is the structured part of the function
\boldsymbol{P}^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\mu}[\phi_{A}]
, while the second factor is the pseudorandom part.

7.3
Analyzing the Structured Part

As promised in the introductory text of
Section

7
, given a sequence of restrictions
\boldsymbol{\zeta}=\left(\mathbf{z}^{(\mathsf{e},j)}\right)_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}
, we need to analyze the “structured-only product”

\prod_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}g_{\mathbf{z}^{(\mathsf{e},j)}}\circ\mathrm{proj}_{\mathsf{e}}\,,

(7.3)

which is a nonnegative-valued function on
\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\mathcal{V}\times[n]}
. A natural question is whether the function is still a density function, i.e., whether its expected value is 1.

It turns out that this is not always the case, as suggested by the following simple counterexample. Suppose there are two distinct players
(\mathsf{e}_{1},j_{2}),(\mathsf{e}_{2},j_{2})
such that

\mathrm{supp}\left(\mathbf{z}^{(\mathsf{e}_{1},j_{1})}\right)\bigcap\mathrm{supp}\left(\mathbf{z}^{(\mathsf{e}_{2},j_{2})}\right)\neq\emptyset,

and
\mu_{\mathsf{e}_{1}}
and
\mu_{\mathsf{e}_{2}}
are two one-wise independent distributions with disjoint supports. Then, it is easy to see that the product of the two functions

\left(g_{\mathbf{z}^{(\mathsf{e}_{1},j_{1})}}\circ\mathrm{proj}_{\mathsf{e}_{1}}\right)\cdot\left(g_{\mathbf{z}^{(\mathsf{e}_{2},j_{2})}}\circ\mathrm{proj}_{\mathsf{e}_{2}}\right)

already collapses to 0, and hence the expected value of (
7.3
) is 0 in this case.

Counterexamples of this type can be fixed by imposing the requirement that the supports in the sequence
\left(\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z}^{(\mathsf{e},j)})\right)_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}
are pairwise disjoint and their union does not contain any cycle. These conditions enable us to make use of the one-wise independent nature of the distributions, and the expected value of (
7.3
) is indeed 1 under these conditions. The formal statement and proof follow.

Lemma 7.10
.

Fix a
\mathsf{DIHP}(G,n,\alpha,K)
communication game, where
G=(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E},N,(\mu_{\mathsf{e}})_{\mathsf{e}\in\mathcal{E}})
. Let
\boldsymbol{\zeta}=\left(\mathbf{z}^{(\mathsf{e},j)}\right)_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}
be a sequence of restrictions, where each restriction
\mathbf{z}^{(\mathsf{e},j)}
is on
\Omega^{\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{e}},\alpha n}
. If the hyperedge sets
\left\{\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z}^{(\mathsf{e},j)})\right\}_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}
are pairwise disjoint, and their union does not contain any cycle, then we have

\underset{\widetilde{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\mathcal{V}\times[n]}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\prod_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}g_{\mathbf{z}^{(\mathsf{e},j)}}\circ\mathrm{proj}_{\mathsf{e}}(\widetilde{x})\right]=1.

Proof.

Let

E=\bigcup_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}\mathrm{supp}\left(\mathbf{z}^{(\mathsf{e},j)}\right)

denote the set of all hyperedges appearing in the restrictions. We say that two distinct hyperedges in
E
are
incident
if they share a common vertex.

We first claim that any two incident hyperedges in
E
can share at most one vertex. Indeed, suppose two hyperedges share at least two vertices. Then, together, they cover at most
2(k-1)
vertices, which would violate the cycle-free assumption on
E
(see
Section

2.1
for the definition of cycle-freeness).

Next, we claim that there exists a total order
\prec
on
E
such that each hyperedge
e\in E
is incident to at most one hyperedge that precedes it under
\prec
. To see this, consider the
line graph

L(E)
defined as follows:

(1)

The vertex set of
L(E)
is
E
;

(2)

The edge set of
L(E)
consists of all pairs of incident hyperedges in
E
.

Suppose, for contradiction, that
L(E)
contains a cycle of length
\ell
. Then the corresponding
\ell
hyperedges in
E
collectively cover at most
\ell(k-1)
vertices, again violating the cycle-free assumption on
E
. Hence,
L(E)
must be acyclic and is therefore a forest. It follows that
E
admits a total ordering
\prec
such that each hyperedge is incident to at most one earlier hyperedge in the order.

Now, for each
e\in E
, let
\langle e\rangle
denote the original hyperedge
\mathsf{e}\in\mathcal{E}
such that
e\in\prod\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{e}}
. Then, we have the identity:

\prod_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}g_{\mathbf{z}^{(\mathsf{e},j)}}\circ\mathrm{proj}_{\mathsf{e}}(\widetilde{x})=\prod_{e\in E}N^{k}\mu_{\langle e\rangle}\Big(\widetilde{x}_{|e}-\mathbf{z}(e)\Big).

(7.4)

Using the total ordering
\prec
on
E
, we now analyze the expected value of the right-hand side of (
7.4
). For any
e\in E
, we have:

\displaystyle\quad\underset{\widetilde{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\mathcal{V}\times[n]}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\prod_{e^{\prime}\preceq e}N^{k}\mu_{\langle e^{\prime}\rangle}\Big(\widetilde{x}_{|e^{\prime}}-\mathbf{z}(e^{\prime})\Big)\right]

\displaystyle=\underset{\widetilde{x}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\prod_{e^{\prime}\prec e}N^{k}\mu_{\langle e^{\prime}\rangle}\Big(\widetilde{x}_{|e^{\prime}}-\mathbf{z}(e^{\prime})\Big)\cdot\underset{\widetilde{x}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[N^{k}\mu_{\langle e\rangle}\Big(\widetilde{x}_{|e}-\mathbf{z}(e)\Big)\,\middle|\,\left(\widetilde{x}_{|e^{\prime}}\right)_{e^{\prime}\prec e}\right]\right].

Note that by our choice of the acyclic ordering
\prec
, we know that conditioning on
(\widetilde{x}_{\mid e^{\prime}})_{e^{\prime}\prec e}
only fixes at most one coordinate of the coordinates in
e
. If no coordinate is fixed, it is easy to see that the inner conditional expectation evaluates to
1
. Otherwise, suppose the
i
-th vertex
v
of
e
is fixed to
b\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}
by the conditioning. In this case, the inner conditional expectation equals

\displaystyle\underset{\widetilde{x}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[N^{k}\mu_{\langle e\rangle}\Big(\widetilde{x}_{|e}-\mathbf{z}(e)\Big)\,\middle|\,\left(\widetilde{x}_{|e^{\prime}}\right)_{e^{\prime}\prec e}\right]=N\cdot\sum_{z\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k},\,z_{i}=b}\mu_{\langle e\rangle}\left(z-\mathbf{z}(e)\right)=N\cdot\frac{1}{N}=1.

due to the one-wise independence of
\mu_{\langle e\rangle}
.
Therefore, the overall expectation remains unchanged when we remove the term associated with
e
.

By applying this argument recursively — removing the maximal element under
\prec
at each step — we can eliminate all hyperedges in
E
without affecting the expectation. Consequently, the expected value of (
7.4
) is equal to 1, as claimed.
∎

7.4
Analyzing the Pseudorandom Part

For the pseudo-random part
\boldsymbol{P}_{\mu}^{\mathcal{U}_{\setminus M},\,m-|M|}\Big[\phi_{A_{\mathrm{rem}}}\Big]
in the decomposition (
7.2
), we will show that it has a good “Fourier-decay” property, defined as follows.

Definition 7.11
.

Suppose
\Lambda
is a ground set,
n
is a positive integer at most
|\Lambda|
, and
w
is a real number in the range
(0,|\Lambda|)
. We say a density function
f:\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\Lambda}\rightarrow[0,\infty)
is
(n,w)
-decaying if for every nonnegative integer
\ell\leqslant|\Lambda|
we have
\left\|f^{=\ell}\right\|_{2}^{2}\leqslant F(n,\ell,w)
, where
F(n,\ell,w)
is defined by

F(n,\ell,w)=\begin{cases}\left(\frac{w}{n}\right)^{\ell/2},&\text{if }0\leqslant\ell\leqslant w,\\
\left(\frac{\ell}{8n}\right)^{\ell/2}\cdot 2^{2w},&\text{if }w<\ell\leqslant n,\\
0,&\text{if }\ell>n.\end{cases}

In particular, it is not hard to see that a probability density function that is, say,
(n,n/2)
-decaying, must be close to uniform.

The following simple observation ensures that the Fourier weight bound
F(n,\ell,w)
is convenient to work with.

Proposition 7.12
.

For fixed
n
and
d
, the bound
F(n,d,w)
is monotone increasing in
w
.

Proof.

Note that the function
F(n,\ell,w)
is continuous and piecewise differentiable in
w
, in the range
w\in(0,\infty)
. The conclusion then follows by verifying that the partial derivative of
F(n,\ell,w)
in
w
is always positive.
∎

We now present the desired lemma that proves Fourier decay properties for pull-backs of density functions of the form
\phi_{A}
, where
A
is a global set.

Lemma 7.13
.

Fix a
k
-universe
\mathcal{U}
, an integer
m
and a real number
w>0
such that
|\mathcal{U}|\geqslant 10^{8}k^{3}m
and
m\geqslant 2(w+1)
. Let
A\subseteq\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}
be a global set with
|A|=2^{-w}\cdot\left|\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}\right|
. Then the density function
\boldsymbol{P}^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\mu}[\phi_{A}]
is
(|\mathcal{U}|,w)
-decaying, for any one-wise independent distribution
\mu
over
\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}
.

Proof.

The proof is deferred to
Section

8
.
∎

7.5
The Hybrid Method

In order to combine the structured-part result
Lemma

7.10
and the pseudorandom-part result
Lemma

7.13
, we need the following important lemma. The proof of this lemma somewhat resembles the hybrid arguments used in previous works such as
[
KKS14
,
CGSV24
]
to extract two-player communication games from multi-player ones.

Lemma 7.14
.

For any nonnegative integer
r
, there exists a constant
\delta=\delta(r)>0
such that the following holds. Suppose
n
is a sufficiently large integer and
\Lambda
is a ground set with
|\Lambda|\geqslant n
. For any density functions
h_{0},h_{1},\dots,h_{r}:\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\Lambda}\rightarrow[0,\infty)
such that

(1)

\|h_{i}\|_{\infty}\leqslant 2^{\delta n^{1/3}}
for all
i\in\{0,1,\dots,r\}
, and

(2)

h_{i}
is
\left(n/2,\delta n^{1/3}\right)
-decaying for all
i\in\{1,2,\dots,r\}
,

we have

\displaystyle\left|\underset{x\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\Lambda}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\prod_{i=0}^{r}h_{i}(x)\right]-1\right|\leqslant 0.001.

Proof.

We let
\delta(r):=10^{-6}N^{-1}(r+1)^{-2}
. Since
\mathbb{E}_{x\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\Lambda}}\left[h_{0}(x)\right]=1
, the statement clearly holds for
r=0
. We proceed by induction on
r
. In the following, assume
r\geqslant 1
and the result holds for all smaller values of
r
.
Writing

\underset{x\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\Lambda}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\prod_{i=0}^{r}h_{i}(x)\right]-1=\underset{x\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\Lambda}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\prod_{i=0}^{r}h_{i}(x)\right]-\underset{x\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\Lambda}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[h_{0}(x)\right]=\sum_{j=1}^{r}\underset{x\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\Lambda}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[(h_{j}(x)-1)\prod_{i=0}^{j-1}h_{i}(x)\right],

(7.5)

it suffices to upper bound the absolute value of each summand in the sum above.

Using the level decomposition, for each
j\in[r]
we have

\displaystyle\left|\underset{x\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\Lambda}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[(h_{j}(x)-1)\prod_{i=0}^{j-1}h_{i}(x)\right]\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\sum_{\ell=0}^{|\Lambda|}\left\langle\left(h_{j}-1\right)^{=\ell},\left(\prod_{i=0}^{j-1}h_{i}\right)^{=\ell}\right\rangle\right|

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{\ell=1}^{|\Lambda|}\left\lVert\left(h_{j}-1\right)^{=\ell}\right\rVert_{2}\cdot\left\lVert\left(\prod_{i=0}^{j-1}h_{i}\right)^{=\ell}\right\rVert_{2},

(7.6)

where we use the Cauchy-Schwarz and the fact that
(h_{j}-1)^{=0}\equiv 0
to deduce the inequality.

We next provide upper bounds on the level-
\ell
Fourier weights of the two functions
h_{j}-1
and
\prod_{i=0}^{j-1}h_{j}
separately. We know that
h_{j}
is
\left(n/2,\delta n^{1/3}\right)
-decaying. Plugging in
Definition

7.11
, we obtain

\left\|(h_{j}-1)^{=\ell}\right\|_{2}^{2}\leqslant\begin{cases}\left(2\delta n^{-2/3}\right)^{\ell/2},&\text{if }1\leqslant\ell\leqslant\delta n^{1/3},\\
\left(\ell/(4n)\right)^{\ell/2}\cdot 2^{2\delta n^{1/3}},&\text{if }\delta n^{1/3}<\ell\leqslant n,\\
0,&\text{if }\ell>n.\end{cases}

(7.7)

Since
\delta=\delta(r)\leqslant\delta(j-1)
and
n
is sufficiently large, we may use the induction hypothesis on
h_{0},\dots,h_{j-1}
and obtain

\displaystyle\left\lVert\prod_{i=0}^{j-1}h_{i}\right\rVert_{1}=1+\left(\underset{x\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\Lambda}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\prod_{i=0}^{j-1}h_{i}(x)\right]-1\right)\in\left[\frac{1}{2},\frac{3}{2}\right].

Furthermore, since
\|h_{i}\|_{\infty}\leqslant 2^{\delta n^{1/3}}
for all
i\in\{0,1,\dots,j-1\}
, the infinity norm (and hence the 2-norm) of
\prod_{i=0}^{j-1}h_{i}
is at most
2^{\delta rn^{1/3}}
. We may apply
Proposition

2.6
and get

\left\lVert\left(\prod_{i=0}^{j-1}h_{i}\right)^{=\ell}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\leqslant\left(\frac{3}{2}\right)^{2}\cdot\left(12N\cdot(\delta rn^{1/3}+2)\right)^{\ell}\leqslant\left(50N(\delta rn^{1/3}+2)\right)^{\ell}.

(7.8)

Plugging (
7.7
) and (
7.8
) into (
7.6
), we get

\displaystyle\quad\left|\underset{x\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\Lambda}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[(h_{i}(x)-1)\prod_{i=0}^{j-1}h_{i}(x)\right]\right|

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{\ell=1}^{\left\lfloor\delta n^{1/3}\right\rfloor}\left(2\delta n^{-2/3}\right)^{\ell/2}\cdot\left(50N(\delta rn^{1/3}+2)\right)^{\ell}+\sum_{\ell=\left\lfloor\delta n^{1/3}\right\rfloor+1}^{n}\left(\frac{\ell}{4n}\right)^{\ell/2}\cdot 2^{2\delta n^{1/3}}\cdot 2^{\delta rn^{1/3}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{\ell=1}^{\infty}\left(\frac{\delta}{3r}\right)^{\ell}+\sum_{\ell=\left\lfloor\delta n^{1/3}\right\rfloor+1}^{\left\lfloor 2^{-2r-4}n\right\rfloor}\left(\sqrt{\frac{\ell}{4n}}\cdot 2^{r+2}\right)^{\delta n^{1/3}}+\sum_{\ell=\left\lfloor 2^{-2r-4}n\right\rfloor+1}^{n}4^{-\ell/2}\cdot 2^{(r+2)\delta n^{1/3}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{\delta}{r}

for sufficiently large
n
. The conclusion then follows by (
7.5
).
∎

7.6
Proof of the Discrepancy Bound

Now, we have all the ingredients needed to prove Lemma
6.12
, restated below.
See
6.12

Proof.

Let
\boldsymbol{\zeta}=\left(\mathbf{z}^{(\mathsf{e},j)}\right)_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}
and let
R=\prod_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}A^{(\mathsf{e},j)}
. Let
h_{0}:\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\mathcal{V}\times[n]}\rightarrow[0,\infty)
be defined by

h_{0}(\widetilde{x}):=\prod_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}g_{\mathbf{z}^{(\mathsf{e},j)}}\circ\mathrm{proj}_{\mathsf{e}}(\widetilde{x}).

For each
(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]
, we have a function
h^{(\mathsf{e},j)}:\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\mathcal{V}\times[n]}\rightarrow[0,\infty)
defined by

h^{(\mathsf{e},j)}:=\boldsymbol{P}_{\mu_{\mathsf{e}}}^{(\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{e}})_{\setminus M},\,\alpha n-|M|}\left[\phi_{A_{\mathrm{rem}}^{(\mathsf{e},j)}}\right]\circ\mathrm{proj}_{\setminus M}\circ\mathrm{proj}_{\mathsf{e}},

where
M
stands for
\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z}^{(\mathsf{e},j)})
.

By
Lemmas

7.5
and
7.9
, we can now write

\displaystyle\frac{\left|\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{yes}}(R)-\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{no}}(R)\right|}{\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{no}}(R)}

\displaystyle=\left|\underset{\widetilde{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\mathcal{V}\times[n]}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\prod_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}\boldsymbol{P}^{\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{e}},\alpha n}_{\mu_{\mathsf{e}}}\Big[\phi_{A^{(\mathsf{e},j)}}\Big]\circ\mathrm{proj}_{\mathsf{e}}(\widetilde{x})\right]-1\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\underset{\widetilde{x}\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\mathcal{V}\times[n]}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[h_{0}(\widetilde{x})\cdot\prod_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}h^{(\mathsf{e},j)}(\widetilde{x})\right]-1\right|.

(7.9)

It suffices to upper bound the right hand side above.

Note that since the infinity norm of each
g_{\mathbf{z}^{(\mathsf{e},j)}}
is clearly at most
N^{k\left|\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z}^{\mathsf{e},j})\right|}
, and using the goodness assumption
\sum_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}\left|\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z}^{\mathsf{e},j})\right|\leqslant\gamma n^{1/3}
, we have

\left\|h_{0}\right\|_{\infty}\leqslant\prod_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}\left\|g_{\mathbf{z}^{(\mathsf{e},j)}}\right\|_{\infty}\leqslant N^{k\cdot\gamma n^{1/3}}=2^{(k\log N)\gamma n^{1/3}}.

(7.10)

The goodness assumption also implies

\displaystyle\left|A_{\mathrm{rem}}^{(\mathsf{e},j)}\right|/\left|\Omega_{\mathbf{z}^{(\mathsf{e},j)}}^{\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{e}},\alpha n}\right|\geqslant 2^{-\gamma n^{1/3}}\quad\text{and hence}\quad\left\lVert\phi_{A_{\mathrm{rem}}^{(\mathsf{e},j)}}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leqslant 2^{\gamma n^{1/3}}.

It then follows from
Proposition

7.2
that

\|h^{(\mathsf{e},j)}\|_{\infty}\leqslant 2^{\gamma n^{1/3}}\quad\text{for each}\quad(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K].

(7.11)

Finally, since we always have

\left|(\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{e}})_{\setminus\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z}^{(\mathsf{e},j)})}\right|=n-\left|\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z}^{(\mathsf{e},j)})\right|\geqslant 10^{8}k^{3}\left(\alpha n-\left|\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z}^{(\mathsf{e},j)})\right|\right),

we may apply
Lemma

7.13
to
A^{(\mathsf{e},j)}_{\mathrm{rem}}
and obtain that the function

p^{(e,j)}:=\boldsymbol{P}_{\mu_{\mathsf{e}}}^{\mathcal{U}_{\setminus M},\,m-|M|}\left[\phi_{A_{\mathrm{rem}}^{(\mathsf{e},j)}}\right],\quad\text{ where }M=\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z}^{(\mathsf{e},j)})

is
\left(\left|(\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{e}})_{\setminus\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z}^{(\mathsf{e},j)})}\right|,\gamma n^{1/3}\right)
-decaying for all
(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]
. Note that

\left|(\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{e}})_{\setminus\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z}^{(\mathsf{e},j)})}\right|\geqslant n-\alpha n\geqslant n/2,

which implies that
p^{(\mathsf{e},j)}
is
\left(n/2,\gamma n^{1/3}\right)
-decaying by
Proposition

7.12
. Since
h^{(\mathsf{e},j)}
has the same Fourier spectrum as
p^{(\mathsf{e},j)}
in the sense that

\displaystyle\widehat{h^{(\mathsf{e},j)}}(b)=\begin{cases}0,&\text{if }\mathrm{supp}(b)\nsubseteq(\bigcup\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{e}})_{\setminus\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z}^{(\mathsf{e},j)})},\\
\widehat{p^{(\mathsf{e},j)}}\left(\mathrm{proj}_{\setminus M}\circ\mathrm{proj}_{\mathsf{e}}(b)\right),&\text{if }\mathrm{supp}(b)\subseteq(\bigcup\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{e}})_{\setminus\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z}^{(\mathsf{e},j)})}\end{cases}

for any
b\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\mathcal{V}\times[n]}
, we also have that

h^{(\mathsf{e},j)}\quad\text{is}\quad\left(n/2,\gamma n^{1/3}\right)\text{-decaying, for }(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K].

(7.12)

Due to the established properties (
7.10
), (
7.11
) and (
7.12
), we may now apply
Lemma

7.14
to
h_{0}
and
(h^{(\mathsf{e},j)})_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}
. It follows that as long as
\gamma
is chosen to be less than

\frac{1}{k\log N}\cdot\delta(K)=\frac{1}{k\log N}\cdot\frac{1}{10^{6}N(K+1)^{2}},

and
n
is sufficiently large, the right hand side of (
7.9
) is upper-bounded by
0.001
, as desired.
∎

8
Fourier Decay from Global Hypercontractivity

The goal of this section is to prove
Lemma

7.13
. The high-level strategy follows the approach of
[
FMW25
, Section 4]
. The first step is to establish a global hypercontractivity result for functions on
\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}
, formulated as a projected level-
d
inequality. The second step is to apply this inequality to the density function
\phi_{A}:\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}\to[0,\infty)
, where
A\subseteq\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}
, and to show how the resulting bound yields the desired Fourier decay for the function
\boldsymbol{P}^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\mu}[\phi_{A}]
.

Since the proof of the projected level-
d
inequality
10
10

10

We are aware of an alternative, shorter proof of the projected level-
d
inequality (yielding slightly weaker parameters) that avoids the machinery of
Appendix

B
. We nevertheless include
Appendix

B
, as it may provide additional conceptual insight.
closely follows that of
[
FMW25
, Section 4]
, we defer the details to
Appendix

B
. Nonetheless, in order to formalize the inequality, we must first introduce several preliminary definitions. This preparatory material occupies
Sections

8.1
,
8.2
and
8.3
. Then, in
Section

8.4
, we study structural properties of the operator
\boldsymbol{P}^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\mu}
that enable the projected level-
d
inequality for
\phi_{A}
to imply Fourier decay of
\boldsymbol{P}^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\mu}[\phi_{A}]
. We conclude with a proof of
Lemma

7.13
in
Section

8.5
.

8.1
Fourier Characters

We begin by introducing a collection of character functions on
\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}
. The characters are indexed by pairs
(M,\mathbf{a})
where
M\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{U},\leqslant m}
is a “partial matching” and
\mathbf{a}:M\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}\setminus\{0\}
is a labeling on the edges in
M
. To facilitate the definition of the character functions, we first introduce the following probability values.

Definition 8.1
.

For integers
n,m
such that
n\geqslant m\geqslant 0
, we define
\Psi(n,m,0):=1
, and for
1\leqslant d\leqslant m
we define inductively
\Psi(n,m,d):=mn^{-k}\cdot\Psi(n-1,m-1,d-1)
.

It is easy to see that
\Psi(n,m,d)
is equal to the probability that a fixed partial matching
M\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{U},d}
, where
\mathcal{U}
is a
k
-universe of cardinality
n
, is contained in a uniformly random matching drawn from
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{U},m}
. We are now ready to define the character functions:

Definition 8.2
.

For a matching
M\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{U},\leqslant m}
and a map
\mathbf{a}:M\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}\setminus\{0\}
, we call
(M,\mathbf{a})
a character index on
\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}
and define the character function
\psi_{M,\mathbf{a}}:\Omega\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
by

\psi_{M,\mathbf{a}}(\mathbf{y}):=\Psi(|\mathcal{U}|,m,|M|)^{-1/2}\cdot\prod_{e\in M}\chi_{\mathbf{a}(e)}(\mathbf{y}(e)).

Specially, we define
\chi_{\mathbf{a}(e)}(\mathbf{y}(e))=0
when
\mathbf{y}(e)=\textup{{nil}}
(see
Section

2.1
for how
\chi_{\mathbf{a}(e)}(\cdot)
is defined on standard inputs).

Note that the character functions defined above do not form a complete basis for
L^{2}(\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m})
. Nevertheless, they will be sufficient for our purpose. The following proposition shows that these characters form an orthonormal set.

Proposition 8.3
.

For character indices
(M_{1},\mathbf{a}_{1})
and
(M_{2},\mathbf{a}_{2})
on
\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}
, we have
\left\langle\psi_{M_{1},\mathbf{a}_{1}},\psi_{M_{2},\mathbf{a}_{2}}\right\rangle=\mathbbm{1}\{M_{1}=M_{2}\text{ and }\mathbf{a}_{1}=\mathbf{a}_{2}\}
.

Proof.

We divide the argument into cases.

Case 1:

M_{1}\cup M_{2}
is not a matching. Then for each
\mathbf{y}\in\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}
, there exists an hyperedge
e\in M_{1}\cup M_{2}
with
\mathbf{y}(e)=\textup{{nil}}
. This forces

\psi_{M_{1},\mathbf{a}_{1}}(\mathbf{y})\cdot\overline{\psi_{M_{2},\mathbf{a}_{2}}(\mathbf{y})}=0

for each
\mathbf{y}\in\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}
, and thus
\left\langle\psi_{M_{1},\mathbf{a}_{1}},\psi_{M_{2},\mathbf{a}_{2}}\right\rangle=0
.

Case 2:

M_{1}\cup M_{2}
is a matching. For each
e\in M_{1}\cup M_{2}
, let

\mathbf{a}(e):=\begin{cases}\mathbf{a}_{1}(e),&\text{if }e\in M_{1}\setminus M_{2},\\
\mathbf{a}_{1}(e)-\mathbf{a}_{2}(e),&\text{if }e\in M_{1}\cap M_{2},\\
-\mathbf{a}_{2}(e),&\text{if }e\in M_{2}\setminus M_{1}.\end{cases}

By
Definition

8.2
we have

\left\langle\psi_{M_{1},\mathbf{a}_{1}},\psi_{M_{2},\mathbf{a}_{2}}\right\rangle=\Psi(|\mathcal{U}|,m,|M_{1}|)^{-1/2}\cdot\Psi(|\mathcal{U}|,m,|M_{2}|)^{-1/2}\cdot\underset{\mathbf{y}\in\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\prod_{e\in M_{1}\cup M_{2}}\chi_{\mathbf{a}(e)}(\mathbf{y}(e))\right].

(8.1)

For a uniformly random
\mathbf{y}\in\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}
conditioned on
\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{y})\supseteq M_{1}\cup M_{2}
, the labels
\{\mathbf{y}(e)\}_{e\in M_{1}\cup M_{2}}
are independent and each uniformly distributed on
\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}
. Therefore, (
8.1
) implies that
\left\langle\psi_{M_{1},\mathbf{a}_{1}},\psi_{M_{2},\mathbf{a}_{2}}\right\rangle=0
unless
\mathbf{a}(e)=0
for all
e\in M_{1}\cup M_{2}
. Since
\mathbf{a}_{1}(e)\neq 0
for all
e\in M_{1}
and
\mathbf{a}_{2}(e)\neq 0
for all
e\in M_{2}
by the definition of characters, it follows that
\left\langle\psi_{M_{1},\mathbf{a}_{1}},\psi_{M_{2},\mathbf{a}_{2}}\right\rangle=0
unless
M_{1}=M_{2}
and
\mathbf{a}_{1}=\mathbf{a}_{2}
, in which case the right hand side of (
8.1
) clearly evaluates to 1.
∎

8.2
Discrete Derivatives

In this subsection, we introduce the notion of discrete derivatives for functions over
\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}
, as well as a related notion of globalness. These concepts are necessary for the statement of the projected level-
d
inequality.

Among all elements of the space
\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}
, we sometimes need to consider those labeled matchings that contain a fixed partial matching
S\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{U},\leqslant m}
. Such labeled matchings are determined by two choices:

1.

Assigning labels to the edges in
S
, which corresponds to selecting an element from
\mathrm{Map}\left(S,\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}\right)
.

2.

Choosing the remaining labeled matching on
\mathcal{U}_{\setminus S}
, which corresponds to an element in the space
\Omega^{\mathcal{U}_{\setminus S},\,m-|S|}
.

This leads to the following definition:

Definition 8.4
.

For a matching
S\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{U},\leqslant m}
, there is a canonical embedding

\mathfrak{i}:\Omega^{\mathcal{U}_{\setminus S},\,m-|S|}\times\mathrm{Map}\left(S,\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}\right)\hookrightarrow\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}.

This embedding proceeds by mapping a pair
(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{z})
from the left hand side to the labeled matching
\boldsymbol{\xi}\in\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}
defined by

1.

\boldsymbol{\xi}(e)=\mathbf{y}(e)
for
e\in\textstyle\prod(\mathcal{U}_{\setminus S})
,

2.

\boldsymbol{\xi}(e)=\mathbf{z}(e)
for
e\in S
, and

3.

\boldsymbol{\xi}(e)=\textup{{nil}}
for all other
e\in\textstyle\prod\mathcal{U}
.

The following definition provides the key gadget for defining discrete derivatives on
\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}
.

Definition 8.5
.

Let
S
be a finite set. For any map
\mathbf{z}\in\mathrm{Map}\left(S,\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}\right)
, we define its Hamming weight
\|\mathbf{z}\|_{\mathsf{H}}
to be the number of edges
e\in S
such that
\mathbf{z}(e)
is a nonzero element of the Abelian group
\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}
. We define a function
H_{S}:\mathrm{Map}\left(S,\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}\right)\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
by letting

H_{S}(\mathbf{z}):=(-1)^{\|\mathbf{z}\|_{\mathsf{H}}}\left(N^{k}-1\right)^{|S|-\|\mathbf{z}\|_{\mathsf{H}}}.

We are now ready to define the discrete derivative operators.

Definition 8.6
.

Consider a function
f:\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
. For a matching
S\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{U},\leqslant m}
and a label
\mathbf{x}\in\mathrm{Map}\left(S,\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}\right)
, we define a function
D_{S,\mathbf{x}}[f]:\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\setminus S}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
by

D_{S,\mathbf{x}}[f](\mathbf{y}):=\underset{\mathbf{z}:S\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[H_{S}(\mathbf{z})\cdot f\Big(\mathfrak{i}(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{z})\Big)\right],

where the embedding
\mathfrak{i}:\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\setminus S}\times\mathrm{Map}\left(S,\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}\right)\rightarrow\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}
is as in
Definition

8.4
.

The discrete derivative operators provide a means of measuring the “globalness” of a
function
on
\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}
, beyond the previous globalness notion (
Definition

6.6
) which is defined only for
subsets
of
\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}
. The following definition of derivative-based globalness parallels
[
KLM23
, Definition 4.4]
and
[
FMW25
, Definition 4.8]
.

Definition 8.7
.

Let
r,\lambda>0
and
1\leqslant p<\infty
. For a function
f:\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
, we say it is
(r,\lambda,d)
-
L^{p}
-global if for every matching
S\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{U},\leqslant d}
and label
\mathbf{x}\in\mathrm{Map}\left(S,\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}\right)
, we have
\left\|D_{S,\mathbf{x}}f\right\|_{p}\leqslant r^{|S|}\lambda
.

The following proposition (similar to
[
KLM23
, Lemma 4.9]
and
[
FMW25
, Proposition 4.10]
) shows that the two notions of globalness are closely related: the globalness of a subset in the sense of
Definition

6.6
implies the derivative-based globalness of the indication function of the subset as in
Definition

8.7
.

Proposition 8.8
.

Suppose a subset
A\subseteq\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}
is a global set (in the sense of
Definition

6.6
). Let
1_{A}:\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}\rightarrow\{0,1\}
be the indicator function of
A
. Then for every
1\leqslant p<\infty
, the function
1_{A}
is
(4,\|1_{A}\|_{p},m)
-
L^{p}
-global.

Proof.

Consider an arbitrary matching
S\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{U},\leqslant m}
, and let
\mathfrak{i}:\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\setminus S}\times\mathrm{Map}\left(S,\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}\right)\hookrightarrow\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}
be the embedding defined in
Definition

8.4
. For any fixed
\mathbf{z}\in\mathrm{Map}\left(S,\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}\right)
, by
Definition

6.6
, the function
1_{A}(\mathfrak{i}(\cdot,\mathbf{z})):\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\setminus S}\rightarrow\{0,1\}
is the indicator function of a set of size at most
2^{|S|}\cdot|A|\cdot|\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\setminus S}|/|\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}|
. As
1_{A}
is Boolean-valued, we get

\left\|1_{A}(\mathfrak{i}(\cdot,\mathbf{z}))\right\|_{p}^{p}\leqslant 2^{|S|}\cdot\left\|1_{A}\right\|_{p}^{p}
. Therefore, for any
\mathbf{x}\in\mathrm{Map}\left(S,\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}\right)
, we have (using the Minkowski inequality)

\displaystyle\left\|D_{S,\mathbf{x}}[1_{A}]\right\|_{p}

\displaystyle=\left\|\underset{\mathbf{z}:S\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[H_{S}(\mathbf{z})\cdot 1_{A}\Big(\mathfrak{i}(\cdot,\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{z})\Big)\right]\right\|_{p}\leqslant\underset{\mathbf{z}:S\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left|H_{S}(\mathbf{z})\right|\cdot\left\|1_{A}\Big(\mathfrak{i}(\cdot,\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{z})\Big)\right\|_{p}\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant\underset{\mathbf{z}:S\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[|H_{S}(\mathbf{z})|\right]\cdot 2^{|S|/p}\cdot\|1_{A}\|_{p}\leqslant 4^{|S|}\cdot\|1_{A}\|_{p},

where we used the simple calculation

\underset{\mathbf{z}:S\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[|H_{S}(\mathbf{z})|\right]=\left(\frac{1}{N^{k}}\cdot(N^{k}-1)+\frac{N^{k}-1}{N^{k}}\cdot 1\right)^{|S|}\leqslant 2^{|S|}.\qed

8.3
Level-
d
Projection

As suggested in its name, the “projected level-
d
inequality” studies the projection of a function onto the linear subspace spanned by a collection of level-
d
character functions. This is formalized in the following two definitions.

Definition 8.9
.

For a nonnegative integer
d
, we denote by
\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{U},d}
the collection of pairs
(M,\mathbf{a})
where
M\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{U},d}
and
\mathbf{a}\in\mathrm{Map}\left(M,\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}\setminus\{0\}\right)
. We also write
\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{U},\leqslant m}:=\bigcup_{0\leqslant d\leqslant m}\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{U},d}
.

Definition 8.10
.

Define the operator
P_{\mathfrak{X}}^{=d}:L^{2}(\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m})\rightarrow L^{2}(\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m})
to be the orthogonal projection onto the linear subspace of the Hilbert space
L^{2}(\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m})
spanned by the characters
\psi_{M,\mathbf{a}}
, where
(M,\mathbf{a})
ranges in
\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{U},d}
.

Using the fact that
\{\psi_{M,\mathbf{a}}:M\in\mathcal{M}_{U,d}\text{ and }\mathbf{a}\in\mathrm{Map}\left(M,\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}\setminus\{0\}\right)\}
forms an orthonormal set (see
Proposition

8.3
), we have the following direct formula for projections.

Proposition 8.11
.

Given an integer
d\geqslant 0
, for each function
f:\Omega^{U,m}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
we have

P_{\mathfrak{X}}^{=d}[f](\mathbf{y}):=\sum_{(M,\mathbf{a})\in\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{U},d}}\langle f,\psi_{M,\mathbf{a}}\rangle\cdot\psi_{M,\mathbf{a}}(\mathbf{y}).

Proof.

Follows immediately from
Proposition

8.3
.
∎

We are now ready to state the projected level-
d
inequality, which is proved in
Appendix

B
:

Theorem 8.12

(Projected level-
d
inequality)
.

Fix integers
d,m
such that
|\mathcal{U}|\geqslant 2km
and
m\geqslant 2(d+1)
. Suppose
f:\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
is both
(r,\lambda_{1},d)
-
L^{1}
-global and
(r,\lambda_{2},d)
-
L^{2}
-global, where
d\leqslant\log(\lambda_{2}/\lambda_{1})
and
r\geqslant 1
. Then

\left\|P_{\mathfrak{X}}^{=d}f\right\|_{2}^{2}\leqslant\lambda_{1}^{2}\left(\frac{10^{5}r^{2}\log(\lambda_{2}/\lambda_{1})}{d}\right)^{d}.

(8.2)

We note that since the bound provided by
Theorem

8.12
grows (exponentially) with the level
d
, a projection onto the span of character functions across multiple levels can be roughly bounded by the level-
d
bound corresponding to the highest level involved. We formalize this observation in the following corollary.

Corollary 8.13
.

Under the same conditions as
Theorem

8.12
, for any real number
\ell\in[1,\log(\lambda_{2}/\lambda_{1})]
we have

\sum_{d=1}^{\lfloor\ell\rfloor}\left\|P^{=d}_{\mathfrak{X}}[f]\right\|_{2}^{2}\leqslant\ell\cdot\lambda_{1}^{2}\left(\frac{10^{5}r^{2}\log(\lambda_{2}/\lambda_{1})}{\ell}\right)^{\ell}.

Proof.

It suffices to observe that the expression on the right hand side of (
8.2
) is monotone increasing in
d
in the range
1\leqslant d\leqslant\log(\lambda_{2}/\lambda_{1})
, even when
d
takes non-integral values.
∎

8.4
Singular Value Decomposition

Having formalized the projected level-
d
inequality on
\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}
, we now proceed to the second step of this section: analyzing the operator
\boldsymbol{P}^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\mu}
. A key property of this operator is that it admits a clean singular value decomposition: it maps character functions on
\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}
to scalar multiples of character functions on
\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\bigcup\mathcal{U}}
. We remark that this map is not 1-to-1, as distinct characters on
\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}
may be mapped to the same character on
\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\bigcup\mathcal{U}}
.

Given a character index
b\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\bigcup\mathcal{U}}
, the following definition identifies all characters on
\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}
that are mapped by
\boldsymbol{P}^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\mu}
to nonzero scalar multiples of
\chi_{b}
.

Definition 8.14
.

Given a character index
b\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\bigcup\mathcal{U}}
, define
\mathcal{X}^{\circ}(b)
to be the collection of character indices
(M,\mathbf{a})\in\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{U},m}
satisfying:

(1)

For every
e\in M
, we have
b_{|e}=\mathbf{a}(e)
;

(2)

For every
e\in M
, the vector
b_{|e}\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}
is nonzero on at least two coordinates;

(3)

If a vertex
v\in\textstyle\bigcup\mathcal{U}
does not appear in any edge of
M
, then
b_{v}=0
.

If condition (2) is removed, the resulting (larger) collection is denoted
\mathcal{X}(b)
, omitting the superscript
\circ
. Note that
\mathcal{X}(b)
is empty if
|\mathrm{supp}(b)|>km
.

The condition (2) in
Definition

8.14
is especially important since it captures the property that not too many characters on
\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}
are associated with a same character on
\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\mathcal{V}\times[n]}
under
\boldsymbol{P}^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\mu}
, as shown by the following lemma.

Lemma 8.15

(
[
CGSV24
, Lemma 6.9]
)
.

For any character index
b\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\mathcal{U}}
with
|\mathrm{supp}(b)|=d
, if
|\mathcal{U}|>100km
then

\sum_{(M,\mathbf{a})\in\mathcal{X}^{\circ}(b)}\Psi(|\mathcal{U}|,m,|M|)\leqslant\left(\frac{100k^{3}md}{|\mathcal{U}|^{2}}\right)^{d/2}.

We are now ready to present the singular value decomposition lemma. In particular, in the proof of the lemma, we will show why the one-wise independence of
\mu
corresponds to the condition (2) in
Definition

8.14
.

Lemma 8.16

(Singular value decomposition)
.

Fix a character index
b\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\bigcup\mathcal{U}}
and a one-wise independent distribution
\mu
over
\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}
. There exist complex numbers
R(M,\mathbf{a})
, each with absolute value at most 1, for all character indices
(M,\mathbf{a})\in\mathcal{X}^{\circ}(b)
, such that

\Big\langle\boldsymbol{P}^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\mu}[f],\chi_{b}\Big\rangle_{L^{2}\big(\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\bigcup\mathcal{U}}\big)}=\sum_{(M,\mathbf{a})\in\mathcal{X}^{\circ}(b)}R(M,\mathbf{a})\cdot\left\langle f,\sqrt{\Psi(|\mathcal{U}|,m,|M|)}\psi_{M,\mathbf{a}}\right\rangle

holds for any function
f\in L^{2}(\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m})
.

Proof.

We regard the distribution
\mu
as a function
\mu:\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}
with
\sum_{z\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}}\mu(z)=1
. For each character index
t\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}
, define

r(t):=\sum_{z\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}}\mu(z)\,\overline{\chi_{t}(z)}=N^{k}\cdot\widehat{\mu}(t).

Since
\mu
is assumed to be one-wise independent (see
Definition

5.6
), we know that
\widehat{\mu}(t)=0
for any
t\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}
with exactly one nonzero coordinate. Thus,
r(t)=0
for such
t
. Additionally, we have
r(0)=1
and
|r(t)|\leqslant 1
for all
t
since
|r(t)|\leqslant\mathbb{E}_{z\sim\mu}\,\left|\overline{\chi_{t}(z)}\right|=1
.

From
Definition

5.5
, we can express

\boldsymbol{P}^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\mu}(x,\mathbf{y})=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{U},m}|}\prod_{e\in\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{y})}\mu(x_{|e}-\mathbf{y}(e)).

Hence, we compute:

\displaystyle\Big\langle\boldsymbol{P}^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\mu}[f],\chi_{b}\Big\rangle_{L^{2}\big(\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\bigcup\mathcal{U}}\big)}

\displaystyle=\underset{x\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\bigcup\mathcal{U}}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\sum_{\mathbf{y}\in\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}}\boldsymbol{P}^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\mu}(x,\mathbf{y})f(\mathbf{y})\overline{\chi_{b}(x)}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{U},m}|}\sum_{\mathbf{y}}\left(f(\mathbf{y})\prod_{e\in\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{y})}\underset{z\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\mu(z-\mathbf{y}(e))\,\overline{\chi_{b_{|e}}(z)}\right]\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{U},m}|}\sum_{\mathbf{y}}\left(f(\mathbf{y})\prod_{e\in\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{y})}\widehat{\mu}(b_{|e})\,\overline{\chi_{b_{|e}}(\mathbf{y}(e))}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{U},m}|\cdot(N^{k})^{\alpha n}}\sum_{\mathbf{y}}\left(f(\mathbf{y})\prod_{e\in\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{y})}r(b_{|e})\,\overline{\chi_{b_{|e}}(\mathbf{y}(e))}\right)

\displaystyle=\underset{\mathbf{y}\in\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[f(\mathbf{y})\prod_{e\in\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{y})}r(b_{|e})\,\overline{\chi_{b_{|e}}(\mathbf{y}(e))}\right].

(8.3)

Now consider a fixed
\mathbf{y}\in\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}
. As
(M,\mathbf{a})
ranges over
\mathcal{X}(b)
, at most one of the character indices
\psi_{M,\mathbf{a}}
is nonzero at
\mathbf{y}
— namely, the unique pair
(M,\mathbf{a})
with
M\subseteq\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{y})
. Therefore, we have:

\sum_{(M,\mathbf{a})\in\mathcal{X}(b)}R(M,\mathbf{a})\sqrt{\Psi(|\mathcal{U}|,m,|M|)}\cdot\overline{\psi_{M,\mathbf{a}}(\mathbf{y})}=\prod_{e\in\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{y})}r(b_{|e})\,\overline{\chi_{b_{|e}}(\mathbf{y}(e))},

(8.4)

where
R(M,\mathbf{a}):=\prod_{e\in M}r(\mathbf{a}(e))
. Since each
r(\mathbf{a}(e))
has absolute value at most 1, so does
R(M,\mathbf{a})
.

Comparing (
8.3
) and (
8.4
), we conclude:

\Big\langle\boldsymbol{P}^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\mu}[f],\chi_{b}\Big\rangle_{L^{2}\big(\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\bigcup\mathcal{U}}\big)}=\sum_{(M,\mathbf{a})\in\mathcal{X}(b)}R(M,\mathbf{a})\cdot\left\langle f,\sqrt{\Psi(|\mathcal{U}|,m,|M|)}\psi_{M,\mathbf{a}}\right\rangle.

Finally, observe that any
(M,\mathbf{a})\in\mathcal{X}(b)
with
R(M,\mathbf{a})\neq 0
must satisfy condition (2) of
Definition

8.14
, and hence lies in
\mathcal{X}^{\circ}(b)
, as desired.
∎

The following corollary summarizes what we have revealed about the singular value decomposition of the operator
\boldsymbol{P}^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\mu}
.

Corollary 8.17
.

Fix a one-wise independent distribution
\mu
over
\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}
. For any character index
b\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\bigcup\mathcal{U}}
, the adjoint operator
\left(\boldsymbol{P}^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\mu}\right)^{\dagger}:L^{2}\big(\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\bigcup\mathcal{U}}\big)\rightarrow L^{2}(\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m})
maps the character function
\chi_{b}\in L^{2}\big(\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\bigcup\mathcal{U}}\big)
to a function
\widetilde{\chi}_{b}\in L^{2}(\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m})
satisfying the following:

(1)

If
|\mathrm{supp}(b)|=\ell
, then
\|\widetilde{\chi}_{b}\|_{2}^{2}\leqslant\left(100k^{3}m\ell\,|\mathcal{U}|^{-2}\right)^{\ell/2}
.

(2)

If
|\mathrm{supp}(b)|=\ell
, then
\widetilde{\chi}_{b}
lies in the linear subspace of
L^{2}(\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m})
spanned by the character functions
\psi_{M,\mathbf{a}}
for
(M,\mathbf{a})\in\bigcup_{d=1}^{\lfloor\ell/2\rfloor}\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{U},d}
.

(3)

For distinct indices
b,b^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\bigcup\mathcal{U}}
, the functions
\widetilde{\chi}_{b}
and
\widetilde{\chi}_{b^{\prime}}
are orthogonal.

Proof.

For
(M,\mathbf{a})\in\mathcal{X}^{\circ}(b)
, let
R(M,\mathbf{a})
be the complex numbers from
Lemma

8.16
. The conclusion of
Lemma

8.16
implies that

\left(\boldsymbol{P}^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\mu}\right)^{\dagger}[\chi_{b}]=\sum_{(M,\mathbf{a})\in\mathcal{X}^{\circ}(b)}\overline{R(M,\mathbf{a})}\cdot\sqrt{\Psi(|\mathcal{U}|,m,|M|)}\cdot\psi_{M,\mathbf{a}}.

(8.5)

The three statements in the corollary can then be easily deduced.

For statement (1): since each
R(M,\mathbf{a})
has absolute value at most 1, it follows that

\left\|\left(\boldsymbol{P}^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\mu}\right)^{\dagger}[\chi_{b}]\right\|_{2}^{2}\leqslant\sum_{(M,\mathbf{a})\in\mathcal{X}^{\circ}}\Psi(|\mathcal{U}|,m,|M|)\leqslant\left(\frac{100k^{3}m\ell}{|\mathcal{U}|^{2}}\right)^{\ell/2},

due to
Lemma

8.15
.

For statement (2): condition (2) of
Definition

8.14
, for any
(M,\mathbf{a})\in\mathcal{X}^{\circ}(b)
, we have
|M|\leqslant|\mathrm{supp}(b)|/2=\ell/2
, and hence the statement follows from (
8.5
).

For statement (3): conditions (1) and (3) of
Definition

8.14
ensures that
\mathcal{X}(b)
and
\mathcal{X}(b^{\prime})
are disjoint. The orthogonality then follows from (
8.5
) and
Proposition

8.3
.
∎

8.5
Proof of
Lemma

7.13

We now prove
Lemma

7.13
, restated below.
See
7.13

Proof.

For any character index
b\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\bigcup\mathcal{U}}
, we define the function
\widetilde{\chi}_{b}=\left(\boldsymbol{P}^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\mu}\right)^{\dagger}[\chi_{b}]
as in
Corollary

8.17
. For any positive integer
\ell
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|\boldsymbol{P}^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\mu}[\varphi_{A}]^{=\ell}\right\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}b\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\bigcup\mathcal{U}}\\
|\mathrm{supp}(b)|=\ell\end{subarray}}\left|\left\langle\boldsymbol{P}^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\mu}[\varphi_{A}],\chi_{b}\right\rangle\right|^{2}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}b\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\bigcup\mathcal{U}}\\
|\mathrm{supp}(b)|=\ell\end{subarray}}\left|\left\langle\varphi_{A},\widetilde{\chi}_{b}\right\rangle\right|^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant\left(\frac{100k^{3}m\ell}{|\mathcal{U}|^{2}}\right)^{\ell/2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}b\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{\bigcup\mathcal{U}}\\
|\mathrm{supp}(b)|=\ell\end{subarray}}\left|\left\langle\varphi_{A},\frac{\widetilde{\chi}_{b}}{\|\widetilde{\chi}_{b}\|_{2}}\right\rangle\right|^{2}

(by
Corollary

8.17
(1))

\displaystyle\leqslant\left(\frac{100k^{3}m\ell}{|\mathcal{U}|^{2}}\right)^{\ell/2}\sum_{d=1}^{\lfloor\ell/2\rfloor}\left\|P^{=d}_{\mathfrak{X}}[\varphi_{A}]\right\|_{2}^{2}.

(by
Corollary

8.17
(2)(3))

Since
A
is a global set in
\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}
of size
2^{-w}\cdot\left|\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}\right|
, it follows from
Proposition

8.8
that
\varphi_{A}=2^{w}\cdot 1_{A}
is both
(4,1,m)
-
L^{1}
-global and
(4,2^{w/2},m)
-
L^{2}
-global. If
\ell\leqslant w
, we can apply
Corollary

8.13
to the final line of the above display and get

\left\|\boldsymbol{P}^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\mu}[\varphi_{A}]^{=\ell}\right\|_{2}^{2}\leqslant\left(\frac{100k^{3}m\ell}{|\mathcal{U}|^{2}}\right)^{\ell/2}\cdot\frac{\ell}{2}\left(\frac{10^{5}\cdot 4\cdot(w/2)}{\ell/2}\right)^{\ell/2}=\frac{\ell}{2}\left(\frac{4\cdot 10^{7}k^{3}mw}{|\mathcal{U}|^{2}}\right)^{\ell/2}\leqslant\left(\frac{w}{|\mathcal{U}|}\right)^{\ell/2},

since
|\mathcal{U}|\geqslant 10^{8}k^{3}m
and
\frac{\ell}{2}\leqslant 2^{\ell/2}
. If
\ell>w/2
, we note that
\sum_{d=1}^{\lfloor\ell/2\rfloor}\left\|P^{=d}_{\mathfrak{X}}[\varphi_{A}]\right\|_{2}^{2}\leqslant\|\varphi_{A}\|_{2}^{2}
. Therefore, we have

\left\|\boldsymbol{P}^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\mu}[\varphi_{A}]^{=\ell}\right\|_{2}^{2}\leqslant\left(\frac{100k^{3}m\ell}{|\mathcal{U}|^{2}}\right)^{-\ell/2}\cdot 2^{w}\leqslant 2^{w}\cdot\left(\frac{\ell}{4|\mathcal{U}|}\right)^{\ell/2},

since
|\mathcal{U}|\geqslant 10^{8}k^{3}m
. Combining the above two displays, we conclude for
1\leqslant\ell\leqslant km
that

\left\|\boldsymbol{P}^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\mu}[\varphi_{A}]^{=\ell}\right\|_{2}^{2}\leqslant F(|\mathcal{U}|,\ell,w/2),

and thus the proof is complete.
∎

Appendix A
The Decomposition Lemma

In this appendix, we prove
Lemma

6.11
. The proof is a rather straightforward (sometimes verbatim) adaptation of
[
FMW25
, Section 2]
. The main difference from
[
FMW25
]
is that here we refine the analysis so that the decomposition applies to protocols with communication complexity as large as
\Theta(\sqrt{n})
, rather than just
\Theta(n^{1/3})
. Nevertheless, this added strength is not needed for the current paper — we still only invoke
Lemma

6.11
for protocols of complexity
O(n^{1/3})
, as in
[
FMW25
]
.

A.1
The Set Decomposition Lemma

The first step towards the decomposition of communication protocols is the decomposition of sets of possible inputs to a single player. Recall from
Definition

5.9
that the input to a player is chosen in a labeled matching space of the form
\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}
, where
\mathcal{U}
is a
k
-universe and
m\leqslant|\mathcal{U}|
is a nonnegative integer. We develop the following set decomposition lemma that applies to a large subset
A\subseteq\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}
, analogous to
[
FMW25
, Lemma 2.6]
.

Recall from
Definition

6.1
that restrictions on
\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}
are given by elements of
\Omega^{\mathcal{U},\leqslant m}
.

Lemma A.1

(Set decomposition)
.

Fix a
k
-universe
\mathcal{U}
and a nonnegative integer
m\leqslant|\mathcal{U}|
. Let
\mathbf{z}^{\prime}\in\Omega^{\mathcal{U},\leqslant m}
be a restriction on
\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}
, and take any subset
A\subseteq\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\mathbf{z}^{\prime}}
. Then we can decompose
A
into a disjoint union of subsets
A_{(1)},A_{(2)},\dots,A_{(\ell)}
such that:

(1)

Globalness:
for each
i\in[\ell]
, there exists a restriction
\mathbf{z}_{(i)}
that subsumes
\mathbf{z}^{\prime}
such that
A_{(i)}\subseteq\Omega_{\mathbf{z}_{(i)}}
and
A_{(\ell)}
is
\mathbf{z}_{(\ell)}
-global.

(2)

Size of the restrictions
: the restrictions
\mathbf{z}_{(i)}
satisfy the following inequality:

\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\frac{\left|A_{(i)}\right|}{|A|}\left(\left|\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z}_{(i)})\right|+\log_{2}\left(\frac{\left|\Omega_{\mathbf{z}_{(i)}}^{\mathcal{U},m}\right|}{\left|A_{(i)}\right|}\right)\right)\leqslant\left|\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z}^{\prime})\right|+\log_{2}\left(\frac{\left|\Omega_{\mathbf{z}^{\prime}}^{\mathcal{U},m}\right|}{|A|}\right)+2.

Proof.

The proof of the lemma is algorithmic, and the decomposition algorithm is given in
Algorithm

5
.

Input :
a restriction
\mathbf{z}^{\prime}
and a set
A\subseteq\Omega_{\mathbf{z}^{\prime}}^{\mathcal{U},m}

Output :
a sequence of sets
A_{(1)},\dots,A_{(\ell)}
and a sequence of restrictions
z_{(1)},\dots,z_{(\ell)}

i\leftarrow 0

1

while

A
is not
\mathbf{z}^{\prime}
-global

do

2

i\leftarrow i+1

3

find a restriction
\mathbf{z}_{(i)}
with largest possible support size such that
\mathbf{z}_{(i)}
subsumes
\mathbf{z}^{\prime}
and

\displaystyle\frac{\left|A\cap\Omega_{\mathbf{z}_{(i)}}^{\mathcal{U},m}\right|}{\left|\Omega_{\mathbf{z}_{(i)}}^{\mathcal{U},m}\right|}>2^{\left|\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z}_{(i)})\right|-|\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z}^{\prime})|}\cdot\frac{|A|}{\left|\Omega_{\mathbf{z}^{\prime}}^{\mathcal{U},m}\right|}

(A.1)

4

A_{(i)}\leftarrow A\cap\Omega_{\mathbf{z}_{(i)}}^{\mathcal{U},m}

5

A\leftarrow A\setminus A_{(i)}

6

if

A
is nonempty (and
\mathbf{z}^{\prime}
-global)

then

7

i\leftarrow i+1

8

A_{(i+1)}\leftarrow A

9

z_{(i+1)}\leftarrow\mathbf{z}^{\prime}

10

\ell\leftarrow i

11

Algorithm 5

Decompose
(A,\mathbf{z}^{\prime})

To check the first property, assume on the contrary that some
A_{(i)}
is not
\mathbf{z}_{(i)}
-global. Then by
Definition

6.6
, there must exists a restriction
\mathbf{z}
that subsumes
\mathbf{z}_{(i)}
and

\frac{\left|A_{(i)}\cap\Omega_{\mathbf{z}}^{\mathcal{U},m}\right|}{\left|\Omega_{\mathbf{z}}^{\mathcal{U},m}\right|}>2^{|\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z})|-\left|\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z}_{(i)})\right|}\cdot\frac{\left|A_{(i)}\cap\Omega_{\mathbf{z}_{(i)}}^{\mathcal{U},m}\right|}{\left|\Omega_{\mathbf{z}_{(i)}}^{\mathcal{U},m}\right|}.

(A.2)

Since the choice of
\mathbf{z}_{(i)}
satisfies (
A.1
) and
A_{(i)}=A\cap\Omega_{\mathbf{z}_{(i)}}
, we can combine (
A.2
) and (
A.1
) and get

\frac{\left|A\cap\Omega_{\mathbf{z}}^{\mathcal{U},m}\right|}{\left|\Omega_{\mathbf{z}}^{\mathcal{U},m}\right|}>2^{|\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z})|-\left|\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z}^{\prime})\right|}\cdot\frac{\left|A\cap\Omega_{\mathbf{z}^{\prime}}^{\mathcal{U},m}\right|}{\left|\Omega_{\mathbf{z}^{\prime}}^{\mathcal{U},m}\right|}.

This contradicts the maximality of the choice of
\mathbf{z}_{(i)}
since
|\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z})|>\left|\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z}_{(i)})\right|
.

We now check the second property, and towards that end we denote
A_{(\geqslant i)}=\bigcup_{j=i}^{\ell}A_{(j)}
.
Taking logs of (
A.1
) gives

\log_{2}\frac{\left|\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\mathbf{z}_{(i)}}\right|}{\left|A_{(i)}\right|}+\left|\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z}_{(i)})\right|\leqslant\log_{2}\frac{\left|\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\mathbf{z}^{\prime}}\right|}{\left|A_{(\geqslant i)}\right|}+|\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z}^{\prime})|=\log_{2}\frac{\left|\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\mathbf{z}^{\prime}}\right|}{|A|}+\log_{2}\frac{|A|}{\left|A_{(\geqslant i)}\right|}+|\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z}^{\prime})|.

Multiplying this inequality by
|A_{(i)}|/|A|
and summing over all
i\in[\ell]
we get:

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\frac{|A_{(i)}|}{|A|}\left(|\mathrm{supp}(z_{(i)})|+\log_{2}\frac{\left|\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\mathbf{z}_{(i)}}\right|}{|A_{(i)}|}\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant\log_{2}\frac{\left|\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\mathbf{z}^{\prime}}\right|}{|A|}+|\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z}^{\prime})|+\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\frac{|A_{(i)}|}{|A|}\cdot\log_{2}\frac{|A|}{|A_{(\geqslant i)}|}

\displaystyle\leqslant\log_{2}\frac{\left|\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\mathbf{z}^{\prime}}\right|}{|A|}+|\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z}^{\prime})|+\int_{0}^{1}\log_{2}\frac{1}{1-x}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leqslant\log_{2}\frac{\left|\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\mathbf{z}^{\prime}}\right|}{|A|}+|\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z}^{\prime})|+2,

where the second transition is because
\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\frac{|A_{(i)}|}{|A|}\cdot\log_{2}\frac{|A|}{|A_{(\geqslant i)}|}
is a lower Riemann sum for
the function
f(x)=\log_{2}\left(1/(1-x)\right)
with points
x_{(i)}=\frac{|A_{(1)}|}{|A|}+\dots+\frac{|A_{(i-1)}|}{|A|}
, and the last transition is by a direct calculation.
∎

A.2
From Arbitrary Protocols to Global Protocols

Now we shift our focus from subsets of a single labeled matching space
\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}
to communication protocols for the game
\mathsf{DIHP}(G,n,\alpha,K)
(defined in
Definition

5.9
).
For the remainder of this appendix, we fix a distribution-labeled
k
-graph
G=(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E},N,(\mu_{\mathsf{e}})_{\mathsf{e}\in\mathcal{E}})
along with an integer
K>0
and a parameter
\alpha>0
.

We will show how to use
Lemma

A.1
to transform any protocol into a
global protocol
.
Before doing so, we must formally define what we mean by a “global protocol” and how we measure its cost.
A global protocol proceeds in discrete communication rounds, with exactly one player speaking in each round.
The globalness requirement demands that, at the end of every round, the set of inputs consistent with the transcript so far is global.
To quantify the amount of information revealed during the communication process, we introduce the following potential function for structured rectangles (as defined in
Definition

6.9
).

Definition A.2
.

For restrictions
\boldsymbol{\zeta}=\left(\mathbf{z}^{(\mathsf{e},j)}\right)_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}
and a rectangle
R=\prod_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}A^{(\mathsf{e},j)}
such that
A^{(\mathsf{e},j)}\subseteq\Omega^{\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{e}},m}_{\mathbf{z}^{(\mathsf{e},j)}}
, we define the potential of
(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)
as:

\displaystyle\phi(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R):=\sum_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}\left|\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z}^{(\mathsf{e},j)})\right|+\log_{2}\left(\frac{\left|\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\mathbf{z}^{(i)}}\right|}{|A^{(i)}|}\right).

The formal definition of global protocols is given as follows.

Definition A.3
.

A communication protocol
\Pi
for
\textsf{DIHP}(G,n,\alpha,K)
is called an
r
-round global communication protocol if it specifies the following procedure of communications:

•

the
K|\mathcal{E}|
players take turns to send messages according
\Pi
;

•

there are at most
r
rounds of communications, and there is only one player sending message in a single round;

•

the length of message in each round of communications is not bounded; instead, from the perspective of rectangles, after each round of communications, a
\boldsymbol{\zeta}
-global rectangle
R
is further partitioned into a disjoint union of rectangles
R_{(1)},\dots,R_{(\ell)}
such that: (1)
R_{(i)}
is
\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{(i)}
-global; (2)
\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{(i)}
subsumes
\boldsymbol{\zeta}
; (3) the following inequality holds:

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{k}\frac{|R_{(i)}|}{|R|}\phi(\boldsymbol{\zeta}_{(i)},R_{(i)})\leqslant\phi(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)+3.

Note that in the above definitions of global protocols, we measure a protocol by its average potential but not its communication cost.

We now show an explicit construction of a global protocol
\Pi^{\mathrm{ref}}
given any communication protocol
\Pi
, and show that
\mathrm{adv}(\Pi^{\mathrm{ref}})\geqslant\mathrm{adv}(\Pi)
.

Lemma A.4
.

Given a communication protocol
\Pi
for
\mathsf{DIHP}(G,n,\alpha,K)
with communication complexity at most
r
, we can construct an
r
-round global protocol
\Pi^{\mathrm{ref}}
for
\mathsf{DIHP}(G,n,\alpha,K)
such that for any leaf rectangle
R
of
\Pi^{\mathrm{ref}}
, the output of
\Pi
is constant on
R
.

Proof.

We start with some setup. For convenience, given an arbitrary communication protocol
\Pi
with
|\Pi|=r
, we consider its tree structure. Without loss of generality, assume that at each round, a player sends exactly one bit of message. In this case, the communication tree is a binary tree. Furthermore, we extend the tree so that all leaf nodes lie at the same depth. In particular, these modifications do not increase the communication cost or decrease the advantage of
\Pi
. Each node
u
on the tree has an associated rectangle
R_{u}=\prod_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}A^{(\mathsf{e},j)}
. We use
\mathcal{N}_{d}
to denote the set of all rectangles (nodes) of
\Pi
of depth
d
, where root node is of depth
0
. In particular,
\mathcal{N}_{r}
denotes the set of all leaf rectangles (nodes) of
\Pi
, and each leaf rectangle (node) is labeled with an output, either “1” or “0”.

With the setup described above, we now construct the global protocol
\Pi^{\mathrm{ref}}
(where the superscript “
\mathrm{ref}
” stands for “refined”).
The formal construction of
\Pi^{\mathrm{ref}}
is described in Algorithm
6
, but it is helpful to think
of the construction slightly less formally. Note that viewing the protocol
\Pi
as a communication tree, we have that each node in it corresponds to a rectangle. Thus,
in
\Pi^{\mathrm{ref}}
we proceed going over the nodes of this tree, starting with the root node of
\Pi
, and decompose each one of these rectangle into structured rectangles (as defined in
Definition

6.9
) using
Lemma

A.1
.

player

gets input
\mathbf{y}^{(\mathsf{e},j)}\in\Omega^{\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{e}},\alpha n}

Output :
a bit
\texttt{ans}\in\{0,1\}

\Pi^{\mathrm{ref}}

(see Lines 3–10 in Algorithm

). Note that every rectangle produced by the refined protocol

R
. We define

after the first
d

\mathcal{N}_{d}

•
