is a set of rectangles, whereas
\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{ref}}_{d}
is a set of pairs, each consisting of a restriction and a rectangle.

•

The notion of “depth” differs: a rectangle
R\in\mathcal{N}_{d}
is obtained by protocol
\Pi
after exactly
d
bits have been communicated, while a pair
(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)\in\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{ref}}_{d}
is produced by protocol
\Pi^{\mathrm{ref}}
after
d
rounds of communication, with each round involving the transmission of a bit
b\in\{0,1\}
and an integer
t
.

First, we show that
\Pi^{\mathrm{ref}}
is a global protocol as per
Definition

A.3
. The discussion above shows that (1)
\Pi^{\mathrm{ref}}
has exactly
r
rounds of communications; (2) after
0\leqslant d\leqslant r
rounds of communications, the resulting rectangles in
\mathcal{N}_{d}^{\mathrm{ref}}
are all global with respect to restrictions; (3) for all
(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)\in\mathcal{N}_{d-1}^{\mathrm{ref}}
and
(\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{\prime},R^{\prime})\in\mathcal{N}_{d}^{\mathrm{ref}}
such that
R^{\prime}\subseteq R
, we have
\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{\prime}
subsume
\boldsymbol{\zeta}
. Thus, we
have the first two items in
Definition

A.3
, and
we next show the third item.

It suffices to upper bound the potential increment after each round of communications. Assume that after
d
rounds of communication according to
\Pi^{\mathrm{ref}}
, we obtain a structured rectangle
(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)\in\mathcal{N}_{d}^{\mathrm{ref}}
and player
(\mathsf{e},j)
will speak in the next round. The communication of the player
(\mathsf{e},j)
divides
A^{(\mathsf{e},j)}
into two parts
A_{0},A_{1}
, which decomposes the rectangle
R
into two disjoint rectangles
R_{0},R_{1}
via the message of
b
(see lines 3 to 6 in Algorithm
6
). In lines 7 to 10,
R_{0}
and
R_{1}
are further decomposed into several structured rectangles separately by the message
t\in[\ell]
. We have:

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{\prime},R^{\prime})\in\mathcal{N}_{d+1}^{\mathrm{ref}}\\
R^{\prime}\subseteq R\end{subarray}}\frac{|R^{\prime}|}{|R|}\cdot\phi(\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{\prime},R^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\frac{|R_{0}|}{|R|}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{\prime},R^{\prime})\in\mathcal{N}_{d+1}^{\mathrm{ref}}\\
R^{\prime}\subseteq R_{0}\end{subarray}}\frac{|R^{\prime}|}{|R_{0}|}\cdot\phi(\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{\prime},R^{\prime})+\frac{|R_{1}|}{|R|}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{\prime},R^{\prime})\in\mathcal{N}_{d+1}^{\mathrm{ref}}\\
R^{\prime}\subseteq R_{1}\end{subarray}}\frac{|R^{\prime}|}{|R_{1}|}\cdot\phi(\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{\prime},R^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{|R_{0}|}{|R|}\left(\phi(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)+\log_{2}\left(\frac{|R|}{|R_{0}|}\right)+2\right)+\frac{|R_{1}|}{|R|}\left(\phi(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)+\log_{2}\left(\frac{|R|}{|R_{1}|}\right)+2\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant\phi(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)+3,

where the second transition is by
Lemma

A.1
, and the last transition comes from the fact that the binary entropy is upper bounded by
1
.

\Pi
is constant on leaf rectangles of
\Pi^{\mathrm{ref}}
.

By construction, it is easy to see that each leaf rectangle
R
of
\Pi
is subdivided into several subrectangles by the refined protocol
\Pi^{\mathrm{ref}}
, thus the statment holds.
∎

Remark A.5
.

The structured rectangles induced by the refined protocol
\Pi^{\mathrm{ref}}
are exactly the collection
\mathcal{R}
that we want to construct in Lemma
6.11
(after deleting some bad pairs). We will formally prove it in Section
A.3
.

A.3
Bounding the Weights of “Bad” Rectangles

Having transformed arbitrary protocols to global ones, we proceed to analyze global protocols. Assume that we have a
r
-round global protocol
\Pi
. To prove Lemma
6.11
for
\Pi
, it suffices to prove that only a small fraction of the structured rectangles in
\mathcal{R}
are not
(10^{5}r)
-good (as defined in
Definition

6.10
). To this end, we make the following definitions.

Definition A.6
.

Let
\Pi
be an
r
-round global protocol.
For each
0\leqslant d\leqslant r
, we let
\mathcal{R}^{d}(\Pi)
denote the collection of structured rectangles obtained after
d
rounds of communication in
\Pi
.
In particular, we write
\mathcal{R}^{\mathrm{leaf}}(\Pi)=\mathcal{R}^{r}(\Pi)
for the set of structured rectangles at the leaves of the protocol tree. For
d\geqslant r+1
, the collection
\mathcal{R}^{d}(\Pi)
is the emptyset.

Definition A.7
.

Let
\Pi
be a global protocol for
\mathsf{DIHP}(G,n,\alpha,K)
and let
W
be a positive real number. We define

\mathcal{R}^{\mathrm{bad}}(\Pi,W)
as the following set of structured rectangles:

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}^{\mathrm{bad}}(\Pi,W):=\left\{(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)\in\mathcal{R}^{\mathrm{leaf}}:\text{$(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)$ is not $W$-good as per \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{def:good_rec}}\right\}.

The main lemma of this section is the following.

Lemma A.8
.

For any fixed distribution-labeled
k
-graph
G
, integer
K>0
and parameter
\alpha>0
, there exists a constant
\eta>0
such that if
r\leqslant\eta\sqrt{n}
, any
r
-round global protocol
\Pi
for
\mathsf{DIHP}(G,n,\alpha,K)
satisfies

\displaystyle\sum_{(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)\in\mathcal{R}^{\mathrm{bad}}(\Pi,10^{5}r)}\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{no}}(R)\leqslant 0.01.

We observe that the desired
Lemma

6.11
(restated below) follows immediately from
Lemma

A.8
.

See
6.11

Proof of
Lemma

6.11
assuming
Lemma

A.8
.

Let
r=|\Pi|
, and apply
Lemma

A.4
to transform
\Pi
into an
r
-round global protocol
\Pi^{\mathrm{ref}}
.
Consider the collection

\mathcal{R}:=\mathcal{R}^{\mathrm{leaf}}\!\left(\Pi^{\mathrm{ref}}\right)\setminus\mathcal{R}^{\mathrm{bad}}\!\left(\Pi^{\mathrm{ref}},10^{5}r\right).

We claim that
\mathcal{R}
satisfies the three conditions stated in the lemma.

The second condition follows directly from
Definition

A.7
, and the third condition follows from the guarantee of
Lemma

A.4
.
The first condition follows from
Lemma

A.8
together with the obvious identity

\sum_{(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)\in\mathcal{R}^{\mathrm{leaf}}(\Pi^{\mathrm{ref}})}\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{no}}(R)=1.\qed

The remainder of this section is devoted for the proof of
Lemma

A.8
, modulo two technical claims that are proved in
Section

A.4
. The following notations will be useful in the proof.

Notation A.9
.

For a set
T
of
k
-hyperedges on the vertex set
\mathcal{V}\times[n]
, we write
V(T)
for the set of vertices incident to at least one hyperedge in
T
. For a restriction sequence
\boldsymbol{\zeta}=\left(\mathbf{z}^{(\mathsf{e},j)}\right)_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}
, we define

V(\boldsymbol{\zeta}):=\bigcup_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}V\left(\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z}^{(\mathsf{e},j)})\right).

Proof of
Lemma

A.8
.

First, we define the following two subcollections
\mathcal{R}_{1},\mathcal{R}_{2}\subseteq\mathcal{R}^{\mathrm{leaf}}(\Pi)
:

(1)

\mathcal{R}_{1}
is the collection of structured rectangles
(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)\in\mathcal{R}^{\mathrm{leaf}}(\Pi)
such that
\phi(\zeta,R)>10^{5}r
;

(2)

\mathcal{R}_{2}
is the collection of
structured rectangles
(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)\in\mathcal{R}^{\mathrm{leaf}}(\Pi)
such that either the hyperedge sets
\left(\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z}^{(\mathsf{e},j)})\right)_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}
are not pairwise disjoint or their union contains a cycle.

It is easy to see that
\mathcal{R}^{\mathrm{bad}}(\Pi,10^{5}r)\subseteq\mathcal{R}_{1}\cup\mathcal{R}_{2}
. The pairs that violate the first condition in the definition of goodness (
Definition

6.10
) are included in
\mathcal{R}_{2}
, while the pairs violating the second or the third conditions are included in
\mathcal{R}_{1}
. It suffices to prove the following bounds regarding the two subcollections
\mathcal{R}_{1},\mathcal{R}_{2}
:

(1)

\sum_{(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)\in\mathcal{R}_{1}}\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{no}}(R)\leqslant 0.005
;

(2)

\sum_{(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)\in\mathcal{R}_{2}\setminus\mathcal{R}_{1}}\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{no}}(R)\leqslant 0.005
.

Upper bound for
\mathcal{R}_{1}
.

To upper bound of the total weight of structured rectangles
(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)
with
\phi(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)\geqslant 10^{5}r
, we first bound the weighted sum of potentials
\phi(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)
over all leaf pairs
(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)\in\mathcal{R}^{\mathrm{leaf}}(\Pi)
. More precisely, we show that

\displaystyle\sum_{(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)\in\mathcal{R}^{\mathrm{leaf}}(\Pi)}\frac{|R|}{\left|\prod_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}\Omega^{\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{e}},\alpha n}\right|}\cdot\phi(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)\leqslant 3\cdot r,

(A.3)

and the proof proceeds by induction argument on the depth
d
: we prove that for all
d
,

\displaystyle\sum_{(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)\in\mathcal{R}^{d}(\Pi)}\frac{|R|}{\left|\prod_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}\Omega^{\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{e}},\alpha n}\right|}\cdot\phi(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)\leqslant 3\cdot d.

(A.4)

When
d=0
the statement is clear as
\mathcal{R}^{0}(\Pi)
only
contains the trivial rectangle
\prod_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}\Omega^{\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{e}},\alpha n}
, whose potential equals
0
.
Let
d>0
and assume that (
A.4
) holds for
d-1
. We have

\displaystyle\quad\sum_{(\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{\prime},R^{\prime})\in\mathcal{R}^{d}(\Pi)}\frac{|R^{\prime}|}{\left|\prod_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}\Omega^{\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{e}},\alpha n}\right|}\cdot\phi(\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{\prime},R^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\sum_{(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)\in\mathcal{R}^{d-1}(\Pi)}\frac{|R|}{\left|\prod_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}\Omega^{\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{e}},\alpha n}\right|}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{\prime},R^{\prime})\in\mathcal{R}^{d}(\Pi)\\
R^{\prime}\subseteq R,\end{subarray}}\frac{|R^{\prime}|}{|R|}\cdot\phi(\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{\prime},R^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)\in\mathcal{R}^{d-1}(\Pi)}\frac{|R|}{\left|\prod_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}\Omega^{\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{e}},\alpha n}\right|}\cdot\left(\phi(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)+3\right)\leqslant 3(d-1)+3=3d,

where the second transition is by
Definition

A.3
, and the last transition is due to the inductive hypothesis. This completes the inductive step, and in particular establishes (
A.3
).

The bound on
\mathcal{R}_{1}
now follows by Markov’s inequality applied on (
A.3
):

\displaystyle\sum_{(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)\in\mathcal{R}_{1}}\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{no}}(R)

\displaystyle=\sum_{(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)\in\mathcal{R}_{1}}\frac{|R|}{\left|\prod_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}\Omega^{\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{e}},\alpha n}\right|}

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{1}{10^{5}r}\sum_{(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)\in\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{leaf}}}\frac{|R|}{\left|\prod_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}\Omega^{\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{e}},\alpha n}\right|}\cdot\phi(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{3\cdot r}{10^{5}r}<0.005.

Upper bound for
\mathcal{R}_{2}\setminus\mathcal{R}_{1}
.

For an integer
d\in\{1,2,\dots,r\}
and a structured rectangle
(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)\in\mathcal{R}^{d}(\Pi)
, we write

(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)\mapsto\left(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\zeta}},\widetilde{R}\right)

for the unique parent structured rectangle
\left(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\zeta}},\widetilde{R}\right)\in\mathcal{R}^{d-1}(\Pi)
such that
R\subseteq\widetilde{R}
. When we write a summation

\sum_{(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)\mapsto\left(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\zeta}},\widetilde{R}\right)}(\cdot),

we mean the sum over all structured rectangles
(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)\in\bigcup_{d=1}^{r}\mathcal{R}^{d}(\Pi)
together with their respective parents
\left(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\zeta}},\widetilde{R}\right)
.

The main component of the proof is to obtain the following inequality for some constant
J
:

\displaystyle\sum_{(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)\in\mathcal{R}_{2}\setminus\mathcal{R}_{1}}\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{no}}(R)\leqslant\frac{J}{n}\cdot\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)\mapsto\left(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\zeta}},\widetilde{R}\right)\\
|V(\boldsymbol{\zeta})|\leqslant 10^{5}r\end{subarray}}\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{no}}(R)\left(|V(\boldsymbol{\zeta})|^{2}-\left|V(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\zeta}})\right|^{2}\right).

(A.5)

Indeed, once we have (
A.5
), a change of variables on the right-hand side gives

\displaystyle\sum_{(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)\in\mathcal{R}_{2}\setminus\mathcal{R}_{1}}\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{no}}(R)

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{J}{n}\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)\mapsto\left(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\zeta}},\widetilde{R}\right)\\
|V(\boldsymbol{\zeta})|\leqslant 10^{5}r\end{subarray}}\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{no}}(R)|V(\boldsymbol{\zeta})|^{2}-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{\prime},R^{\prime})\mapsto(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)\\
|V(\boldsymbol{\zeta^{\prime}})|\leqslant 10^{5}r\end{subarray}}\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{no}}(R^{\prime})|V(\boldsymbol{\zeta})|^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{J}{n}\cdot\sum_{d=0}^{r}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)\in\mathcal{R}^{d}(\Pi)\\
|V(\boldsymbol{\zeta})|\leqslant 10^{5}r\end{subarray}}|V(\boldsymbol{\zeta})|^{2}\cdot\mathcal{D}\Big(\mathrm{Reduced}(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)\Big),

(A.6)

where for any
R\in\mathcal{R}^{d}(\Pi)
with
0\leqslant d\leqslant r
, we define

\mathrm{Reduced}(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R):=R\setminus\left(\bigcup_{(\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{\prime},R^{\prime})}R^{\prime}\right),

with the union taken over all children pairs
(\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{\prime},R^{\prime})\in\mathcal{R}^{d+1}(\Pi)
such that
|V(\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{\prime})|\leqslant 10^{5}r
and
(\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{\prime},R^{\prime})\mapsto(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)
. Note that since any leaf pair
(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)\in\mathcal{R}^{r}(\Pi)
has no children in
\mathcal{R}^{r+1}(\Pi)
(which is empty), for these structured rectangles we have
\mathrm{Reduced}(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R){\mathrel{\to}^{\text{def}}}R
.

Since the collection of rectangles
\mathrm{Reduced}(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)
for
(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)\in\bigcup_{d=0}^{r}\mathcal{R}^{d}(\Pi)
are clearly pairwise disjoint, the sum of their
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{no}}
weights is at most 1. We can thus conclude from (
A.6
)

\displaystyle\sum_{(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)\in\mathcal{R}_{2}\setminus\mathcal{R}_{1}}\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{no}}(R)\leqslant\frac{J}{n}\cdot(10^{5}r)^{2}\sum_{d=0}^{r}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)\in\mathcal{R}^{d}(\Pi)\\
|V(\boldsymbol{\zeta})|\leqslant 10^{5}r\end{subarray}}\mathcal{D}\left(\mathrm{Reduced}(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)\right)\leqslant\frac{J}{n}\cdot(10^{5}r)^{2}.

When
\eta
is chosen to be sufficiently small and
r\leqslant\eta\sqrt{n}
, the value of
\frac{J}{n}\cdot(10^{5}r)^{2}
is at most
0.005
, as desired.

Proof of the inequality (
A.5
).

Suppose
(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)
is a structured rectangle in
\bigcup_{d=1}^{r}\mathcal{R}^{d}(\Pi)
, where the restriction sequence
\boldsymbol{\zeta}
is written out as
\left(\mathbf{z}^{(\mathsf{e},j)}\right)_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}
. Let
\left(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\zeta}},\widetilde{R}\right)
be parent of
(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)
. We define the subset
B(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)\subseteq R
as the set of all joint inputs
\left(\mathbf{y}^{(\mathsf{e},j)}\right)_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times K}\in R
satisfying the following: there exists a player
(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]
and an edge
e\in\mathrm{supp}\left(\mathbf{y}^{(\mathsf{e},j)}\right)\setminus\mathrm{supp}\left(\mathbf{z}^{(\mathsf{e},j)}\right)
such that

\left|V(\{e\})\cap V(\boldsymbol{\zeta})\right|\geqslant 2\quad\text{and}\quad\left|V(\{e\})\cap\left(V(\boldsymbol{\zeta})\setminus V(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\zeta}})\right)\right|\geqslant 1.

We make the following two claims, the proofs of which are deferred to
Section

A.4
.

Claim A.10
.

For every structured rectangle
(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)\in\mathcal{R}_{2}\setminus\mathcal{R}_{1}
, there exists an integer
d\in[r]
and a structured rectangle
(\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{\mathrm{anc}},R^{\mathrm{anc}})\in\mathcal{R}^{d}(\Pi)
such that
|V(\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{\mathrm{anc}})|\leqslant 10^{5}r
and
R\subseteq B(\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{\mathrm{anc}},R^{\mathrm{anc}})
.

Claim A.11
.

There exists a constant
J
(depending only on
G,\alpha,K
) such that for any structured rectangle
(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)\in\bigcup_{d=1}^{r}\mathcal{R}^{d}(\Pi)
with
|V(\boldsymbol{\zeta})|\leqslant\sqrt{n}
, if
\left(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\zeta}},\widetilde{R}\right)
is its parent, then

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{no}}(B(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R))\leqslant\frac{J}{n}\cdot\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{no}}(R)\left(|V(\boldsymbol{\zeta})|^{2}-\left|V(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\zeta}})\right|^{2}\right).

A.10
implies

\displaystyle\sum_{(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)\in\mathcal{R}_{2}\setminus\mathcal{R}_{1}}\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{no}}(R)\leqslant\sum_{d=1}^{r}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\left(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R\right)\in\mathcal{R}^{d}(\Pi)\\
|\boldsymbol{\zeta}|\leqslant 10^{5}r\end{subarray}}\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{no}}(B(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)),

(A.7)

since the structured rectangles in
\mathcal{R}_{2}\setminus\mathcal{R}_{1}
are pairwise disjoint. Then plugging
A.11
into (
A.7
) yields the desired inequality (
A.5
), as long as
\eta\leqslant 10^{-5}
.
∎

A.4
Proofs of the Technical Claims

In this subsection, we finish the proofs of
A.10
and
A.11
, thereby completing the proof of
Lemma

A.8
. Throughout the proofs, we keep the notations introduced in the proof of
Lemma

A.8
.
We first prove
A.10
.

Proof of
A.10
.

Fix a structured rectangle
(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)\in\mathcal{R}_{2}\setminus\mathcal{R}_{1}
. By definition,
(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)\in\mathcal{R}^{\mathrm{leaf}}(\Pi)=\mathcal{R}^{r}(\Pi)
and
|V(\boldsymbol{\zeta})|\leqslant 10^{5}r
.

There is a unique path
P
in the protocol tree of
\Pi
that starts at this leaf
(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)
and terminates at the root rectangle (namely the whole space
\prod_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}\Omega^{\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{e}},\alpha n}
), obtained by iteratively replacing each node by its parent. In other words, each rectangle on the path
P
is the parent of its predecessor, and the sequence ends at the root. Let

\left(\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{\prime}=\left(\mathbf{z}^{\prime(\mathsf{e},j)}\right)_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]},\,R^{\prime}\right)

be the first structured rectangle along this path that satisfies the first condition of goodness (in
Definition

6.10
), namely:

•

The support sets
\bigl(\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z}^{(\mathsf{e},j)})\bigr)_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}
are pairwise disjoint;

•

The union
\bigcup_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z}^{(\mathsf{e},j)})
is cycle-free.

Note that by the definition of
\mathcal{R}_{2}
, the initial structured rectangle on the path
P
— namely, the leaf
(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)
— does not satisfy this condition. On the other hand, the final structured rectangle on the path, i.e., the whole space
\prod_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}\Omega^{\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{e}},\alpha n}
with empty restrictions, clearly does. Hence
(\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{\prime},R^{\prime})
is well defined as an element of
\mathcal{R}^{d}(\Pi)
for some
d\in\{0,1,\dots,r-1\}
.

We let

\left(\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{\prime\prime}=\left(\mathbf{z}^{\prime\prime(\mathsf{e},j)}\right)_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]},\,R^{\prime\prime}\right)

be the predecessor of
(\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{\prime},R^{\prime})
on the path. By the choice of
(\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{\prime},R^{\prime})
, we know that the structured rectangle
(\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{\prime\prime},R^{\prime\prime})
does not satisfy the first condition of goodness. In particular,
\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{\prime\prime}\neq\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{\prime}
. Furthermore, because exactly one player speaks in each round of the protocol
\Pi
, there exists exactly one player
(\mathsf{e}^{*},j^{*})\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]
for which
\mathbf{z}^{\prime\prime(\mathsf{e}^{*},j^{*})}\neq\mathbf{z}^{\prime(\mathsf{e}^{*},j^{*})}
.

The fact that
(\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{\prime\prime},R^{\prime\prime})
does not satisfy the first condition of goodness gives rise to the following two cases.

Case 1:
\bigcup_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}\mathrm{supp}\left(\mathbf{z}^{\prime\prime(\mathsf{e},j)}\right)
contains a cycle.

Since
(\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{\prime},R^{\prime})
satisfies the first condition of goodness, we know that in this case, the union

E:=\bigcup_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}\mathrm{supp}\left(\mathbf{z}^{\prime(\mathsf{e},j)}\right)

(A.8)

is cycle-free, while appending the matching

M:=\mathrm{supp}\left(\mathbf{z}^{\prime\prime(\mathsf{e}^{*},j^{*})}\right)\setminus\mathrm{supp}\left(\mathbf{z}^{\prime(\mathsf{e}^{*},j^{*})}\right)

(A.9)

to
E
creates some cycle. We claim that there must exist an edge
e\in M
such that at least two of its vertices already lie in
V(E)
. Indeed, suppose otherwise. Then there would exist a set of edges in
E\cup M
, with
\ell_{1}
edges from
E
and
\ell_{2}
edges from
M
, that covers at most
(\ell_{1}+\ell_{2})(k-1)
vertices (see
Section

2.1
for the definition of cycle-freeness). Since
E
is cycle-free the
\ell_{1}
edges from
E
together cover at least
\ell_{1}(k-1)+1
vertices. Then each of the
\ell_{2}
edges from
M
would then contribute at least
k-1
new vertices, giving an additional
\ell_{2}(k-1)
. Thus the total number of covered vertices would be at least
(\ell_{1}+\ell_{2})(k-1)+1
, contradicting the assumption. Hence such an edge
e
must exist.

Now let

\left(\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{\mathrm{anc}}=\left({\mathbf{z}^{\mathrm{anc}}}^{(\mathsf{e},j)}\right)_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]},\,R^{\mathrm{anc}}\right)

be the last structured rectangle along the segment of the path
P
from
(\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{\prime},R^{\prime})
to the root such that
V(\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{\mathrm{anc}})
contains at least two vertices of
e
. We clearly have
\left|V(\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{\mathrm{anc}})\right|\leqslant|V(\boldsymbol{\zeta})|\leqslant 10^{5}r
. It remains to show
R\subseteq B(\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{\mathrm{anc}},R^{\mathrm{anc}})
. Since
R\subseteq R^{\prime\prime}
, it suffices to show that
R^{\prime\prime}\subseteq B(\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{\mathrm{anc}},R^{\mathrm{anc}})
. Indeed, as
e
belongs to the difference

\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z}^{\prime\prime(\mathsf{e}^{*},j^{*})})\setminus\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z}^{\prime(\mathsf{e}^{*},j^{*})}),

we deduce that
e
also belongs to

\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{y}^{\prime\prime(\mathsf{e}^{*},j^{*})})\setminus\mathrm{supp}({\mathbf{z}^{\mathrm{anc}}}^{(\mathsf{e}^{*},j^{*})}),

since this is an enlargement of the larger set and a shrinking of the smaller one. Consequently, we have

\left(\mathbf{y}^{\prime\prime(\mathsf{e},j)}\right)_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}\in B(\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{\mathrm{anc}},R^{\mathrm{anc}}),

because

\left|V(\{e\})\cap V(\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{\mathrm{anc}})\right|\geqslant 2\quad\text{and}\quad\left|V(\{e\})\cap\left(V(\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{\mathrm{anc}})\setminus V(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{\mathrm{anc}}})\right)\right|\geqslant 1,

where
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{\mathrm{anc}}}
denotes the restriction sequence of the parent of
(\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{\mathrm{anc}},R^{\mathrm{anc}})
.

Case 2: The support sets
\bigl(\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z}^{(\mathsf{e},j)})\bigr)_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}
are not pairwise disjoint.

In this case, we still define the edge sets
E
and
M
as in (
A.8
) and (
A.9
). Now there must exists an edge
e\in M
that already belongs to
E
. In particular, at least two vertices of
e
belongs to
V(E)
. We can again let
(\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{\mathrm{anc}},R^{\mathrm{anc}})
be the last structured rectangle along the segment of the path
P
from
(\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{\prime},R^{\prime})
to the root such that
V(\boldsymbol{\zeta}^{\mathrm{anc}})
contains at least two vertices of
e
. The same conclusions as in Case 1 follows.
∎
Next, we prove
A.11
.

Proof of
A.11
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\zeta}=\left(\mathbf{z}^{(\mathsf{e},j)}\right)_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}
and
R=\prod_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}A^{(\mathsf{e},j)}
. For each
(\mathsf{e}^{*},j^{*})\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]
, we define
B^{(\mathsf{e}^{*},j^{*})}(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)
to be the set of all joint inputs

(\mathbf{y}^{(\mathsf{e},j)})_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}\in R

whose
(\mathsf{e}^{*},j^{*})
-coordinate,
\mathbf{y}^{(\mathsf{e}^{*},j^{*})}
, satisfies the following: there exists an edge

e\in\mathrm{supp}\left(\mathbf{y}^{(\mathsf{e}^{*},j^{*})}\right)\setminus\mathrm{supp}\left(\mathbf{z}^{(\mathsf{e}^{*},j^{*})}\right)

such that

\left|V(\{e\})\cap V(\boldsymbol{\zeta})\right|\geqslant 2\quad\text{and}\quad\left|V(\{e\})\cap\left(V(\boldsymbol{\zeta})\setminus V(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\zeta}})\right)\right|\geqslant 1.

By definition,
B(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)=\bigcup_{(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]}B^{(\mathsf{e},j)}(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)
. Therefore, it suffices to show that for any fixed player
(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]
, we have

\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{no}}\left(B^{(\mathsf{e},j)}(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)\right)\leqslant\frac{J}{|\mathcal{E}|K\cdot n}\cdot\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{no}}(R)\left(|V(\boldsymbol{\zeta})|^{2}-\left|V(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\zeta}})\right|^{2}\right)

(A.10)

for some constant
J
. In the remaining of the proof, we fix a player
(\mathsf{e},j)\in\mathcal{E}\times[K]
.

We define the edge set

E:=\left\{e\in\textstyle\prod(\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{e}})_{\setminus\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z}^{(\mathsf{e},j)})}\,\middle|\,\left|V(\{e\})\cap V(\boldsymbol{\zeta})\right|\geqslant 2\text{ and }\left|V(\{e\})\cap\left(V(\boldsymbol{\zeta})\setminus V(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\zeta}})\right)\right|\geqslant 1\right\}.

It is easy to see that

|E|\leqslant\left|V(\boldsymbol{\zeta})\setminus V(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\zeta}})\right|\cdot\left|V(\boldsymbol{\zeta})\right|\cdot n^{k-2}\leqslant n^{k-2}\left(|V(\boldsymbol{\zeta})|^{2}-\left|V(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\zeta}})\right|^{2}\right).

(A.11)

For each edge
e\in E
, we define the restricted domain

\Omega[e]:=\left\{\mathbf{y}\in\Omega^{\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{e}},\alpha n}_{\mathbf{z}^{(\mathsf{e},j)}}\,\middle|\,e\in\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{y})\right\}.

By the definitions of
B^{(\mathsf{e},j)}(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)
and the uniformity of
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{no}}
, we have

\frac{\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{no}}\left(B^{(\mathsf{e},j)}(\boldsymbol{\zeta},R)\right)}{\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{no}}(R)}=\frac{\left|A^{(\mathsf{e},j)}\cap\bigcup_{e\in E}\Omega[e]\right|}{\left|A^{(\mathsf{e},j)}\right|}\leqslant\sum_{e\in E}\frac{\left|A^{(\mathsf{e},j)}\cap\Omega[e]\right|}{\left|A^{(\mathsf{e},j)}\right|}.

(A.12)

The
\mathbf{z}^{(\mathsf{e},j)}
-globalness of
A^{(\mathsf{e},j)}
implies that for each
e\in E
,

\frac{\left|A^{(\mathsf{e},j)}\cap\Omega[e]\right|}{\left|A^{(\mathsf{e},j)}\right|}\leqslant 2\cdot\frac{\left|\Omega^{\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{e}},\alpha n}_{\mathbf{z}^{(\mathsf{e},j)}}\cap\Omega[e]\right|}{\left|\Omega^{\mathcal{U}_{\mathsf{e}},\alpha n}_{\mathbf{z}^{(\mathsf{e},j)}}\right|}=2\cdot\frac{\alpha n-\left|\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z}^{(\mathsf{e},j)})\right|}{\left(n-\left|\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{z}^{(\mathsf{e},j)})\right|\right)^{k}}\leqslant\frac{J}{|\mathcal{E}|K\cdot n^{k-1}}

(A.13)

for some constant
J>0
.
Here, the last transition uses the assumption that
|V(\boldsymbol{\zeta})|\leqslant\sqrt{n}
. Combining (
A.11
), (
A.12
) and (
A.13
) yields the desired inequality (
A.10
).
∎

Appendix B
Global Hypercontractivity in
\Omega

In this appendix we prove
Theorem

8.12
. The proof is a rather straightforward adaptation of
[
FMW25
, Section 4]
. We begin in
Sections

B.1
and
B.2
by establishing basic properties of the derivative operators (defined in
Definition

8.6
) and the projection operators (defined in
Definition

8.10
). In
Sections

B.3
and
B.4
, we incorporate a result from
[
KLM23
]
by comparing our labeled matching space
\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}
with a product space. Finally, we conclude with the proof of
Theorem

8.12
in
Section

B.5
.

The following two notations will be used throughout this appendix.

Notation B.1
.

Suppose
S
and
T
are disjoint finite sets. For two maps
\mathbf{x}_{1}:S\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}
and
\mathbf{x}_{2}:T\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}
, we define their concatenation
\mathbf{x}_{1}\uplus\mathbf{x}_{2}:S\sqcup T\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}
by setting
(\mathbf{x}_{1}\uplus\mathbf{x}_{2})(e):=\mathbf{x}_{1}(e)
for
e\in S
and
(\mathbf{x}_{1}\uplus\mathbf{x}_{2})(e):=\mathbf{x}_{2}(e)
for
e\in T
.

Notation B.2
.

Suppose
S
and
M
are finite sets such that
S\subseteq M
. For a map
\mathbf{a}:M\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}
, we define
\mathbf{a}_{|S}:S\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}
to be the restriction of
\mathbf{a}
to
S
, and define
\mathbf{a}_{\setminus S}:M\setminus S\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}
to be the restriction of
\mathbf{a}
to
M\setminus S
.

B.1
Derivatives Compose

We observe that the gadget function
H_{S}
(defined in
Definition

8.5
) used in the definition of the discrete derivative operators has the following simple Fourier decomposition.

Lemma B.3
.

We have the identity

H_{S}=\sum_{\mathbf{a}:S\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}\setminus\{0\}}\chi_{\mathbf{a}}.

Proof.

Straightforward calculation shows that for any
\mathbf{z}\in\mathrm{Map}\left(S,\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}\right)
,

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathbf{a}:S\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}\setminus\{0\}}\chi_{\mathbf{a}}(\mathbf{z})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{a}:S\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}\setminus\{0\}}\left(\prod_{e\in S}\chi_{\mathbf{a}(e)}(\mathbf{z}(e))\right)=\prod_{e\in S}\left(\sum_{\mathbf{a}(e)\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}\setminus\{0\}}\chi_{\mathbf{a}(e)}(\mathbf{z}(e))\right)

\displaystyle=\prod_{e\in S}\left(N^{k}\cdot\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathbf{z}(e)=0\right\}-\chi_{0}(\mathbf{z}(e))\right)=\prod_{e\in S}\left(N^{k}\cdot\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathbf{z}(e)=0\right\}-1\right)

\displaystyle=\prod_{e\in S}\left(N^{k}-1\right)^{\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathbf{z}(e)=0\right\}}(-1)^{\mathbbm{1}\left\{\mathbf{z}(e)\neq 0\right\}}=H_{S}(\mathbf{z}).\qed

This Fourier decomposition allows us to prove that the derivative operators (defined in
Definition

8.6
) compose with each other in the following natural way.

Lemma B.4
.

Suppose
S
and
T
are vertex disjoint matchings in
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{U},\leqslant m}
with
|S\cup T|\leqslant m
. Fix labels
\mathbf{x}_{1}:S\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}
and
\mathbf{x}_{2}:T\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}
. For any
f:\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
we have

D_{S,\mathbf{x}_{1}}D_{T,\mathbf{x}_{2}}[f]=D_{S\cup T,\mathbf{x}_{1}\uplus\mathbf{x}_{2}}[f].

Proof.

We have the following three canonical embeddings from
Definition

8.4
:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{i}

\displaystyle:\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\setminus(S\cup T)}\times\mathrm{Map}\left(S\cup T,\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}\right)\hookrightarrow\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m},

\displaystyle\mathfrak{i}_{1}

\displaystyle:\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\setminus(S\cup T)}\times\mathrm{Map}\left(S,\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}\right)\hookrightarrow\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\setminus T},\text{ and}

\displaystyle\mathfrak{i}_{2}

\displaystyle:\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\setminus(S\cup T)}\times\mathrm{Map}\left(S,\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}\right)\times\mathrm{Map}\left(T,\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}\right)\hookrightarrow\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}.

Directly from
Definition

8.6
we get

\displaystyle D_{S,\mathbf{x}_{1}}D_{T,\mathbf{x}_{2}}[f](\mathbf{y})

\displaystyle=\underset{\mathbf{z}_{1}:S\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[H_{S}(\mathbf{z}_{1})\cdot D_{T,\mathbf{x}_{2}}[f]\Big(\mathfrak{i}_{1}(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{x}_{1}-\mathbf{z}_{1})\Big)\right]

\displaystyle=\underset{\mathbf{z}_{1}:S\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[H_{S}(\mathbf{z}_{1})\cdot\underset{\mathbf{z}_{2}:T\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[H_{T}(\mathbf{z}_{2})\cdot f\Big(\mathfrak{i}_{2}(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{x}_{1}-\mathbf{z}_{1},\mathbf{x}_{2}-\mathbf{z}_{2})\Big)\right]\right]

\displaystyle=\underset{\mathbf{z}:S\cup T\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[H_{S\cup T}(\mathbf{z})\cdot f\Big(\mathfrak{i}(\mathbf{y},(\mathbf{x}_{1}\uplus\mathbf{x}_{2})-\mathbf{z}\Big)\right]

\displaystyle=D_{S\cup T,\mathbf{x}_{1}\uplus\mathbf{x}_{2}}[f](\mathbf{y}).\qed

Lemma

B.4
implies the following important corollary about the derivative-based globalness notion (defined in
Definition

8.7
).

Corollary B.5
.

If
f:\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
is
(r,\lambda,d)
-
L^{p}
-global, then for any matching
S\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{U},\leqslant d}
and label
\mathbf{x}\in\mathrm{Map}\left(S,\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}\right)
, the derivative
D_{S,\mathbf{x}}[f]
is
(r,r^{|S|}\lambda,d-|S|)
-
L^{p}
-global.

Proof.

For each matching
T\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{U}_{\setminus S},\,\leqslant d-|S|}
, we know that
S\cup T\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{U},\leqslant d}
. So by the assumption that
f
is
(r,\lambda,d)
-
L^{p}
-global, we have
\left\|D_{S\cup T,\mathbf{x}^{\prime\prime}}f\right\|_{p}\leqslant r^{|S|+|T|}\lambda
for any label
\mathbf{x}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathrm{Map}\left(S\cup T,\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}\right)
.
By
Lemma

B.4
it follows that
\left\|D_{T,\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}\left[D_{S,\mathbf{x}}f\right]\right\|_{p}\leqslant r^{|T|}\cdot r^{|S|}\lambda
for any label
\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Map}\left(T,\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}\right)
, as required.
∎

B.2
Projections Commutes with Derivatives

The goal of this subsection is to show that the derivative operators “commute” with the projection operators defined in
Definition

8.10
. For that purpose, we first compute the derivatives of character functions.

Proposition B.6
.

On the space
\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}
, given two pairs
(S,\mathbf{x}),(M,\mathbf{a})\in\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{U},\leqslant m}
, we have

D_{S,\mathbf{x}}[\psi_{M,\mathbf{a}}]=\begin{cases}\Psi(|\mathcal{U}|,m,|S|)^{-1/2}\cdot\chi_{\mathbf{a}_{|S}}(\mathbf{x})\cdot\psi_{M\setminus S,\,\mathbf{a}_{\setminus S}}&\text{if }S\subseteq M,\\
0&\text{if }S\not\subseteq M,\end{cases}

We note that in the above equation,
\psi_{M,\mathbf{a}}
is a character on
\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}
, while
\psi_{M\setminus S,\,\mathbf{a}_{\setminus S}}
is a character on
\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\setminus S}
.

Proof.

We consider the following two cases respectively.

Case 1:

S\not\subseteq M
. If
M\cup S
is not a matching, then
\psi_{M,\mathbf{a}}(\mathfrak{i}(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{z}))=0
for all
\mathbf{y}\in\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\setminus S}
and
\mathbf{z}\in\mathrm{Map}\left(S,\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}\right)
, and hence
D_{S,\mathbf{x}}[\psi_{M,\mathbf{a}}]=0
by definition. If
M\cup S
is a matching, pick an edge
e\in S\setminus M
and let
\mathbf{x}_{\setminus\{e\}}
be the restriction of
\mathbf{x}
to
S\setminus\{e\}
. It is easy to see that the value of the function
h:=D_{S\setminus\{e\},\,\mathbf{x}_{\setminus\{e\}}}[\psi_{M,\mathbf{a}}]
at an input
\mathbf{y}^{\prime}\in\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\setminus(S\setminus\{e\})}
does not depend on the coordinate
\mathbf{y}^{\prime}(e)
. Therefore by
Definition

8.6
, for any
\mathbf{y}\in\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\setminus S}
we can pick an arbitrary
\mathbf{y}^{\prime}\in\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\setminus(S\setminus\{e\})}
that extends
\mathbf{y}
and have

D_{\{e\},\mathbf{x}(e)}[h](\mathbf{y})=\frac{1}{N^{k}}\cdot\left(N^{k}-1\right)\cdot h(\mathbf{y}^{\prime})+\frac{N^{k}-1}{N^{k}}\cdot(-1)\cdot h(\mathbf{y}^{\prime})=0.

Now by
Lemma

B.4
we have
D_{S,\mathbf{x}}[\psi_{M,\mathbf{a}}]=D_{\{e\},\mathbf{x}(e)}[h]=0
.

Case 2:

S\subseteq M
. By
Definitions

8.2
and
8.6
, for
\mathbf{y}\in\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\setminus S}
we have

D_{S,\mathbf{x}}[\psi_{M,\mathbf{a}}](\mathbf{y})=\Psi(|\mathcal{U}|,m,|M|)^{-1/2}\prod_{e\in M\setminus S}\chi_{\mathbf{a}(e)}(\mathbf{y}(e))\cdot\underset{\mathbf{z}:S\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[H_{S}(\mathbf{z})\prod_{e\in S}\chi_{\mathbf{a}(e)}(\mathbf{x}(e)-\mathbf{z}(e))\right]

(B.1)

Using
Lemma

B.3
, we have

\displaystyle\underset{\mathbf{z}:S\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[H_{S}(\mathbf{z})\cdot\prod_{e\in S}\chi_{\mathbf{a}(e)}(\mathbf{x}(e)-\mathbf{z}(e))\right]

\displaystyle=\chi_{\mathbf{a}}(\mathbf{x})\cdot\left\langle H_{S},\chi_{\mathbf{a}_{|S}}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\chi_{\mathbf{a}}(\mathbf{x})\cdot\left\langle\sum_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}:S\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}\setminus\{0\}}\chi_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}},\chi_{\mathbf{a}_{|S}}\right\rangle=\chi_{\mathbf{a}}(\mathbf{x}).

(B.2)

By
Definition

8.6
again we have

\prod_{e\in M\setminus S}\chi_{\mathbf{a}(e)}(\mathbf{y}(e))=\Psi\left(|\mathcal{U}_{\setminus S}|,m-|S|,|M\setminus S|\right)^{1/2}\cdot\psi_{M\setminus S,\,\mathbf{a}_{\setminus S}}(\mathbf{y}).

(B.3)

Finally, by
Definition

8.1
we know that

\Psi(|\mathcal{U}|,m,|M|)^{-1/2}=\Psi(|\mathcal{U}|,m,|S|)^{-1/2}\cdot\Psi\left(|\mathcal{U}_{\setminus S}|,m-|S|,|M\setminus S|\right)^{-1/2}.

(B.4)

Plugging (
B.2
), (
B.3
), and (
B.4
) into (
B.1
) yields the conclusion.
∎

The following lemma then shows that the projection operators (defined in
Definition

8.10
) commute with the derivative operators, up to the obvious change in degrees.

Lemma B.7
.

Given an integer
d\leqslant m
, a function
f:\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
and any character
(S,\mathbf{x})\in\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{U},\leqslant m}
with
|S|\leqslant d
, we have

D_{S,\mathbf{x}}P_{\mathfrak{X}}^{=d}[f]=P_{\mathfrak{X}}^{=d-|S|}D_{S,\mathbf{x}}[f].

Proof.

On the one hand, it follows from
Propositions

8.11
and
B.6
that

D_{S,\mathbf{x}}P_{\mathfrak{X}}^{=d}[f]=\Psi(|\mathcal{U}|,m,|S|)^{-1/2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(M,\mathbf{a})\in\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{U},d}\\
M\supseteq S\end{subarray}}\langle f,\psi_{M,\mathbf{a}}\rangle\cdot\chi_{\mathbf{a}_{|S}}(\mathbf{x})\cdot\psi_{M\setminus S,\,\mathbf{a}_{\setminus S}}.

(B.5)

Note that in the above equation
\psi_{M,\mathbf{a}}
is the character on
\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}
while
\psi_{M\setminus S,\,\mathbf{a}_{\setminus S}}
is the character on
\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\setminus S}
.

On the other hand, for any character
(T,\mathbf{a})\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{U}_{\setminus S},\,d-|S|}
, we can calculate (using
Lemma

B.3
in the second transition)

\displaystyle\quad\Psi\Big(|\mathcal{U}_{\setminus S}|,m-|S|,|T|\Big)^{1/2}\cdot\left\langle D_{S,\mathbf{x}}[f],\psi_{T,\mathbf{a}^{\prime}}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\underset{\mathbf{y}\in\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\setminus S}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\underset{\mathbf{z}:S\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[H_{S}(\mathbf{z})\cdot f\Big(\mathfrak{i}(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{z})\Big)\right]\cdot\overline{\prod_{e\in T}\chi_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}(e)}(\mathbf{y}(e))}\right]

\displaystyle=\underset{\mathbf{y}\in\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\setminus S},\,\mathbf{z}:S\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left(\sum_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime\prime}:S\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}\setminus\{0\}}\chi_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime\prime}}(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{z})\right)\cdot f\Big(\mathfrak{i}(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{z})\Big)\cdot\overline{\prod_{e\in T}\chi_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}(e)}(\mathbf{y}(e))}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime\prime}:S\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}\setminus\{0\}}\chi_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime\prime}}(\mathbf{x})\cdot\underset{\mathbf{y}\in\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\setminus S},\,\mathbf{z}:S\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[f\Big(\mathfrak{i}(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{z})\Big)\cdot\overline{\prod_{e\in S}\chi_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime\prime}(e)}(\mathbf{z}(e))\prod_{e\in T}\chi_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}(e)}(\mathbf{y}(e))}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{a}:S\cup T\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}\setminus\{0\}\\
\mathbf{a}_{\setminus S}=\mathbf{a}^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\chi_{\mathbf{a}_{|S}}(\mathbf{x})\cdot\underset{\mathbf{y}\in\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\setminus S},\,\mathbf{z}:S\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[f\Big(\mathfrak{i}(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{z})\Big)\cdot\overline{\prod_{e\in S}\chi_{\mathbf{a}(e)}(\mathbf{z}(e))\prod_{e\in T}\chi_{\mathbf{a}(e)}(\mathbf{y}(e))}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{a}:S\cup T\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}\setminus\{0\}\\
\mathbf{a}_{\setminus S}=\mathbf{a}^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\chi_{\mathbf{a}_{|S}}(\mathbf{x})\cdot\Psi(|\mathcal{U}|,m,|S|)^{-1}\cdot\underset{\boldsymbol{\xi}\in\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[f(\boldsymbol{\xi})\cdot\overline{\prod_{e\in S\cup T}\chi_{\mathbf{a}(e)}(\boldsymbol{\xi}(e))}\right]

\displaystyle=\Psi(|\mathcal{U}|,m,|S|)^{-1}\cdot\sum_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}:S\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}\setminus\{0\}}\chi_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{x})\cdot\Psi(|\mathcal{U}|,m,|S\cup T|)^{1/2}\cdot\left\langle f,\psi_{S\cup T,\mathbf{a}^{\prime}\uplus\mathbf{a}}\right\rangle.

In the fifth transition above, we use the facts that
\prod_{e\in S\cup T}\chi_{\mathbf{a}(e)}(\boldsymbol{\xi}(e))
is nonzero only if
\boldsymbol{\xi}
lies in the image of the embedding
\mathfrak{i}:\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\setminus S}\times\mathrm{Map}\left(S,\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}\right)\hookrightarrow\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}
, and that this image has size
\Psi(|\mathcal{U}|,m,|S|)\cdot\left|\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}\right|
.
Now plugging into the above display the relation

\Psi\Big(|\mathcal{U}_{\setminus S}|,m-|S|,|T|\Big)^{1/2}=\Psi(|\mathcal{U}|,m,|S|)^{-1/2}\cdot\Psi(|\mathcal{U}|,m,|S\cup T|)^{1/2}

yields

\left\langle D_{S,\mathbf{x}}[f],\psi_{T,\mathbf{a}^{\prime}}\right\rangle=\Psi(|\mathcal{U}|,m,|S|)^{-1/2}\cdot\sum_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}:S\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}\setminus\{0\}}\chi_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{x})\cdot\left\langle f,\psi_{S\cup T,\mathbf{a}^{\prime}\uplus\mathbf{a}}\right\rangle.

Comparing the above with (
B.5
), one can see that
D_{S,\mathbf{x}}P_{\mathfrak{X}}^{=d}[f]=P_{\mathfrak{X}}^{=d-|S|}D_{S,\mathbf{x}}[f]
.
∎

B.3
Comparison with Product Space

In this subsection, we compare our labeled matching space
\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}
with the following product space:

Definition B.8
.

Fix
p\in(0,1)
. Consider a random element
Z
of
\mathrm{Map}\left(\textstyle\prod\mathcal{U},\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}\cup\{\textup{{nil}}\}\right)
such that for each
e\in\textstyle\prod\mathcal{U}
, the value
Z(e)
is independent and identically distributed according to

\mathbb{P}\left[Z(e)=\textup{{nil}}\right]=1-p,\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbb{P}\left[Z(e)=z\right]=N^{-k}p\text{ for each }z\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}.

The ground set
\mathrm{Map}\left(\textstyle\prod\mathcal{U},\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}\cup\{\textup{{nil}}\}\right)
endowed with the distribution of such a random element
Z
is a probability space, which we denote by
\Gamma^{\mathcal{U},p}
.

B.3.1
Mimicking on a Given Level

We show that for a fixed degree
d\leqslant m
, one can choose an appropriate parameter
p
such that the degree-
d
Fourier level of the product space
\Gamma^{\mathcal{U},p}
“mimics” the level-
d
characters of the space
\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}
. To make this notion precise, we start with an abstract collection of Fourier coefficients indexed by
\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{U},d}
(as defined in
Definition

8.9
), and compare the corresponding “Fourier inverse functions” they define on the two spaces.

Definition B.9
.

Fix a nonnegative integers
d
such that
d\leqslant m
. For any map
\varphi:\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{U},d}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
, we associate with it a “Fourier inverse” function
\varphi^{\vee}_{(m)}\in L^{2}(\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m})
by

\varphi^{\vee}_{(m)}(\mathbf{y}):=\sum_{(M,\mathbf{a})\in\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{U},d}}\varphi(M,\mathbf{a})\cdot\prod_{e\in M}\chi_{\mathbf{a}(e)}(\mathbf{y}(e)).

Definition B.10
.

Fix
p\in(0,1)
. For any map
\varphi:\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{U},d}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
, we define a function
\varphi^{\natural}_{(p)}\in L^{2}(\Gamma^{\mathcal{U},p})
. Specifically, for every
\mathbf{y}\in\mathrm{Map}\left(\textstyle\prod\mathcal{U},\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}\cup\{\textup{{nil}}\}\right)
we let

\varphi^{\natural}_{(p)}(\mathbf{y}):=\sum_{(M,\mathbf{a})\in\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{U},d}}\left|\varphi(M,\mathbf{a})\right|\cdot\prod_{e\in M}\chi_{\mathbf{a}(e)}(\mathbf{y}(e)).

The following proposition demonstrates that for an appropriate parameter
p
, the two Fourier inverse functions have the same
L^{2}
-norm.

Proposition B.11
.

Fix integers
d,m
such that
d\leqslant m\leqslant|\mathcal{U}|
, and let
p=\Psi(|\mathcal{U}|,m,d)^{1/d}
. For any
\varphi:\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{U},d}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
, we have
\left\|\varphi^{\vee}_{(m)}\right\|_{2}=\left\|\varphi^{\natural}_{(p)}\right\|_{2}
.

Proof.

Simply expanding
\left\|\varphi^{\vee}_{(m)}\right\|_{2}^{2}=\underset{\mathbf{y}\in\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\varphi^{\vee}_{(m)}(\mathbf{y})\cdot\overline{\varphi^{\vee}_{(m)}(\mathbf{y})}\right]
yields

\left\|\varphi^{\vee}_{(m)}\right\|_{2}^{2}=\sum_{(M,\mathbf{a})\in\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{U},d}}\left|\varphi(M,\mathbf{a})\right|^{2}\cdot\Psi(|\mathcal{U}|,m,d),

(B.6)

and expanding

\left\|\varphi^{\natural}_{(p)}\right\|_{2}^{2}=\underset{\mathbf{y}\sim\Gamma^{\mathcal{U},m}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\varphi^{\natural}_{(p)}(\mathbf{y})\cdot\overline{\varphi^{\natural}_{(m)}(\mathbf{y})}\right]
yields

\left\|\varphi^{\natural}_{(p)}\right\|_{2}^{2}=\sum_{(M,\mathbf{a})\in\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{U},d}}\left|\varphi(M,\mathbf{a})\right|^{2}\cdot p^{d}.

(B.7)

The conclusion then follows by comparing (
B.6
) with (
B.7
) and using
p^{d}=\Psi(|\mathcal{U}|,m,d)
.
∎

B.3.2
Mimicking on other Levels

We claim that not only does the parameter
p
chosen in
Proposition

B.11
not only ensures that the degree-
d
Fourier levels of
\Gamma^{\mathcal{U},p}
and
\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}
resemble each other, but also that the nearby levels — those not too far from degree
d
— exhibit a similar approximation under the same choice of
p
. This effect on nearby levels is primarily due to the fact that the probability parameters
\Psi(n,m,d)
(defined in
Definition

8.1
) grows approximately exponentially in
d
when
d
is small, as formalized below.

Proposition B.12
.

Fix integers
n,m,d
such that
n\geqslant 2km
and
m\geqslant 2(d+1)
. Let
p=\Psi(n,m,d)^{1/d}
.

(1)

For
\ell\in\{0,1,\dots,d\}
we have
p^{\ell}\leqslant\Psi(n,m,\ell)\leqslant(2p)^{\ell}
.

(2)

For
\ell\in\{d,d+1,\dots,m\}
we have
\Psi(n,m,\ell)\leqslant p^{\ell}
.

Proof.

For
i\in\{0,1,\dots,m-1\}
, we have

\frac{\Psi(n-i-1,m-i-1,1)}{\Psi(n-i,m-i,1)}=\frac{(n-i)^{k}}{(n-i-1)^{k}}\cdot\frac{m-i-1}{m-i}<1.

So
\Psi(n-i,m-i,1)
is decreasing in
i
. Furthermore,

\frac{\Psi(n,m,1)}{\Psi(n-d,m-d,1)}=\frac{(n-d)^{k}}{n^{k}}\cdot\frac{m}{m-d}\leqslant\frac{m}{m-d}\leqslant 2.

Therefore, for
\ell\in\{0,1,\dots,d\}
we have

\displaystyle\Psi(n,m,\ell)

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=0}^{\ell-1}\Psi(n-i,m-i,1)\leqslant 2^{\ell}\cdot\Psi(n-d,m-d,1)^{\ell}

\displaystyle\leqslant 2^{\ell}\cdot\left(\prod_{i=0}^{d-1}\Psi(n-i,m-i,1)\right)^{\ell/d}=2^{\ell}\cdot\Psi(n,m,d)^{\ell/d}=(2p)^{\ell},

as well as

\Psi(n,m,\ell)=\prod_{i=0}^{\ell-1}\Psi(n-i,m-i,1)\geqslant\left(\prod_{i=0}^{d}\Psi(n-i,m-i,1)\right)^{\ell/d}=\Psi(n,m,d)^{\ell/d}=p^{\ell}.

For
\ell\geqslant d
we have

\displaystyle\Psi(n,m,\ell)

\displaystyle=\Psi(n,m,d)\cdot\prod_{i=d}^{\ell-1}\Psi(n-i,m-i,1)\leqslant\Psi(n,m,d)\cdot\Psi(n-d,m-d,1)^{\ell-d}

\displaystyle\leqslant\Psi(n,m,d)\cdot\left(\prod_{i=0}^{d-1}\Psi(n-i,m-i,1)\right)^{(\ell-d)/d}=\Psi(n,m,d)^{\ell/d}=p^{\ell}.\qed

B.3.3
Comparison of
q
-Norms

Recall that
Proposition

B.11
establishes the equality of the
L^{2}
-norms of the two Fourier inverse functions corresponding to the same collection of Fourier coefficients. Equipped with
Proposition

B.12
, we now extend this comparison (but with an inequality instead of equality) to the
L^{q}
-norms of these functions, for any positive even integer
q
.

Lemma B.13
.

Fix integers
d,m
such that
d\leqslant m\leqslant|\mathcal{U}|
, and let
p=\Psi(|\mathcal{U}|,m,d)^{1/d}
. For any
\varphi:\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{U},d}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
and any positive integer
q
, we have
\left\|\varphi^{\vee}_{(m)}\right\|_{2q}\leqslant\left\|\varphi^{\natural}_{(p)}\right\|_{2q}
.

Proof.

Let
\mathsf{GOOD}
be the collection of all sequences
(M_{1},\mathbf{a}_{1}),\dots,(M_{2q},\mathbf{a}_{2q})\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{U},d}
such that for every
e\in\textstyle\prod\mathcal{U}
,

\sum_{i=1}^{q}\widetilde{\mathbf{a}_{i}}(e)=\sum_{i=q+1}^{2q}\widetilde{\mathbf{a}_{i}}(e),

where
\widetilde{\mathbf{a}_{i}}:\textstyle\prod\mathcal{U}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}
is the extension of
\mathbf{a}_{i}
by value 0 on
(\textstyle\prod\mathcal{U})\setminus M_{i}
, for each
i\in[2q]
. It is easy to see that for the expected value

\mathscr{E}_{\Omega}\Big((M_{1},\mathbf{a}_{1}),\dots,(M_{2q},\mathbf{a}_{2q})\Big):=\underset{\mathbf{y}\in\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{q}\left(\prod_{e\in M_{i}}\chi_{\mathbf{a}_{i}(e)}(\mathbf{y}(e))\right)\prod_{i=q+1}^{2q}\left(\prod_{e\in M_{i}}\overline{\chi_{\mathbf{a}_{i}(e)}(\mathbf{y}(e))}\right)\right]

(B.8)

equals
\Psi\Big(|\mathcal{U}|,m,\big|M_{1}\cup\dots\cup M_{2q}\big|\Big)
if
((M_{1},\mathbf{a}_{1}),\dots,(M_{2q},\mathbf{a}_{2q}))\in\textsf{GOOD}
and equals 0 otherwise. The same conclusion also holds if the expected value in (
B.8
) is evaluated not for a random element
\mathbf{y}\in\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}
but for
\mathbf{y}
sampled from
\Gamma^{\mathcal{U},p}
:

\mathscr{E}_{\Gamma}\Big((M_{1},\mathbf{a}_{1}),\dots,(M_{2q},\mathbf{a}_{2q})\Big):=\underset{\mathbf{y}\in\Gamma^{\mathcal{U},p}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{q}\left(\prod_{e\in M_{i}}\chi_{\mathbf{a}_{i}(e)}(\mathbf{y}(e))\right)\prod_{i=q+1}^{2q}\left(\prod_{e\in M_{i}}\overline{\chi_{\mathbf{a}_{i}(e)}(\mathbf{y}(e))}\right)\right]

(B.9)

equals
p^{|M_{1}\cup\dots\cup M_{2q}|}
if
((M_{1},\mathbf{a}_{1}),\dots,(M_{2q},\mathbf{a}_{2q}))\in\textsf{GOOD}
and equals 0 otherwise. Therefore the values of (
B.8
) and (
B.9
) are always nonnegative real numbers. Furthermore, since
p^{|M_{1}\cup\dots\cup M_{2q}|}
is always at least
\Psi\Big(|\mathcal{U},m,\big|M_{1}\cup\dots\cup M_{2q}\big|\Big)
, by
Proposition

B.12
(2), the value of (
B.8
) is always at most the value of (
B.9
), whether the sequence
((M_{1},\mathbf{a}_{1}),\dots,(M_{2q},\mathbf{a}_{2q}))
belongs to
GOOD
or not. Now since
\left\|\varphi^{\vee}_{(m)}\right\|_{2q}^{2q}=\underset{\mathbf{y}\in\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\varphi^{\vee}_{(m)}(\mathbf{y})^{q}\cdot\overline{\varphi^{\vee}_{(m)}(\mathbf{y})^{q}}\right]
expands into

\sum_{(M_{1},\mathbf{a}_{1}),\dots,(M_{2q},\mathbf{a}_{2q})\in\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{U},d}}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{q}\varphi(M_{i},\mathbf{a}_{i})\prod_{i=q+1}^{2q}\overline{\varphi(M_{i},\mathbf{a}_{i})}\cdot\mathscr{E}_{\Omega}\Big((M_{1},\mathbf{a}_{1}),\dots,(M_{2q},\mathbf{a}_{2q})\Big)\right)

and
\left\|\varphi^{\natural}_{(p)}\right\|_{2q}^{2q}=\underset{\mathbf{y}\sim\Gamma^{\mathcal{U},p}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\varphi^{\natural}_{(p)}(\mathbf{y})^{q}\cdot\overline{\varphi^{\natural}_{(p)}(\mathbf{y})^{q}}\right]
expands into

\sum_{(M_{1},\mathbf{a}_{1}),\dots,(M_{2q},\mathbf{a}_{2q})\in\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{U},d}}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{q}\left|\varphi(M_{i},\mathbf{a}_{i})\right|\prod_{i=q+1}^{2q}\left|\varphi(M_{i},\mathbf{a}_{i})\right|\cdot\mathscr{E}_{\Gamma}\Big((M_{1},\mathbf{a}_{1}),\dots,(M_{2q},\mathbf{a}_{2q})\Big)\right),

by term-wise comparison it follows that
\left\|\varphi^{\vee}_{(m)}\right\|_{2q}^{2q}\leqslant\left\|\varphi^{\natural}_{(p)}\right\|_{2q}^{2q}
.
∎

B.3.4
Comparison of Derivatives

The final comparison required between the spaces
\Gamma^{\mathcal{U},p}
and
\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}
concerns their respective derivative operators. For the space
\Gamma^{\mathcal{U},p}
, we adopt the following definition of formal derivatives that act purely on Fourier coefficients.

Definition B.14
.

Fix a nonnegative integer
d\leqslant|\mathcal{U}|
. For any matching
S\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{U},\leqslant d}
and
\mathbf{x}\in\mathrm{Map}\left(S,\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}\right)
, we define the formal derivative operator
\widehat{D_{S,\mathbf{x}}}:\mathrm{Map}\left(\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{U},d},\mathbb{C}\right)\rightarrow\mathrm{Map}\left(\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{U}_{\setminus S},\,d-|S|},\mathbb{C}\right)
as follows. For each
\varphi:\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{U},d}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
and
(T,\mathbf{a})\in\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{U}_{\setminus S},\,d-|S|}
, let

\left(\widehat{D_{S,\mathbf{x}}}[\varphi]\right)(T,\mathbf{a}):=\sum_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}:S\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}\setminus\{0\}}\chi_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{x})\cdot\varphi(S\cup T,\mathbf{a}^{\prime}\uplus\mathbf{a}).

Our comparison lemma for derivatives is as follows.

Lemma B.15
.

Fix integers
d,m
such that
d\leqslant m\leqslant|\mathcal{U}|
, and let
p=\Psi(|\mathcal{U}|,m,d)^{1/d}
. For any
\varphi:\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{U},d}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
, any
S\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{U},\leqslant d}
and any
\mathbf{x}\in\mathrm{Map}\left(S,\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}\right)
, we have

\left\|D_{S,\mathbf{x}}\big[\varphi^{\vee}_{(m)}\big]\right\|_{2}\geqslant 2^{-|S|/2}\cdot\left\|\left(\widehat{D_{S,\mathbf{x}}}[\varphi]\right)^{\natural}_{(p)}\right\|_{2}.

Proof.

We first note that it follows easily from
Definitions

8.2
and
B.14
and
Proposition

B.6
that

D_{S,\mathbf{x}}\big[\varphi^{\vee}_{(m)}\big]=\left(\widehat{D_{S,\mathbf{x}}}[\varphi]\right)^{\vee}_{(m-|S|)}

as functions on
\Omega^{\mathcal{U},d}_{\setminus S}
. Therefore we may write for convenience
\varphi^{\prime}:=\widehat{D_{S,\mathbf{x}}}[\varphi]
and it suffices to show

\left\|(\varphi^{\prime})^{\vee}_{(m-|S|)}\right\|_{2}\geqslant 2^{-|S|/2}\left\|(\varphi^{\prime})^{\natural}_{(p)}\right\|_{2}.

(B.10)

Similarly to (
B.6
) and (
B.7
), we have the identities

\left\|(\varphi^{\prime})^{\vee}_{(m)}\right\|_{2}^{2}=\sum_{(T,\mathbf{a})\in\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{U}_{\setminus S},\,d-|S|}}\left|\varphi(T,\mathbf{a})\right|^{2}\cdot\Psi\Big(|\mathcal{U}_{\setminus S}|,m-|S|,d-|S|\Big),

and

\left\|(\varphi^{\prime})^{\natural}_{(p)}\right\|_{2}^{2}=\sum_{(T,\mathbf{a})\in\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{U}_{\setminus S},\,d-|S|}}\left|\varphi(T,\mathbf{a})\right|^{2}\cdot p^{d-|S|}.

Now note that by
Proposition

B.12
(1) we have

\Psi\Big(|\mathcal{U}_{\setminus S}|,m-|S|,d-|S|\Big)=\frac{\Psi(|\mathcal{U}|,m,d)}{\Psi(|\mathcal{U}|,m,|S|)}\geqslant\frac{p^{d}}{(2p)^{|S|}}=2^{-|S|}p^{d-|S|},

and combining the above three displays leads to the desired conclusion (
B.10
).
∎

The next lemma is a standard identity for formal derivatives over product spaces (cf.
[
KLM23
, Theorem 4.6]
for analogous statements).

Lemma B.16
.

Fix
p\in(0,1)
. For any
\varphi:\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{U},d}
, we have the identity

\sum_{S\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{U},\leqslant d}}p^{|S|}\underset{\mathbf{x}:S\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left\|\left(\widehat{D_{S,\mathbf{x}}}[\varphi]\right)^{\natural}_{(p)}\right\|_{2}^{2}\right]=2^{d}\left\|\varphi^{\natural}_{(p)}\right\|_{2}^{2}.

Proof.

Expanding the 2-norm on the left hand side yields

\displaystyle\left\|\left(\widehat{D_{S,\mathbf{x}}}[\varphi]\right)^{\natural}_{(p)}\right\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\underset{\mathbf{y}\in\Gamma^{\mathcal{U}_{\setminus S},\,p}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left(\widehat{D_{S,\mathbf{x}}}[\varphi]\right)^{\natural}_{(p)}(\mathbf{y})\cdot\overline{\left(\widehat{D_{S,\mathbf{x}}}[\varphi]\right)^{\natural}_{(p)}(\mathbf{y})}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{(T,\mathbf{a})\in\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{U}_{\setminus S},\,d-|S|}}\left|\widehat{D_{S,\mathbf{x}}}[\varphi](T,\mathbf{a})\right|^{2}\cdot p^{d-|S|}.

(B.11)

By
Definition

B.14
we have

\displaystyle\quad\underset{\mathbf{x}:S\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\sum_{(T,\mathbf{a})\in\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{U}_{\setminus S},\,d-|S|}}\left|\widehat{D_{S,\mathbf{x}}}[\varphi](T,\mathbf{a})\right|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{(T,\mathbf{a})\in\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{U}_{\setminus S},\,d-|S|}}\underset{\mathbf{x}:S\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left|\sum_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}:S\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}\setminus\{0\}}\chi_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{x})\cdot\varphi(S\cup T,\mathbf{a}^{\prime}\uplus\mathbf{a})\right|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{(T,\mathbf{a})\in\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{U}_{\setminus S},\,d-|S|}}\left(\sum_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}_{1},\mathbf{a}^{\prime}_{2}:S\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}\setminus\{0\}}\varphi(S\cup T,\mathbf{a}^{\prime}_{1}\uplus\mathbf{a})\overline{\varphi(S\cup T,\mathbf{a}^{\prime}_{2}\uplus\mathbf{a})}\cdot\underset{\mathbf{x}:S\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\chi_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}_{1}-\mathbf{a}^{\prime}_{2}}(\mathbf{x})\right]\right).

\displaystyle=\sum_{(T,\mathbf{a})\in\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{U}_{\setminus S},\,d-|S|}}\sum_{\mathbf{a}^{\prime}:S\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}\setminus\{0\}}\left|\varphi(S\cup T,\mathbf{a}^{\prime}\uplus\mathbf{a})\right|^{2}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(M,\mathbf{a}^{\prime\prime})\in\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{U},d}\\
M\supseteq S\end{subarray}}\left|\varphi(M,\mathbf{a}^{\prime\prime})\right|^{2}.

(B.12)

Combining (
B.11
) and (
B.12
), we obtain the desired result

\displaystyle\sum_{S\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{U},\leqslant d}}p^{|S|}\underset{\mathbf{x}:S\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left\|\left(\widehat{D_{S,\mathbf{x}}}[\varphi]\right)^{\natural}_{(p)}\right\|_{2}^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=p^{d}\sum_{S\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{U},\leqslant d}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(M,\mathbf{a}^{\prime\prime})\in\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{U},d}\\
M\supseteq S\end{subarray}}\left|\varphi(M,\mathbf{a}^{\prime\prime})\right|^{2}

\displaystyle=p^{d}\cdot 2^{d}\sum_{(M,\mathbf{a}^{\prime\prime})\in\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{U},d}}\left|\varphi(M,\mathbf{a}^{\prime\prime})\right|^{2}=2^{d}\left\|\varphi^{\natural}_{(p)}\right\|_{2}^{2},

where in the last transition we use (
B.7
).
∎

B.4
The Hypercontractive Inequality

We are now prepared to derive a derivative-based hypercontractive inequality for our space
\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}
by leveraging the corresponding inequality already established for product spaces.
In particular, applying the general result of
[
KLM23
, Theorem 4.1]
to the product space
\Gamma^{\mathcal{U},p}
yields the following.
12
12

12

Strictly speaking,
[
KLM23
]
proves the inequality only for real-valued functions, whereas our application requires it for complex-valued functions. Nevertheless, the proof in
[
KLM23
]
extends to the complex setting without difficulty. Alternatively, one can apply
[
KLM23
, Theorem 4.1]
separately to the real and imaginary parts of
\varphi^{\natural}_{(p)}
, which introduces an additional factor of
2^{2q}
on the right-hand side of (
B.13
), easily absorbed into the other parameters.

Lemma B.17

(
[
KLM23
, Theorem 4.1]
)
.

Let
p\in(0,1)
. Suppose
q
is a positive integer and
\rho\in(0,\frac{1}{3\sqrt{2q}})
. For any
\varphi:\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{U},d}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
, we have
13
13

13

Compared with the inequality in
[
KLM23
, Theorem 4.1]
, the right-hand side of (
B.13
) contains an additional factor of
p^{|S|}
.
This arises because the expectation in (
B.13
) is taken over
\mathbf{x}:S\to\mathbb{Z}_{n}^{k}
rather than over
\mathbf{x}:S\to\mathbb{Z}_{n}^{k}\cup\{\textup{{nil}}\}
; whenever
\mathbf{x}
contains a
nil
, the derivative with respect to
\mathbf{x}
is zero.

\left\|\varphi^{\natural}_{(p)}\right\|_{2q}^{2q}\leqslant\rho^{-2dq}\sum_{S\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{U},\leqslant d}}\beta^{2q|S|}(2q)^{-q|S|}p^{|S|}\underset{\mathbf{x}:S\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left\|\left(\widehat{D_{S,\mathbf{x}}}[\varphi]\right)^{\natural}_{(p)}\right\|_{2}^{2q}\right],

(B.13)

where

\beta:=\rho\sqrt{2q}\left(1+\frac{4(q-1)}{\ln(\rho^{-1}(2q)^{-1/2})}\right)
.

We combine
Lemma

B.17
with the comparison results established in
Section

B.3
to obtain the desired hypercontractive inequality for our space
\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}
.

Lemma B.18

(Derivative-based hypercontractivity)
.

Fix integers
d,m
such that
|\mathcal{U}|\geqslant 2km
and
m\geqslant 2(d+1)
. Fix
r>0
and integer
q\geqslant 1
. For
f:\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
such that
f
lies in the subspace
\mathrm{span}\left\{\psi_{M,\mathbf{a}}:(M,\mathbf{a})\in\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{U},d}\right\}
of
L^{2}(\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m})
, we have

\left\|f\right\|_{2q}^{2q}\leqslant 2^{d}\rho^{-2dq}\left\|f\right\|_{2}^{2}\cdot\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{U},\leqslant d}\\
\mathbf{x}:S\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}\end{subarray}}\left(r^{-|S|}\left\|D_{S,\mathbf{x}}f\right\|_{2}\right)^{2q-2},

where

\rho:=\frac{1}{4\sqrt{2}}\min\left\{q^{-1/2},q^{-1}r^{-\frac{q-1}{q}}\right\}.

(B.14)

Proof.

Since
f\in\mathrm{span}\left\{\psi_{M,\mathbf{a}}:(M,\mathbf{a})\in\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{U},d}\right\}
, we may define
\varphi:\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{U},d}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
by

\varphi(M,\mathbf{a}):=\Psi(|\mathcal{U}|,m,|M|)^{-1/2}\cdot\left\langle f,\psi_{M,\mathbf{a}}\right\rangle

for
(M,\mathbf{a})\in\mathfrak{X}^{\mathcal{U},d}
, and then by
Definitions

8.2
and
B.9
we have
f=\varphi^{\vee}_{(m)}
.

Let
\beta:=\rho\sqrt{2q}\left(1+\frac{4(q-1)}{\ln(\rho^{-1}(2q)^{-1/2})}\right)
, as in
Lemma

B.17
. Using
\rho^{-1}(2q)^{-1/2}\geqslant 4
we get
\beta\leqslant\rho\sqrt{2q}\cdot 4q
. Now the other upper bound
\rho\leqslant\frac{1}{4\sqrt{2}}q^{-1}r^{-\frac{q-1}{q}}
yields
\beta^{2q}q^{-q}r^{2q-2}\leqslant 1
.

According to
Lemma

B.17
, we have

\left\|\varphi^{\natural}_{(p)}\right\|_{2q}^{2q}\leqslant\rho^{-2dq}\sum_{S\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{U},\leqslant d}}\beta^{2q|S|}(2q)^{-q|S|}p^{|S|}\underset{\mathbf{x}:S\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left\|\left(\widehat{D_{S,\mathbf{x}}}[\varphi]\right)^{\natural}_{(p)}\right\|_{2}^{2q}\right].

We can apply
Lemmas

B.13
and
B.15
to the above inequality and get

\left\|f\right\|_{2q}^{2q}\leqslant\rho^{-2dq}\sum_{S\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{U},\leqslant d}}\beta^{2q|S|}q^{-q|S|}p^{|S|}\underset{\mathbf{x}:S\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left\|\left(\widehat{D_{S,\mathbf{x}}}[\varphi]\right)^{\natural}_{(p)}\right\|_{2}^{2}\cdot\left\|D_{S,\mathbf{x}}[f]\right\|_{2}^{2q-2}\right].

Using the estimations obtained in the preceding paragraph, we simplify the above into

\displaystyle\left\|f\right\|_{2q}^{2q}

\displaystyle\leqslant\rho^{-2dq}\sum_{S\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{U},\leqslant d}}p^{|S|}r^{-(2q-2)|S|}\underset{\mathbf{x}:S\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left\|\left(\widehat{D_{S,\mathbf{x}}}[\varphi]\right)^{\natural}_{(p)}\right\|_{2}^{2}\cdot\left\|D_{S,\mathbf{x}}[f]\right\|_{2}^{2q-2}\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant\rho^{-2dq}\left(\sum_{S\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{U},\leqslant d}}p^{|S|}\underset{\mathbf{x}:S\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left\|\left(\widehat{D_{S,\mathbf{x}}}[\varphi]\right)^{\natural}_{(p)}\right\|_{2}^{2}\right]\right)\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{U},\leqslant d}\\
\mathbf{x}:S\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}\end{subarray}}\left(r^{-|S|}\left\|D_{S,\mathbf{x}}f\right\|_{2}\right)^{2q-2}

\displaystyle=2^{d}\rho^{-2dq}\left\|\varphi^{\natural}_{(p)}\right\|_{2}^{2}\cdot\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{U},\leqslant d}\\
\mathbf{x}:S\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}\end{subarray}}\left(r^{-|S|}\left\|D_{S,\mathbf{x}}f\right\|_{2}\right)^{2q-2},

where in the last transition we use
Lemma

B.16
. The proof is concluded by noting that by
Proposition

B.11
we have that
\left\|\varphi^{\natural}_{(p)}\right\|_{2}^{2}=\|f\|_{2}^{2}
.
∎

B.5
Proof of the Level-
d
Inequality

Now we complete the proof of the level-
d
inequality, restated below, by an induction argument.
See
8.12

Proof.

The conclusion in the case
d=0
simply comes from
\underset{\mathbf{y}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[f(\mathbf{y})\right]^{2}\leqslant\lambda_{1}^{2}
, which holds by the
L^{1}
-globalness assumption. We proceed by an induction on
d
.
Towards this end, fix
d\geqslant 1
and assume that the statement holds for all
d^{\prime}<d
.

Fix
S\neq\emptyset
and
\mathbf{x}:S\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}
, so that by
Corollary

B.5
we know that
D_{S,\mathbf{x}}[f]
is both
(r,r^{|S|}\lambda_{1},d-|S|)
-
L^{1}
-global and
(r,r^{|S|}\lambda_{2},d-|S|)
-
L^{2}
-global.
Our first goal
will be to show that
P_{\mathfrak{X}}^{=d}[f]
has discrete derivatives with small norms, and towards this end we use the induction hypothesis.
Since
|\mathcal{U}_{\setminus S}|\geqslant 2k(m-|S|)
and
m-|S|\geqslant 2(d-|S|+1)
, we can apply the induction hypothesis on
D_{S,\mathbf{x}}[f]:\Omega^{\mathcal{U},m}_{\setminus S}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
. Combining with
Lemma

B.7
, we get

\displaystyle\left\|D_{S,\mathbf{x}}P_{\mathfrak{X}}^{=d}[f]\right\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\left\|P_{\mathfrak{X}}^{=d-|S|}D_{S,\mathbf{x}}[f]\right\|_{2}^{2}

(B.15)

\displaystyle\leqslant r^{2|S|}\lambda_{1}^{2}\left(\frac{10^{5}r^{2}\log(\lambda_{2}/\lambda_{1})}{d-|S|}\right)^{d-|S|}

\displaystyle=10^{5(d-|S|)}\lambda_{1}^{2}r^{2d}\log^{d-|S|}(\lambda_{2}/\lambda_{1})d^{-(d-|S|)}\left(1+\frac{|S|}{d-|S|}\right)^{d-|S|}

\displaystyle\leqslant 10^{5(d-|S|)}\lambda_{1}^{2}r^{2d}\log^{d-|S|}(\lambda_{2}/\lambda_{1})d^{-(d-|S|)}\cdot 10^{5|S|}

\displaystyle=\lambda_{1}^{2}\left(\frac{10^{5}r^{2}\log(\lambda_{2}/\lambda_{1})}{d}\right)^{d}\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}}{\log^{1/2}(\lambda_{2}/\lambda_{1})}\right)^{2|S|}

\displaystyle=(r^{\prime})^{2|S|}(\lambda^{\prime})^{2},

(B.16)

where we let

\lambda^{\prime}=\lambda_{1}\left(\frac{10^{5}r^{2}\log(\lambda_{2}/\lambda_{1})}{d}\right)^{d/2}\quad\text{ and }\quad r^{\prime}=\frac{\sqrt{d}}{\log^{1/2}(\lambda_{2}/\lambda_{1})}.

We intend to apply
Lemma

B.18
, and for that we pick

q=\left\lfloor\frac{4\log(\lambda_{2}/\lambda_{1})}{d}\right\rfloor\quad\text{ and }\quad\rho=\frac{1}{4\sqrt{2}}\min\left\{q^{-1/2},q^{-1}(r^{\prime})^{-\frac{q-1}{q}}\right\}.

This choice of parameters ensure that
\rho^{-2}\leqslant 10^{3}q
, and thus

\displaystyle 2^{d}\rho^{-2dq}\lambda_{1}^{2q}\left(\lambda_{2}/\lambda_{1}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant\lambda_{1}^{2q}\left(2\rho^{-2}(\lambda_{2}/\lambda_{1})^{2/(dq)}\right)^{dq}\leqslant\lambda_{1}^{2q}(10^{4}q)^{dq}

\displaystyle\leqslant\lambda_{1}^{2q}\left(\frac{10^{5}r^{2}\log(\lambda_{2}/\lambda_{1})}{d}\right)^{dq}=(\lambda^{\prime})^{2q}.

(B.17)

Since
P_{\mathfrak{X}}^{=d}
is an orthogonal projection, we have

\displaystyle\left\|P_{\mathfrak{X}}^{=d}f\right\|_{2}^{4q}=\left\langle f,P_{\mathfrak{X}}^{=d}f\right\rangle^{2q}

\displaystyle\leqslant\left\|P_{\mathfrak{X}}^{=d}f\right\|_{2q}^{2q}\cdot\|f\|_{2q/(2q-1)}^{2q}

\displaystyle\leqslant\left\|P_{\mathfrak{X}}^{=d}f\right\|_{2q}^{2q}\cdot\|f\|_{1}^{2q-2}\cdot\|f\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant 2^{d}\rho^{-2dq}\lambda_{1}^{2q-2}\lambda_{2}^{2}\left\|P_{\mathfrak{X}}^{=d}f\right\|_{2}^{2}\cdot\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{U},\leqslant d}\\
\mathbf{x}:S\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{N}^{k}\end{subarray}}\left((r^{\prime})^{-|S|}\left\|D_{S,\mathbf{x}}P_{\mathfrak{X}}^{=d}f\right\|_{2}\right)^{2q-2}

\displaystyle\leqslant(\lambda^{\prime})^{2q}\cdot\left\|P_{\mathfrak{X}}^{=d}f\right\|_{2}^{2}\max\left(\left\|P_{\mathfrak{X}}^{=d}f\right\|_{2}^{2q-2},(\lambda^{\prime})^{2q-2}\right),

where in the second and third transitions we used Hölder’s inequality, the fourth transition is by
Lemma

B.18
, and the last transition is by
(
B.17
) and (
B.16
). It follows that
\left\|P_{\mathfrak{X}}^{=d}f\right\|_{2}\leqslant\lambda^{\prime}
, as desired.
∎
